When you take its outer product with $a_{1}$, it's still a linear combination of outer products, and each term is even or odd as $r$ is even or odd. $\square$

Now I define for any $A=\sum_{r} A_{r}$ and $B=\sum_{s} B_{s}$

$$
\begin{aligned}
A\rfloor B & :=\sum_{r, s}\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{s-r} \\
A\lfloor B & :=\sum_{r, s}\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r-s} \\
A \wedge B & :=\sum_{r, s}\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r+s}
\end{aligned}
$$

All previous expressions for the inner and outer products of two objects are special cases of these definitions. Further, this defintion for the outer product of two objects and the definition for the outer product of arbitrarily many vectors in Eq. (30) are consistent with each other thanks to Eq. (54), which shows that

$$
a \wedge\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right)=a \wedge a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r} .
$$

Some other facts are worth mentioning.

1. $\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{r}=A_{r}\left\lfloor B_{r}=\left\langle A_{r} B_{r}\right\rangle\right.$.
2. If $r>s$ then $\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{s}=B_{s}\left\lfloor A_{r}=0\right.$ because all negative-grade multivectors vanish.
3. The lowest grade term in $A_{r} B_{s}$ is $A_{r}$ ↓ $B_{s}$ if $r \leq s$ and $A_{r}\left\lfloor B_{s}\right.$ if $r \geq s$.
4. The highest grade term in $A_{r} B_{s}$ is $A_{r} \wedge B_{s}$.
5. For any $\lambda, \lambda A=\lambda\rfloor A=\lambda \wedge A$ and $A \lambda=A\lfloor\lambda=A \wedge \lambda$. The product of a vector and any multivector is a sum of inner and outer products, as shown by Eqs. (64) and (66). In all other cases there are additional terms of intermediate grades.

These definitions make the inner and outer products much easier to work with, because in general the geometric product has nicer algebraic properties. The main advantage of the inner and outer products over the full product is nice behavior under interchange of the factors; as you'll see in Section 5.2, $A_{r} B_{s}$ and $B_{s} A_{r}$ are related, but not in a way that's easy to use, while going from $\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{s}$ to $B_{s}\left\lfloor A_{r}\right.$ or from $A_{r} \wedge B_{s}$ to $B_{s} \wedge A_{r}$ is just a matter of a sign change (Eqs. (141) and (143)).

The definitions also allow me to deduce three more identities from Eqs. (74); I take the $r+s-1$-grade term of the first of Eqs. (74), the $r-s+1$-grade term of the third, and the $s-r+1$-grade term of the last, with the results

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.a \downharpoonleft\left(A_{r} \wedge B_{s}\right)=\left(a \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right) \wedge B_{s}+(-1)^{r} A_{r} \wedge(a\rfloor B_{s}\right) \\
& a \wedge\left(A_{r}\left\lfloor B_{s}\right)=\left(a \wedge A_{r}\right)\left\lfloor B_{s}-(-1)^{r} A_{r}\left\lfloor(a\rfloor B_{s}\right)\right.\right. \\
& \left.\left.\left.\left.a \wedge\left(A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{s}\right)=(a\rfloor A_{r}\right)\right\rfloor B_{s}+(-1)^{r} A_{r}\right\rfloor\left(a \wedge B_{s}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

(Taking an appropriate-grade term of the second identity only yields a special case of the third of Eqs. (83) below.) Again, these expressions are actually valid for general $B$, and also for general $A$ when I use some results from Section 5.1.

Like the geometric product, the inner and outer products are distributive, and they obey these identities.
Theorem 13.

$$
A \wedge(B \wedge C)=(A \wedge B) \wedge C
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
A\rfloor(B \downarrow C) & =(A\rfloor B)\lfloor C \\
A\rfloor(B\rfloor C) & =(A \wedge B)\rfloor C \\
A\lfloor(B \wedge C) & =(A\lfloor B)\lfloor C
\end{aligned}
$$

So the outer product and certain combinations of left and right inner products are associative, but neither left nor right inner products are associative by themselves. In the homogeneous case, the first relation above becomes

$$
A_{r} \wedge\left(B_{s} \wedge C_{t}\right)=\left\langle A_{r} B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{r+s+t}
$$

An important special case of this is

$$
a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}=\left\langle a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right\rangle_{r} .
$$

Associativity of the outer product plus its properties under interchange of factors leads to the result

$$
a \wedge A \wedge b=-b \wedge A \wedge a
$$

where $a$ and $b$ are any vectors and $A$ is any multivector.
Proof. For the first relation, I note that

$$
\begin{aligned}
A_{r} \wedge\left(B_{s} \wedge C_{t}\right) & =A_{r} \wedge\left\langle B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{s+t} \\
& =\left\langle A_{r}\left\langle B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{s+t}\right\rangle_{r+s+t} \\
& =\left\langle A_{r} B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{r+s+t} \\
& =\left\langle\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r+s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{r+s+t} \\
& =\left(A_{r} \wedge B_{s}\right) \wedge C_{t}
\end{aligned}
$$

The crucial step is taken on the third line, where the $\left\rangle_{s+t}\right.$ is dropped. This can be done because the only term in $A_{r} B_{s} C_{t}$ that has grade $r+s+t$ is the term that comes from multiplying $A_{r}$ by the highest grade term in $B_{s} C_{t}$. From this it follows that $A \wedge(B \wedge C)=(A \wedge B) \wedge C$ for any $A$, $B$, and $C$. The third line also gives me Eq. (84). For the second relation, consider

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor\left(B_{s}\left\lfloor C_{t}\right)\right. & \left.=A_{r}\right\rfloor\left\langle B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{s-t} \\
& =\left\langle A_{r}\left\langle B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{s-t}\right\rangle_{s-(r+t)}
\end{aligned}
$$

Now this vanishes automatically unless $r \leq s-t$, in which case

$$
\left\langle A_{r}\left\langle B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{s-t}\right\rangle_{s-(r+t)}=\left\langle A_{r} B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{s-(r+t)}
$$

because when the inequality is satisfied, the only term in $A_{r} B_{s} C_{t}$ that has grade $s-(r+t)$ is the term that comes from multiplying $A_{r}$ by the lowest grade term in $B_{s} C_{t}$. Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor\left(B_{s}\left\lfloor C_{t}\right)\right. & =\left\langle A_{r} B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{s-(r+t)} \\
& =\left\langle A_{r} B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{(s-r)-t} \\
& =\left\langle\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{s-r} C_{t}\right\rangle_{(s-r)-t} \\
& =\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{s-r}\left\lfloor C_{t}\right. \\
& \left.=\left(A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{s}\right)\left\lfloor C_{t}\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

This expression also vanishes unless the inequality is satisfied, so $A\rfloor(B\lfloor C)=(A\rfloor B)\lfloor C$ in general. Finally,

$$
\left.\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor\left(B_{s}\right\rfloor C_{t}\right)=A_{r} \downharpoonleft\left\langle B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{t-s}
$$

$$
=\left\langle A_{r}\left\langle B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{t-s}\right\rangle_{t-(r+s)}
$$

Now this vanishes automatically unless $r+s \leq t$, in which case logic similar to that used above yields

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor\left(B_{s} \downharpoonleft C_{t}\right) & =\left\langle A_{r} B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{t-(r+s)} \\
& =\left\langle\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r+s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{t-(r+s)} \\
& \left.=\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r+s}\right\rfloor C_{t} \\
& \left.=\left(A_{r} \wedge B_{s}\right)\right\rfloor C_{t}
\end{aligned}
$$

This final expression also vanishes unless the inequality is satisfied; therefore $A\rfloor(B\rfloor C)=$ $(A \wedge B)\rfloor C$ for all $A, B$, and $C$. A similar proof shows that ( $A\lfloor B)\lfloor C=A\lfloor(B \wedge C)$. $\square$

It's useful to introduce an order of operations of these products to cut down on parentheses. The order is outer products, followed by inner products, followed by geometric products. Thus, for example,

$$
A\rfloor B \wedge C D=\{A\rfloor(B \wedge C)\} D .
$$

Despite this convention, I'll occasionally put the parentheses back in for clarity. However, I will use it a lot in Section 7 on projections, rotations, and reflections. (This is not the only convention in use; the other one reverses the order of inner and outer products. I picked this one.)

Now that I've defined two separate inner products, I should probably say why. Generally, we associate inner products with projections, but that turns out not to be quite right. Look back at Eq. (5) for the projection of $v$ along $u$; noting that the inner product of a scalar into a vector is actually their product, you'll see Eq. (5) can also be written

$$
\left.P_{u}(v)=(v \downharpoonleft u)\right\rfloor u^{-1} .
$$

So orthogonal projection is actually a double inner product. The geometric meaning of a single inner product, as I'll show in Section 4.3, is this: for blades $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}, \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is also a blade, and it represents the subspace of vectors orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and contained in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. So the roles played by the factors in the inner product are not the same; that's why the product is asymmetric and there are two of them, so either factor can play either role.

Finally, I can use the new definition of the left inner product to generalize Theorem 11.
Theorem 14. If $r \leq s$ then

$$
B_{r} \downharpoonleft\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{s}\right)=\sum(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(i_{j}-j\right)}\left(B_{r} \downharpoonleft a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) a_{i_{r+1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{s}},
$$

where the sum is performed over all possible choices of $\left\{a_{i_{j}}\right\}_{j=1, \ldots, r}$ out of $\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, s}$, and in each term $i_{1}$ through $i_{r}$ and $i_{r+1}$ through $i_{s}$ separately are in ascending order.

The coefficients in this sum can be remembered using the same trick used for Theorem 11. In that case, you imagine that you need to permute each vector to the far left in order to act on it with $a$. For this theorem, you imagine that you need to permute each distinct subset of $r$ vectors to the far left, keeping them in their original order, in order to act on them with $B_{r}$. In both cases, permuting vectors to the far left introduces a power of -1 equal to the required number of interchanges of adjacent vectors.

Just as in Theorem 11, each inner product in the sum is a scalar. Since each term picks $r$ elements out of a set $s$ elements, the sum has $\binom{s}{r}$ terms.

Proof. If the result is true for an $r$-blade, it is true for any $r$-vector, so assume $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is an $r$-blade. Now I'll proceed by induction on $r$. If $r=1$ this becomes Theorem 11; so assume it's true for $r-1$. Now $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=a \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$ where $a$ is a vector and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$ is an $r-1$-blade, so using the third of Eqs. (83) I can write

$$
\left.\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{s}\right)=\left(a \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right)\right\rfloor\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{s}\right)
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.=a\rfloor\left[\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{s}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=\sum(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\left(i_{j}-j\right)} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r-1}}\right) \times \\
& \quad a \downharpoonleft\left[a_{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{s}}\right]
\end{aligned}
$$

where $i_{1}$ through $i_{r-1}$ and $i_{r}$ through $i_{s}$ are in ascending order separately. Now I use Theorem 11 to get

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{s}\right)= & \sum(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\left(i_{j}-j\right)} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \downharpoonleft\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r-1}}\right) \times \\
& {\left[\sum_{k=r}^{s}(-1)^{k-r} a \downharpoonleft a_{i_{k}} a_{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i_{k}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{s}}\right] . }
\end{aligned}
$$

This expression has $(s-r+1)\binom{s}{r-1}=r\binom{s}{r}$ terms, which is too many by a factor of $r$, so it's time to do some grouping.

First, notice that each term is a scalar calculated using $r$ of the vectors, multiplied by the outer product of the remaining $s-r$ vectors arranged in ascending order, and that every possible choice of $r$ vectors occurs. That means that for some choice of scalars $C\left(a_{i_{1}}, \ldots, a_{i_{r}}\right)$,

$$
\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{s}\right)=\sum C\left(a_{i_{1}}, \ldots, a_{i_{r}}\right) a_{i_{r+1}} \wedge \ldots \wedge a_{i_{s}}
$$

where the sum is over all choices of $r$ vectors out of the set of $s$. All that remains is to figure out what the coefficients $C\left(a_{i_{1}}, \ldots, a_{i_{r}}\right)$ are. Well, for a given choice of $a_{i_{1}}$ through $a_{i_{r}}$, the coefficient will include terms in which one of the $a_{i_{j}}$ is in the inner product with $a$ while the others are in the inner product with $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$, or

$$
C\left(a_{i_{1}}, \ldots, a_{i_{r}}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(-1)^{\epsilon_{j}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \downharpoonleft\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i_{j}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) a \downharpoonleft a_{i_{j}}
$$

for some value of $\epsilon_{j}$ for each $j$. This sum has $r$ terms, one for each of the $a_{i_{j}}$, which is exactly the number I need, so now I need to figure out the exponents $\epsilon_{j}$. Remember the mnemonic I've been using: each vector's contribution to $\epsilon_{j}$ equals the difference between its position in the original outer product and the position to which it is moved to compute the inner product. (You can verify that this is true for every vector in Eq. (97) by inspection.) So all we have to do is figure out those positions. First consider every $a_{i_{k}}$ in the inner product with $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$ where $k<j$ : each one is moved from position $i_{k}$ to position $k$, so it contributes $i_{k}-k$ to $\epsilon_{j}$. Now consider the $a_{i_{k}}$ where $k>j$ : each one is moved from position $i_{k}$ to position $k-1$ (because position $j$ is empty), so it contributes $i_{k}-k+1$.

Finally, let's take a look at $a_{i_{j}}$. If its inner product is taken with $a$, then Eq. (97) tells me that it is part of the second group of vectors. Therefore vectors $a_{i_{j+1}}$ through $a_{i_{r}}$ had to be moved to its left, moving it from its original position $i_{j}$ ahead to $i_{j}-j+r$. It is then moved to position $r$ for the inner product with $a$, so its contribution to $\epsilon_{j}$ is $i_{j}-j$. Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
\epsilon_{j} & =\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}\left(i_{k}-k\right)+\sum_{k=j+1}^{r}\left(i_{k}-k+1\right)+i_{j}-j \\
& =\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(i_{k}-k\right)+r-j
\end{aligned}
$$

Putting this in Eq. (99) gets me

$$
C\left(a_{i_{1}}, \ldots, a_{i_{r}}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(i_{k}-k\right)+r-j} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \downharpoonleft\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i_{j}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) a \downharpoonleft a_{i_{j}}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.\left.=(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(i_{k}-k\right)}(-1)^{r-1} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor\left[\sum_{j=1}^{r}(-1)^{1-j}(a\rfloor a_{i_{j}}\right) a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i_{j}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right] \\
& \left.\left.=(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(i_{k}-k\right)}(-1)^{r-1} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor[a\rfloor\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(i_{k}-k\right)}(-1)^{r-1}\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \wedge a\right)\right\rfloor\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) \\
& \left.=(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(i_{k}-k\right)}\left(a \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right)\right\rfloor\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) \\
& \left.=(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(i_{k}-k\right)} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

Comparing this expression for $C\left(a_{i_{1}}, \ldots, a_{i_{r}}\right)$ with the statement of the theorem, I see that I've proved the result. $\square$

It's easy to verify that

$$
\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(i_{j}-j\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(i_{j}-1\right)-\frac{r(r-1)}{2},
$$

so this is another way to write the exponent of -1 in the statement of the theorem. I'll use this later.
Now I can answer some questions left hanging in Section 1.3. I asked how you could tell what $n v n^{-1}$ and $v \wedge n n^{-1}$ are without knowing how they were derived. (Notice I'm using order of operations to drop parentheses.) First let's do $n v n^{-1}$; since this is proportional to $n v n$, I'll look at that instead. We know now that the product of three vectors will in general by the sum of a vector and a trivector, and the trivector is the outer product of the factors. In this case the outer product is $n \wedge v \wedge n$, which vanishes because $n$ appears twice, so the product must be pure vector.

Next let's look at $v \wedge n n^{-1}$; again I'll consider $v \wedge n n$ because the answer will be the same. The easiest thing to do here is expand the product with the final $n$ into inner and outer products:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v \wedge n n & =v \wedge n\lfloor n+v \wedge n \wedge n \\
& =v \wedge n\lfloor n
\end{aligned}
$$

because the $v \wedge n \wedge n$ term vanishes. Even if you didn't know the remaining term had to be a vector, you could figure it out because it starts as a vector and has one grade raising and one grade lowering operation applied to it.

I also asked how to calculate $\left.\left(v \wedge n n^{-1}\right)\right\rfloor n$ to verify that $v \wedge n n^{-1}$ really is perpendicular to $n$. (Remember that ⋅ has changed to 」since we got through Section 2.) Since the inner product of vectors is just the scalar part of their geometric product,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.\left(v \wedge n n^{-1}\right)\right\rfloor n & =\left\langle v \wedge n n^{-1} n\right\rangle \\
& =\langle v \wedge n\rangle \\
& =0
\end{aligned}
$$

since $v \wedge n$ has no scalar part. Easy, huh?

### 4.3. The geometric meaning of the inner and outer products

I've now accomplished all I set out to do in this section except for geometric interpretation. First I'll handle the outer product.

Theorem 15. Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ be nonzero blades where $r, s \geq 1$.

(a) $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ iff $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ share nonzero vectors.
(b) $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, if nonzero, represents the direct sum of the corresponding subspaces.

Proof. This is true because, by the same reasoning used in Theorem 3, $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ iff the factors of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ form a linearly dependent set, which is true iff the subspaces share a nonzero vector. The fact that nonzero $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ represents the direct sum of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ follows immediately, since $a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ iff $a$ is a linear combination of the factors of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. $\square$

So forming the outer product is equivalent to taking the direct sum, and it's nonzero iff the direct sum can be taken.

Next let's move on to the inner product. I already said at the end of Section 4.2 that the inner product combines inclusion in one subspace and orthogonality to the other, and I need some terminology to more conveniently describe this. In Section 3 I defined the orthogonal complement of a subspace: it's the set of all vectors orthogonal to every vector in the subspace, or equivalently the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is the set of all vectors $a$ satisfying $a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0$. Here's another definition: the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is the intersection of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ and the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. Algebraically, $a$ is in the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ iff $a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0$ and $a \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$. Now I'm ready to prove the result.

Theorem 16. Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ be nonzero blades where $r, s \geq 1$.

(a) $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ iff $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ contains a nonzero vector orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.
(b) If $r<s$ then $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, if nonzero, is an $s-r$-blade representing the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.

Again, this is why two different inner products are defined; the geometric roles played by the two factors in the product aren't the same. In contrast, Theorem 15 shows that the roles played by the two factors in the outer product are the same, which is why there's only one outer product. This also explains geometrically why $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ when $r>s$; as I show in the proof, if one subspace is higher-dimensional than other, the larger subspace always contains a nonzero vector that is orthogonal to the smaller one.

Proof. First I'll consider the case $r \leq s$; try to be surprised that the proof is by induction on $r$. The $r=1$ result is taken care of by the proof of Theorem 6 (see both the theorem and the discussion right after the proof), so assume the results have been proved for $r-1$ and consider $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. To prove part (a), let $a_{1}$ be any vector in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$; then for some $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$ I can write $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \wedge a_{1}$, which means

$$
\left.\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \wedge a_{1} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{1} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) .
$$

Suppose $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ contains a vector orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$; then let that vector be $a_{1}$, so $a_{1} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$, so $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$. For the converse, assume $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$; then it follows that $\left.\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)=0$. There are now three possibilities. The first is $\left.a_{1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$, in which case $a_{1}$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ and I'm done. If not, then by the $r-1$ result $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$ contains a vector $a_{2}$ orthogonal to $\left.a_{1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$; if that vector happens to be orthogonal to all of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ then I'm also done. Now for the third case: $a_{2}$ is orthogonal to $\left.a_{1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ but not $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. The proof of Theorem 6 showed that I can factor $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ as $b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}$ where $a_{1} \downharpoonleft b_{1} \neq 0$ while the other $b_{j}$ are orthogonal to $a_{1}$, so

$$
\left.a_{1} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\left(a_{1}\right\rfloor b_{1}\right) b_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s} .
$$

The only way $a_{2}$ can be orthogonal to $\left.a_{1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ but not $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is if $a_{2} \downarrow b_{1} \neq 0$ while $a_{2}$ is orthogonal to all the other $b_{j}$. In that case consider

$$
a=a_{1}-\left(\frac{\left.a_{1}\right\rfloor b_{1}}{\left.a_{2}\right\rfloor b_{1}}\right) a_{2} .
$$

This vector lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$; it's nonzero because $a_{1}$ and $a_{2}$ are linearly independent; and it's orthogonal to all the $b_{j}$, including $b_{1}$, by construction. Thus it's orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. So in all three cases $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ contains a vector orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. To prove part (b), assume $r<s$ and $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=$ $\left.\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) \neq 0$. Then by the $r-1$ result $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is the space of all vectors in $\left.a_{1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ that
are orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$. However, by the $r=1$ result $\left.a_{1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is the orthogonal complement of $a_{1}$ in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, so a vector lies in $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ iff it lies in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ and is orthogonal both to $a_{1}$ and to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$, and thus to all of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. This proves both parts for $r \leq s$.

Now for the case $r>s ; \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ automatically, so I can forget about part (b) and I only need to show that $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ always contains a vector orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. Consider $\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$; either it vanishes or it doesn't. If it doesn't, then it contains vectors in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ and I'm done. If it does vanish, then $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ contains vectors orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$; let $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{p}}$ represent the subspace of all such vectors. If $p=s$, then $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and I'm also done. If $p<s$, then $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{p}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{p}}$ where $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{p}}$ contains no vectors orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, which implies $\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{p}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \neq 0$. Then any vector in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{p}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{p}}$; but by lying in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ the vector is already orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{p}}$, so it's orthogonal to all of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ and the result is proved. $\square$

Incidentally, I'll show in Section 5.2 that $A_{r}\left\lfloor B_{s}=(-1)^{r(s-1)} B_{s}\right\rfloor A_{r}$ for any $r$ - and $s$-vectors, so the geometric interpretation of the right inner product is the same as the left product, but with the factors reversed, which certainly seems reasonable.

As a fun exercise, at this point you might look back at the identities in Eqs. (83) in the special case that $A, B$, and $C$ are blades and try to figure out the geometrical meaning of each one.

To explain the next few theorems, I need some facts about blade $\boldsymbol{A}$ to be proved in Section 5.4.

1. $\boldsymbol{A}^{2}$ is a scalar.
2. $\boldsymbol{A}$ is invertible iff $\boldsymbol{A}^{2} \neq 0$, and $\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{A} / \boldsymbol{A}^{2}$. Therefore $\boldsymbol{A}$ and $\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}$ represent the same subspace.
3. $\boldsymbol{A}$ is invertible iff the inner product is nondegenerate on $\boldsymbol{A}$.

The inner and outer products of blades can shed light on how their subspaces are related. For example, if one subspace lies inside another, their blades are related as follows.

Theorem 17. Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ be nonzero blades where $1 \leq r \leq s$.

(a) If $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a subspace of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, then $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.
(b) The converse is true if either (1) $r=1$ or $s=1$ or (2) $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ or $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is invertible.

Proof. First let $r=1$; then $a \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ iff $a \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$, which is true iff $a$ belongs to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. Now assume the result is true for $r-1$ and let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ be a subspace of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} ; \mathrm{I}$ can write $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} a$ for some vector $a$ such that $a$ and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$ are orthogonal, so the $r=1$ result lets me write

$$
\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} a \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} a\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} .
$$

Now $a\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is the orthogonal complement of $a$ in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, which means $a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ contains $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}$, so by the $r-1$ result

$$
\begin{aligned}
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} & =\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \downharpoonleft\left(a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) \\
& \left.=\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \wedge a\right)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \\
& =\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}},
\end{aligned}
$$

which is the desired result. (If $a\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ also by Theorem 16, so if one side vanishes then so does the other.) I've already shown the converse is true when $r=1$, and $s=1$ implies $r=1$, so assume $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is invertible; then $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ implies $\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. By assumption $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \neq 0$, so $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \neq 0$ also. Therefore if $r=s$, then $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is just a nonzero multiple of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$. Since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ represent the same subspace, $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ also represent the same subspace. If $r<s$, then since $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is an $s$-vector, $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ must be too; but only its highest grade term, its outer product, has grade $s$, so for this relation to hold the product must equal the outer product, so $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \wedge\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)$. Therefore $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is the direct sum of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$;
but $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ represents the same subspace as $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, so $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is obviously a subspace of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. The proof when $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is invertible is similar; $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ implies $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1}$, so if $r=s, \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a nonzero multiple of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. If $r<s$, then since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is an $r$-vector, $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1}$ must be too; but only its lowest grade term, the inner product, has grade $r$, so the product must equal the inner product, so $\left.\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1}$. Therefore, $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a subspace of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1}$, and thus of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. $\square$

Another possible relationship is orthogonality: two subspaces are orthogonal if every vector in one is orthogonal to every vector in the other. In that case, their blades are related as follows.

Theorem 18. Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ be nonzero blades where $r, s \geq 1$.

(a) If $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ are orthogonal, then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.
(b) The converse is true if either (1) $r=1$ or $s=1$ or (2) $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ or $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is invertible.

Proof. To begin, I note that $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ can be written $a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}$ where the $a_{i}$ are orthogonal to each other; similarly, $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ can be expressed $b_{1} b_{2} \cdots b_{s}$ where the $b_{j}$ are also orthogonal to each other. Now suppose that $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ are orthogonal; then all of the $a_{i}$ and $b_{j}$ are orthogonal to each other as well, so now I can use the rule that the product of orthogonal vectors equals their outer product to get

$$
\begin{aligned}
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} & =a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r} b_{1} b_{2} \cdots b_{s} \\
& =a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r} \wedge b_{1} \wedge b_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s} \\
& =\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}
\end{aligned}
$$

To prove the converse, first let $r=1$; then $a \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=a \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ iff $\left.a\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$, which is true iff $a$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. Now assume $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is invertible and let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$; then $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=$ $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. Since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ represents the same subspace as $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, which is a subspace of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, it follows that $\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right\rfloor\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)$, so $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. The proof when $s=1$ or $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is invertible proceeds similarly. $\square$

You may be surprised that the converse parts of these theorems aren't generally true; let me give an example to show why not. Consider an algebra with orthogonal vectors $e_{1}, e_{2}, e_{3}$, and $e_{4}$ such that $e_{2}$ is null but the others aren't. (These four vectors define only a subspace of the full space of vectors, or Axiom 5 would be violated.) Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{3}}=e_{1} e_{2} e_{3}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{3}}=e_{2} e_{3} e_{4}$; then $e_{2}^{2}=0$ implies $\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{3}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{3}}=0$, so $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{3}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{3}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{3}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{3}}=0$ also. However, neither $\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{3}}$ nor $\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{3}}$ is a subspace of the other, so the converse part of Theorem 17 doesn't hold, and the subspaces are not orthogonal to each other (they both contain non-null $e_{3}$ ), so the converse part of Theorem 18 doesn't hold either. Null vectors make life hard sometimes.

Incidentally, $\left.A_{r} B_{s}=A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{s}$ implies $B_{s} A_{r}=B_{s}\left\lfloor A_{r}\right.$ for any $r$ - and $s$-vectors, and the same is true if the inner product is replaced with the outer product (this follows from Eq. (140)), so the last two theorems don't depend on the order of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. Really, it would be weird if they did.

Let me end with a result that combines the previous few theorems in an interesting way. Suppose $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a subspace of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$; then it seems plausible that $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ should be the direct sum of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and its orthogonal complement in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. (For example, three-dimensional Euclidean space is the direct sum of the $z$ axis and its orthogonal complement, the $x y$ plane.) Using our theorems, that suggests something like $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)$. Now that can't be right as it stands because the result shouldn't depend on the weight of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, just its attitude. That's easy to fix, though: maybe $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)$ instead. That turns out to be right, but with some caveats. Let me prove a theorem I need first.

Theorem 19. If $1 \leq r \leq s$ and nonzero blades $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ satisfy $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=$ $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)$.

Proof. Believe it or not, this result can be proved without induction. Suppose the condition is true; then

$$
A_{r}^{2} B_{s}=A_{r}\left(A_{r} B_{s}\right)=A_{r}\left(A_{r} \downharpoonleft B_{s}\right) .
$$

Since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2}$ is a number, the left hand side is an $s$-vector; so the right hand side must be also. Using the same logic as in the proof of Theorem 17, the product on the right hand side must equal the outer product, and that proves the result. $\square$

To interpret this theorem, suppose $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a subspace of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, so $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ by Theorem 17. Now either $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is invertible or it's not; first suppose it is. Then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2} \neq 0$, so taking the result of Theorem 19 and dividing by $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2}$ yields $\left.\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)$, which is the result I was after. Notice I had to assume $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ was invertible to get this, though. What if it isn't? In that case $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ contains a nonzero vector orthogonal to all of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, so one of two things can happen: either that vector is also orthogonal to all of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, so $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$, or it isn't, in which case that vector also lies in $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, so $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)=0$. Since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2}=0$, the theorem nicely includes both of those cases.

## 5. Other operations

Our computational powers have grown by leaps and bounds, but they're not yet complete. Some more operations will be useful to us later, so I'll describe them all here. Please be aware that different authors use different symbols for some of these operations; I've listed all my symbol choices in Appendix A.

### 5.1. Grade involution

The first has the formidable name grade involution. It is represented by an * and defined as follows.

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda^{*} & :=\lambda \\
a^{*} & :=-a \\
(A B)^{*} & :=A^{*} B^{*} \\
(A+B)^{*} & :=A^{*}+B^{*} .
\end{aligned}
$$

This operation takes reflection of vectors through the origin (the second line in the definition), sometimes called the parity operation, and extends it to the whole algebra. From these rules it follows that

$$
\left(a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right)^{*}=(-1)^{r} a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r},
$$

which implies

$$
A_{r}^{*}=(-1)^{r} A_{r},
$$

so grade involution leaves even grades alone while changing the sign of odd-grade multivectors, or

$$
A^{*}=\langle A\rangle_{+}-\langle A\rangle_{-} .
$$

This is equivalent to the occasionally handy result

$$
\langle A\rangle_{ \pm}=\frac{1}{2}\left(A \pm A^{*}\right) .
$$

Eq. (114) tells me that

$$
\langle A\rangle_{r}^{*}=\left\langle A^{*}\right\rangle_{r},
$$

so grade involution commutes with taking the grade- $r$ part, and

$$
A^{* *}=A
$$

for any multivector $A$. (That's what makes it an involution.) Suppose $A$ is invertible; then since $\left(A^{-1}\right)^{*} A^{*}=$ $\left(A^{-1} A\right)^{*}=1^{*}=1$,

$$
\left(A^{-1}\right)^{*}=\left(A^{*}\right)^{-1} .
$$

By projecting onto terms of appropriate grade, the third rule in the definition becomes

$$
(A\rfloor B)^{*}=A^{*} \downharpoonleft B^{*}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(A\lfloor B)^{*}\right. & =A^{*}\left\lfloor B^{*}\right. \\
(A \wedge B)^{*} & =A^{*} \wedge B^{*} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Formulas with factors of $(-1)^{r}$ are usually simplified by grade involution. For example, Eqs. (42) and (51) for the inner and outer products of vector $a$ with multivector $A$ become

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft A & =\frac{1}{2}\left(a A-A^{*} a\right) \\
a \wedge A & =\frac{1}{2}\left(a A+A^{*} a\right),
\end{aligned}
$$

the definitions of $A\lfloor a$ and $A \wedge a$ from Eqs. (43) and (52) become

$$
\begin{aligned}
& A\lfloor a=-a\rfloor A^{*} \\
& A \wedge a=a \wedge A^{*}
\end{aligned}
$$

and finally the identities from Eqs. (74) and (82) can now be written

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft(A B) & =(a \downharpoonleft A) B+A^{*}(a \downharpoonleft B) \\
& =(a \wedge A) B-A^{*}(a \wedge B) \\
a \wedge(A B) & \left.=(a \wedge A) B-A^{*}(a\rfloor B\right) \\
& =(a \downharpoonleft A) B+A^{*}(a \wedge B) .
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft(A \wedge B) & \left.=(a\lrcorner A) \wedge B+A^{*} \wedge(a\lrcorner B\right) \\
a \wedge(A\lfloor B) & =(a \wedge A)\left\lfloor B-A^{*}\lfloor(a\rfloor B)\right. \\
a \wedge(A\rfloor B) & \left.=(a\rfloor A)\rfloor B+A^{*}\right\rfloor(a \wedge B) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Every once in a while I'll use ${ }^{* r}$ to indicate grade involution taken $r$ times. $A^{* r}$ equals $A$ if $r$ is even and $A^{*}$ if $r$ is odd.

This operation is called "inversion" by some authors, but that can be confused with the multiplicative inverse, which would be bad because both are important and are used frequently (sometimes at the same time).

A blade and its grade involution represent the same subspace since each is a multiple of the other by Eq. (114).

### 5.2. Reversion

The second operation is called reversion or taking the reverse and is represented by a ${ }^{\dagger}$. It's a little more complicated, and it's defined as follows.

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda^{\dagger} & :=\lambda \\
a^{\dagger} & :=a \\
(A B)^{\dagger} & :=B^{\dagger} A^{\dagger} \\
(A+B)^{\dagger} & :=A^{\dagger}+B^{\dagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

From this it follows that, for example,

$$
\left(a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right)^{\dagger}=a_{r} \cdots a_{2} a_{1},
$$

which shows that the reverse of any multivector is found by writing it as a sum of blades and reversing the order of the vectors in each blade. Hence the name. This also implies

$$
A^{\dagger \dagger}=A
$$

for any multivector $A$. So reversion is also an involution.
Let $\left\{e_{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, r}$ be an anticommuting set; then

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(e_{1} e_{2} \cdots e_{r}\right)^{\dagger} & =e_{r} \cdots e_{2} e_{1} \\
& =(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2} e_{1} e_{2} \cdots e_{r}
\end{aligned}
$$

because $r(r-1) / 2$ interchanges are needed to return the vectors to their original order; therefore

$$
A_{r}^{\dagger}=(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2} A_{r} .
$$

If you evaluate this expression for different $r$, you quickly find (a) multivectors two grades apart behave oppositely under reversion, and (b) two adjacent grades behave the same under reversion iff the lower grade is even (scalars and vectors, for example). Since the effect of either reversion or grade involution is to change signs of a multivector grade by grade, these operations commute:

$$
A^{* \dagger}=A^{\dagger *} .
$$

Eq. (129) also shows that

$$
\langle A\rangle_{r}^{\dagger}=\left\langle A^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{r},
$$

so taking the reverse commutes with taking the grade- $r$ part, just as grade involution does. Suppose $A$ is invertible; then since $\left(A^{-1}\right)^{\dagger} A^{\dagger}=\left(A A^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}=1^{\dagger}=1$,

$$
\left(A^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}=\left(A^{\dagger}\right)^{-1} .
$$

By projecting onto terms of appropriate grade, the third rule in the definition becomes

$$
\begin{aligned}
(A\rfloor B)^{\dagger} & =B^{\dagger}\left\lfloor A^{\dagger}\right. \\
\left(A\lfloor B)^{\dagger}\right. & \left.=B^{\dagger}\right\rfloor A^{\dagger} \\
(A \wedge B)^{\dagger} & =B^{\dagger} \wedge A^{\dagger} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Among other things, this shows that the right inner product can be defined in terms of the left inner product and reversion and is thus technically redundant. Oh well.

Here's an easy application of Eq. (129):

$$
\begin{aligned}
\langle A B\rangle_{r} & =(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2}\left\langle(A B)^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{r} \\
& =(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2}\left\langle B^{\dagger} A^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{r}
\end{aligned}
$$

with the nice special case

$$
\langle A B\rangle=\left\langle B^{\dagger} A^{\dagger}\right\rangle .
$$

The even more special case where $A$ and $B$ are homogeneous is also useful. The product of two homogeneous multivectors of different grades doesn't have a scalar part, so $\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle=\left\langle B_{s} A_{r}\right\rangle=0$ if $r \neq s$, and when $r=s$ we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left\langle A_{r} B_{r}\right\rangle & =\left\langle B_{r}^{\dagger} A_{r}^{\dagger}\right\rangle \\
& =(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2}(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2}\left\langle B_{r} A_{r}\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle B_{r} A_{r}\right\rangle .
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore $\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle=\left\langle B_{s} A_{r}\right\rangle$ in general. Since the geometric product is distributive, I can go all the way to

$$
\langle A B\rangle=\langle B A\rangle
$$

for any $A$ and $B$, or better yet

$$
\langle A B \cdots C D\rangle=\langle D A B \cdots C\rangle,
$$

so the scalar part of a product is cyclic in its factors. This is very useful. Eqs. (135) and (137) together also imply

$$
\langle A B\rangle=\left\langle A^{\dagger} B^{\dagger}\right\rangle .
$$

Almost every identity involving reverses is proved by successively applying Eq. (129) and using the rules in Eqs. (125). For example, let's examine a generic term in $A_{r} B_{s}$ :

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r+s-2 j} & =(-1)^{(r+s-2 j)(r+s-2 j-1) / 2}\left\langle\left(A_{r} B_{s}\right)^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{r+s-2 j} \\
& =(-1)^{(r+s-2 j)(r+s-2 j-1) / 2}\left\langle B_{s}^{\dagger} A_{r}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{r+s-2 j} \\
& =(-1)^{(r+s-2 j)(r+s-2 j-1) / 2}(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2}(-1)^{s(s-1) / 2}\left\langle B_{s} A_{r}\right\rangle_{r+s-2 j} \\
& =(-1)^{r s-j}\left\langle B_{s} A_{r}\right\rangle_{r+s-2 j}
\end{aligned}
$$

So multiplication may not commute, but $A_{r} B_{s}$ and $B_{s} A_{r}$ aren't totally unrelated; term by term, they're actually equal up to signs. (Also, successive terms of $A_{r} B_{s}$, whose grades differ by 2 , have opposite behavior under reversion, which I expected given what I said after Eq. (129).) This result also has two important special cases. First, suppose $r \leq s$; then Eq. (140) with $j=r$ refers to the lowest grade term, so

$$
\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{s}=(-1)^{r(s-1)} B_{s}\left\lfloor A_{r} .\right.
$$

Notice that this expression also holds when $r>s$ because both sides vanish. So the inner product of an odd-grade multivector into an even-grade multivector anticommutes (with left changing to right and vice versa), as in

$$
A_{-} \downharpoonleft B_{+}=-B_{+}\left\lfloor A_{-},\right.
$$

but in all other cases it commutes. Without any restrictions on $r$ and $s$, Eq. (140) when $j=0$ gives for the highest grade term

$$
A_{r} \wedge B_{s}=(-1)^{r s} B_{s} \wedge A_{r},
$$

so the outer product of two odd-grade multivectors anticommutes like so,

$$
A_{-} \wedge B_{-}=-B_{-} \wedge A_{-},
$$

with all other cases commuting. (These last few results are equivalent to Eq. (133), by the way.)
The properties of objects under reversion are sometimes helpful in sorting out their grades. As an example, let me reconsider the product $n v n$ of three vectors from Section 1.3. Notice that $(n v n)^{\dagger}=n v n$. Now vectors don't change sign under reversion but trivectors do. Therefore $n v n$ has no trivector component and is pure vector.

A blade and its reverse represent the same subspace since each is a multiple of the other by Eq. (129).

### 5.3. Clifford conjugation

The third involution in a geometric algebra is called Clifford conjugation or taking the Clifford conjugate. It's represented by a ${ }^{\ddagger}$ and defined as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda^{\ddagger} & :=\lambda \\
a^{\ddagger} & :=-a \\
(A B)^{\ddagger} & :=B^{\ddagger} A^{\ddagger} \\
(A+B)^{\ddagger} & :=A^{\ddagger}+B^{\ddagger} .
\end{aligned}
$$

If this looks like a mixture of grade involution and reversion, that's because it is; in fact,

$$
A^{\ddagger}=A^{* \dagger} .
$$

This immediately tells us that Clifford conjugation really is an involution,

$$
A^{\ddagger \ddagger}=A,
$$

that it commutes with taking the grade- $r$ part,

$$
\langle A\rangle_{r}^{\ddagger}=\left\langle A^{\ddagger}\right\rangle_{r} \text {, }
$$

that when $A$ is invertible

$$
\left(A^{-1}\right)^{\ddagger}=\left(A^{\ddagger}\right)^{-1},
$$

and finally

$$
\begin{aligned}
(A\rfloor B)^{\ddagger} & =B^{\ddagger}\left\lfloor A^{\ddagger}\right. \\
\left(A\lfloor B)^{\ddagger}\right. & \left.=B^{\ddagger}\right\rfloor A^{\ddagger} \\
(A \wedge B)^{\ddagger} & =B^{\ddagger} \wedge A^{\ddagger} .
\end{aligned}
$$

The Clifford conjugate of an $r$-vector is given by

$$
\begin{aligned}
A_{r}^{\ddagger} & =A_{r}^{* \dagger} \\
& =(-1)^{r}(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2} A_{r} \\
& =(-1)^{r(r+1) / 2} A_{r} .
\end{aligned}
$$

This looks a lot like reversion. If you evaluate this for different $r$ you find that (a) multivectors two grades apart behave oppositely under Clifford conjugation, just as with reversion, but (b) two adjacent grades behave the same under Clifford conjugation iff the lower grade is odd, not even (vectors and bivectors, for example). So Clifford conjugation resembles reversion with grades shifted by 1, so to speak.

A blade and its Clifford conjugate represent the same subspace since each is a multiple of the other by Eq. (151).

### 5.4. The scalar product

Next is the scalar product, defined by

$$
A * B:=\left\langle A^{\dagger} B\right\rangle .
$$

(Some authors define $A * B=\langle A B\rangle$. I'll tell you why I don't shortly.) First consider the scalar product of homogeneous multivectors. Only the lowest-grade term in the product (the inner product) has any chance of being a scalar, so it's certainly true that

$$
\left.A_{r} * B_{s}=\left\langle A_{r}^{\dagger}\right\rfloor B_{s}\right\rangle=\left\langle A_{r}^{\dagger}\left\lfloor B_{s}\right\rangle .\right.
$$

Since the scalar product and inner products are distributive by construction, it follows that

$$
A * B=\left\langle A^{\dagger} \downharpoonleft B\right\rangle=\left\langle A^{\dagger}\lfloor B\rangle\right.
$$

for any multivectors. Now of course we actually know a little more than Eq. (153) lets on. Only the product of two equal-grade homogeneous multivectors has a scalar part, so

$$
\left.A_{r} * B_{s}=\left(A_{r}^{\dagger}\right\rfloor B_{s}\right) \delta_{r s}=\left(A_{r}^{\dagger}\left\lfloor B_{s}\right) \delta_{r s} .\right.
$$

Therefore, homogeneous multivectors of different grades are orthogonal under the scalar product. That means the scalar product of two general multivectors may also be written

$$
\begin{aligned}
A * B & =\sum_{r} A_{r} * B_{r} \\
& \left.=\sum_{r} A_{r}^{\dagger}\right\rfloor B_{r}=\sum_{r} A_{r}^{\dagger}\left\lfloor B_{r} .\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

This also makes it clear that

$$
A * B=A^{*} * B^{*} .
$$

My results for reversion and Clifford conjugation also establish some properties of this product; for example,

$$
A * B=B * A=A^{\dagger} * B^{\dagger}=A^{\ddagger} * B^{\ddagger}
$$

The next to last equality shows that an equivalent definition of the scalar product is $\left\langle A B^{\dagger}\right\rangle$. The scalar product interacts with the other products we know this way.

Theorem 20.

$$
\begin{aligned}
A *(B C) & =\left(B^{\dagger} A\right) * C \\
A *(B\lfloor C) & =\left(B^{\dagger}\llcorner A) * C\right. \\
A *(B\rfloor C) & =\left(B^{\dagger} \wedge A\right) * C \\
A *(B \wedge C) & \left.=\left(B^{\dagger}\right\rfloor A\right) * C
\end{aligned}
$$

Proof. The first identity is proved as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}
A *(B C) & =\left\langle A^{\dagger} B C\right\rangle \\
& =\left(A^{\dagger} B\right)^{\dagger} * C \\
& =\left(B^{\dagger} A\right) * C .
\end{aligned}
$$

The remaining three are proved roughly the same way, so I'll prove only the first one. Using Eqs. (156) and (133) and the second of Eqs. (83),

$$
\begin{aligned}
A *(B\lfloor C) & =\left\langle A^{\dagger}\right\rfloor(B\lfloor C)\rangle \\
& =\left\langle\left(A^{\dagger}\right\rfloor B\right)\lfloor C\rangle \\
& \left.=\left(A^{\dagger}\right\rfloor B\right)^{\dagger} * C \\
& =\left(B^{\dagger}\lfloor A) * C .\right.
\end{aligned}
$$ $\square$

The last of Eqs. (159) is the basis for a different approach to geometric algebra, followed for example in [5]. You start by defining the outer product and the scalar product; then you decide you'd like to be able to factor the term $B$ out of expressions like $A *(B \wedge C)$. You do this by defining an inner product that obeys the last of Eqs. (159). Then you define the geometric product of two vectors to be the sum of their inner and outer products, and you're off and running. This has the advantage that it starts with two products that have clearly separated geometric functions: the outer product builds subspaces out of vectors, and the scalar product carries all the metric information. It's thus more congenial to the point of view inherent in differential forms, which are built using only an outer product and which clearly separate metric and non-metric properties. Personally, I think it's cleaner to start with the fundamental product and define every other product directly in terms of it, which is why I follow the approach given here.

I use the scalar product to define the magnitude or norm of a multivector by

$$
|A|^{2}:=A * A .
$$

$A$ is said to be null if $|A|^{2}=0$ and a unit multivector if $|A|^{2}= \pm 1$. (Despite the notation, $|A|^{2}$ can be negative. In fact, $|A|^{2}$ can be all sorts of things, since the scalars aren't necessarily real numbers.) Eqs. (157) and (158) imply

$$
|A|^{2}=\left|A^{*}\right|^{2}=\left|A^{\dagger}\right|^{2}=\left|A^{\ddagger}\right|^{2} .
$$

I define $|A|^{n}$ for other powers $n$ in the obvious way as a power of $|A|^{2}$, but due care should be taken that the power in question is well-defined (for example, be careful if $|A|^{2}$ is negative).

The squared magnitude of a scalar or vector is just its square, and that result can be generalized a bit. Suppose $A$ is an $r$-versor, so it is a product $a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}$; then

$$
\begin{aligned}
|A|^{2} & =A * A=\left\langle A^{\dagger} A\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle\left(a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right)^{\dagger} a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle a_{r} \cdots a_{2} a_{1} a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right\rangle \\
& =a_{1}^{2} a_{2}^{2} \cdots a_{r}^{2}
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore $|A|^{2}$ is the product of the squares of its factors. (This is why I included the reverse in the definition.) Notice also that if $A$ is a versor then $A^{\dagger} A$ also equals $|A|^{2}$. This gives me a couple of useful results.

First, versors can be factored out of scalar products in an interesting way.
Theorem 21. Versor $A$ and general multivectors $B$ and $C$ obey

$$
(A B) *(A C)=(B A) *(C A)=|A|^{2} B * C .
$$

Therefore if either $A$ or $B$ is a versor,

$$
|A B|^{2}=|A|^{2}|B|^{2} .
$$

Proof.

$$
\begin{aligned}
(A B) *(A C) & =\left\langle(A B)^{\dagger} A C\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle B^{\dagger} A^{\dagger} A C\right\rangle \\
& \left.=\left.\langle | A\right|^{2} B^{\dagger} C\right\rangle \\
& =|A|^{2} B * C
\end{aligned}
$$

and a similar argument using Eq. (138) proves the other part of the equation. The second part follows by setting $B=C$. $\square$

Second, versors are easy to invert.
Theorem 22. Versor $A$ is invertible iff it's non-null, its inverse is given by

$$
A^{-1}=\frac{A^{\dagger}}{|A|^{2}},
$$

and the squared norm of the inverse is given by

$$
\left|A^{-1}\right|^{2}=|A|^{-2} .
$$

Proof. If $|A| \neq 0$, then clearly Eq. (168) gives an inverse of $A$, so $A$ must be invertible. Conversely, suppose $A$ is invertible; then there exists a $B$ such that $A B=1$. Then it follows that $B^{\dagger} A^{\dagger}=1$ also, so

$$
\begin{aligned}
1 & =B^{\dagger} A^{\dagger} A B \\
& =|A|^{2} B^{\dagger} B,
\end{aligned}
$$

so $|A| \neq 0$; thus a product of vectors is invertible iff it's non-null, and its inverse is given by the above expression. For the squared norm, just calculate $\left|A^{-1}\right|^{2}$ using Eq. (168). $\square$

An $r$-blade $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a special type of $r$-versor, so these theorems apply to blades too. But for blades, a few more results are also true. Since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\dagger}=(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}},|A|^{2}=A^{\dagger} A$ becomes $\left|\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right|^{2}=(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2}$. Therefore the norm of an $r$-blade differs from its square at most by a sign. That means unit $r$-blades also satisfy $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2}= \pm 1$, although that ±1 may not be the blade's squared norm. It also follows that the inverse of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ equals the additional expressions

$$
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}=(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2} \frac{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}{\left|\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right|^{2}}=\frac{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2}} .
$$

So the inverse of an $r$-blade is a multiple of the original $r$-blade, just as with vectors. Therefore they represent the same subspace.

In Section 1.3, I asked how you would calculate the inverse of 2-blade $a \wedge b$. Well, now we know: divide the original blade by its square. I actually calculated $(a \wedge b)^{2}$ in Section 1.1, and the result was $-a^{2} b^{2} \sin ^{2} \theta$. Therefore

$$
(a \wedge b)^{-1}=\frac{b \wedge a}{a^{2} b^{2} \sin ^{2} \theta}
$$

By the way, this is also the reverse of $a \wedge b$ divided by its norm squared, as it should be.
Next, I give a geometric property of null blades.
Theorem 23. A nonzero blade is null (and thus noninvertible) iff the inner product is degenerate on the subspace it represents.

Proof. $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=e_{1} e_{2} \cdots e_{r}$ is null iff $e_{i}^{2}=0$ for at least one $i$, in which case $e_{i}$ is orthogonal to every vector in the span of $\left\{e_{j}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, r}$, which is just $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. That means that either (a) $e_{i}=0$ or (b) $e_{i} \neq 0$ but the inner product is degenerate on $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. Since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \neq 0$, none of the $e_{i}$ vanish, so that leaves case (b): the inner product must be degenerate. Therefore nonzero $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is null iff the inner product is degenerate on $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. $\square$

So every nonzero blade is invertible in a Euclidean space, while in non-Euclidean spaces things aren't as simple.

And here's an interesting property of products of versors.
Theorem 24. If $r, s \geq 1$ and nonzero versors $A_{r}$ and $B_{s}$ satisfy $A_{r} B_{s}=0$, then both versors are null.
Proof. This one is easy: if $A_{r} B_{s}=0$, then $\left|A_{r}\right|^{2} B_{s}=A_{r}^{\dagger} A_{r} B_{s}=0$ also. Now since $B_{s}$ is assumed nonzero, it follows that $\left|A_{r}\right|^{2}=0$, or $A_{r}$ is null. Going back to $A_{r} B_{s}=0$ and multiplying from the right by $B_{s}^{\dagger}$ establishes that $B_{s}$ is also null. $\square$

This also means that the product of a non-null versor and any versor is nonzero.
A special case of this theorem arises if a vector $a$ both lies in blade $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\left(a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0\right)$ and is orthogonal to it $\left.(a\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0\right)$. In that case $a \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0$, and we say a annihilates $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. The theorem tells us that $a$ must be a null vector and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ must be a null blade, which is clear from Theorem 23 since the existence of such a vector makes the inner product degenerate on $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$.

To conclude this section, I assume the scalars are real so I can define the weight of an $r$-blade, as I said I would back in Section 3. The weight is supposed to be a higher-dimensional generalization of volume, and one way to get that is the following: express $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ as a product of $r$ orthogonal vectors, and define the weight to be the product of the lengths of those vectors. Then the weight is the volume of an $r$-dimensional parallelepiped that spans the correct subspace. That's what the norm gives us, as Eq. (164) shows, so I define

$$
\operatorname{weight}\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right):=\sqrt{\left\|\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\|^{2} \mid} .
$$

The extra || is under the square root because, as I've repeatedly mentioned, the squared norm can be negative. By this definition when $r=0$, the weight of a scalar is its absolute value. This definition only works on scalars for which an absolute value and square root are defined, which is why I'm defining it only for real algebras.

When I get to integral calculus on geometric algebras, I'll be using the weights of blades not to define the theory but to interpret parts of it. Thus integration will be defined on any geometric algebra, but some of its meaning will apply only to real algebras. Since all of our applications will be on real algebras, I think we'll be fine.

### 5.5. The dual

The next operation is called a duality transformation or taking the dual. Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ be an invertible $r$-blade; then the dual of any multivector $B$ by $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is $\left.B\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$. (Duality gets its own symbol only in a special case, which I'll describe below.) To understand what taking the dual does, let $B$ be a $s$-blade $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.

1. If $s>r$, the dual of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ vanishes.
2. If $s=r$, the dual of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is a scalar which is zero iff $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ contains a vector orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ (Theorem (16).
3. If $s<r$, the dual of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is either zero or an $r-s$-blade representing the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ (Theorem 16 again). If $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ was inside $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ to begin with, the dual of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is just $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ (Theorem 17).

The dual of an arbitrary $B$ is a sum of these results. Duality transformations are useful both for taking orthogonal complements of blades (based on the observations above) and for performing orthogonal projections into subspaces, as I'll show in Section 7.1.

Although one can take the dual by any invertible blade, one class of blades is by far the most important: those that represent the entire vector space. The dual by these blades is very useful and also has simpler properties than the dual in general.

Let the dimension of the vector space be $n$; then all $n$-blades either vanish identically (if the factors are dependent) or represent the same subspace (namely the whole space); therefore Theorem 4 says that all $n$-blades are multiples of one another. Since the inner product on the whole space is nondegenerate by Axiom 5, Theorem 23 says that all nonzero $n$-blades are also invertible and thus non-null, so I define a volume element $\boldsymbol{I}$ to be a unit $n$-blade. This determines $\boldsymbol{I}$ to within a sign. (Some people call a volume element a pseudoscalar, but I won't.) In fact, I can calculate $|\boldsymbol{I}|^{2}$ explicitly. Let $\left\{e_{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, r}$ be an orthonormal basis, and suppose $p$ of the $e_{i}$ square to -1 while the rest square to 1 . Let $\boldsymbol{I}=e_{1} e_{2} \cdots e_{n}$; then using Eq. (164),

$$
|\boldsymbol{I}|^{2}=e_{1}^{2} e_{2}^{2} \cdots e_{n}^{2}=(-1)^{p} .
$$

Therefore $|\boldsymbol{I}|^{2}=1$ in any Euclidean space, while $|\boldsymbol{I}|^{2}=-1$ in Minkowski space. Since $|\boldsymbol{I}|^{2}=\boldsymbol{I}^{\dagger} \boldsymbol{I}$, this implies

$$
\boldsymbol{I}^{2}=(-1)^{n(n-1) / 2+p} .
$$

Given Theorem 23, we can now see that Axiom 5 is just another way to say "volume elements are invertible." I could have used instead a weaker axiom that implies only "volume elements are nonzero," and that would have been enough to prove some foundational results, like this one that I've been promising for some time.

Theorem 25. The outer product of linearly independent vectors is nonzero.
Proof. Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ be the outer product of linearly independent vectors. Since volume elements are nonzero, $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ must lie in a subspace represented by a nonzero blade $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$; then by Theorem 5 there exists a blade $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{r}}$ such that $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{r}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. Thus $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a factor of a nonzero blade, so $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is nonzero too. $\square$

This theorem is actually equivalent to "volume elements are nonzero" because each implies the other. Because of this, some authors take this weaker statement as an axiom instead of my Axiom 5. I still like my axiom, though, because if $\boldsymbol{I}$ is invertible, taking the dual by $\boldsymbol{I}$ is also invertible. This makes the dual much more useful, as you'll see below.

Unless otherwise specified, "the dual of $A$ " means "the dual of $A$ by $\boldsymbol{I}$ " and is denoted $A^{\perp}$. Let's reconsider the three ways the dual of a blade can turn out when we're taking the dual by $\boldsymbol{I}$.

1. There are no $s$-blades for $s>n$, so the first option can't happen.
2. Any $n$-blade $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{n}}=\lambda \boldsymbol{I}$ for some $\lambda$, in which case the dual of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{n}}$ is just $\lambda$.

3. If $s<n$, then $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ represents a subspace of the full space, so the dual of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is just $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}$. It cannot be zero; if it were, then Theorem 16 would say that $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ contains a nonzero vector orthogonal to the whole space, which Axiom 5 doesn't allow. The theorem also tells me that the dual of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ represents the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.

So the general formula for the dual of multivector $A$ is

$$
\left.A^{\perp}:=A\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}=A \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}
$$

and the dual of a blade represents its orthogonal complement. (Hence the choice of symbol.) Taking the dual by $\boldsymbol{I}^{-1}$ instead of $\boldsymbol{I}$ is the inverse operation; it's denoted by $A^{-\perp}$. Since $\boldsymbol{I}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{I} / \boldsymbol{I}^{2}, A^{\perp}$ and $A^{-\perp}$ differ only by a factor of $\boldsymbol{I}^{2}$, so the duality transformation is its own inverse up to at most a sign.

Since the product of any multivector with $\boldsymbol{I}$ is an inner product, it's true for any $A_{r}$ that

$$
\begin{aligned}
A_{r} \boldsymbol{I} & =(-1)^{r(n-1)} \boldsymbol{I} A_{r} \\
& =\boldsymbol{I} A_{r}^{*(n-1)},
\end{aligned}
$$

so for any multivector $A$,

$$
A \boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{I} A^{*(n-1)} .
$$

This has several consequences.

1. Even multivectors commute with $\boldsymbol{I}$ regardless of the value of $n$, so their duals can be taken be taken from either side with no difference.
2. In odd-dimensional spaces, the dual of any multivector can be taken from either side.
3. In even-dimensional spaces, the dual of an odd multivector can still be taken from either side, and the results differ only by a sign.
4. The first of Eqs. (177) is true even if $A_{r}$ is an $r$-versor because all terms in $A_{r}$ are even or odd as $r$ is even or odd.
5. In even-dimensional spaces Eqs. (116) and (178) can be used to separate the pure even and pure odd parts of a multivector:
$$
\langle A\rangle_{ \pm}=\frac{1}{2}\left(A \pm \boldsymbol{I} A \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}\right) \quad \text { if } n \text { is even. }
$$

Duality lets me prove a surprising result.
Theorem 26. If the vector space is $n$-dimensional, every $n-1$-vector is an $n-1$-blade.
Proof. Let $A_{n-1}$ by an $n-1$-vector. The dual of $A_{n-1}$ is a vector, so $A_{n-1}$ is the dual of a vector. But vectors are 1-blades, and the dual of a blade is also a blade, so $A_{n-1}$ is an $n$-1-blade. $\square$

One corollary of this is that in dimensions below four, all $r$-vectors are actually $r$-blades. 0 -vectors and 1 -vectors are always blades, $n$-vectors are always blades (which takes care of bivectors in two dimensions and trivectors in three), and bivectors in three dimensions are $n-1$-vectors and thus blades.

The dual is distributive over addition, and it's easy to show that

$$
(A B)^{\perp}=A B^{\perp} .
$$

Taking appropriate-grade terms also shows that

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.(A \wedge B)^{\perp}=A\right\rfloor B^{\perp} \\
& (A\rfloor B)^{\perp}=A \wedge B^{\perp} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus the dual relates the inner and outer products. (Here's another way to prove these results: start with the third of Eqs. (83) and set $C=\boldsymbol{I}^{-1}$. That gets you the first equation. Then replace $B$ with $B^{\perp}$ and take the inverse dual of both sides; that gets you the other equation.) A special case of this is $\left(a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)^{\perp}=a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}$, which means vector $a$ is orthogonal to subspace $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ iff $a$ lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}$. That's further confirmation that duals represent orthogonal complements. It also shows that any subspace has a direct representation (all $a$ such that $a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0$ ) and a dual representation (all $a$ such that $a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}=0$ ). These two representations are both useful in different situations.

In the discussion around Theorem 19, I said that if $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is invertible, then any space that contains $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is the direct sum of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and its orthogonal complement. This is certainly true for the whole space, and it's nicely expressed in terms of duals.

Theorem 27. The whole space is the direct sum of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}$ iff $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is invertible.
Proof.

$$
A_{r} \wedge A_{r}^{\perp}=\left(A_{r} \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right)^{\perp}=A_{r}^{2} I^{-1}=\frac{A_{r}^{2}}{I^{2}} I
$$

Now Theorem 22 and Eq. (171) tell me $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is invertible iff $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2} \neq 0$. So if $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is invertible, Eq. (182) shows that $\boldsymbol{I}$ is the direct sum of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and its orthogonal complement; and if $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is not invertible, the equation shows that $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and its dual have vectors in common, so they don't even have a direct sum. $\square$

If $A$ is invertible, so is $A^{\perp}$ :

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(A^{\perp}\right)^{-1} & =\left(A \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}\right)^{-1} \\
& =\boldsymbol{I} A^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

The dual of a grade involution is given by

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(A^{*}\right)^{\perp} & =A^{*} \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} \\
& =(-1)^{n} A^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{I}^{-1}\right)^{*} \\
& =(-1)^{n}\left(A \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}\right)^{*} \\
& =(-1)^{n}\left(A^{\perp}\right)^{*}
\end{aligned}
$$

The dual of a reverse is

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(A^{\dagger}\right)^{\perp} & =\left[\left(A^{\perp} \boldsymbol{I}\right)^{\dagger}\right]^{\perp} \\
& =\left[\boldsymbol{I}^{\dagger}\left(A^{\perp}\right)^{\dagger}\right]^{\perp} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Combining these results, the dual of a Clifford conjugate is

$$
\left(A^{\ddagger}\right)^{\perp}=\left[\boldsymbol{I}^{\ddagger}\left(A^{\perp}\right)^{\ddagger}\right]^{\perp} .
$$

Finally, Theorem 21 and the second part of Theorem 22 show that the dual almost preserves scalar products:

$$
\begin{aligned}
A^{\perp} * B^{\perp} & =\left(A \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}\right) *\left(B \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}\right) \\
& =\left|\boldsymbol{I}^{-1}\right|^{2} A * B \\
& =|\boldsymbol{I}|^{-2} A * B .
\end{aligned}
$$

So taking the dual preserves scalar products up to a scale factor.
Occasionally it's convenient to take the dual by volume elements that aren't normalized. In that case, the dual and its inverse may differ by more than a sign, but the difference is still only a scalar multiple. All the results in this section are valid regardless of the normalization of $\boldsymbol{I}$.

### 5.6. The commutator

The final operation is called the commutator, defined as follows.

$$
A \times B:=\frac{1}{2}(A B-B A) \text {. }
$$

Notice the factor of $\frac{1}{2}$, which is not present in the usual definition of the commutator, used for example in quantum mechanics. The commutator obeys the identity

$$
A \times(B C)=(A \times B) C+B(A \times C),
$$

which is easily verified by expanding out the commutators. This shows that the commutator is a derivation on the algebra (it obeys the Leibnitz rule). Use this identity to expand $A \times(B C)$ and $A \times(C B)$ and take half the difference; the result is the Jacobi identity

$$
A \times(B \times C)=(A \times B) \times C+B \times(A \times C) \text {. }
$$

The presence of the second term on the right hand side shows that the commutator is not associative. This identity is often given in the cyclic form

$$
A \times(B \times C)+B \times(C \times A)+C \times(A \times B)=0 .
$$

From the defining properties of the three involutions it's easy to see that

$$
\begin{aligned}
& (A \times B)^{*}=A^{*} \times B^{*} \\
& (A \times B)^{\dagger}=B^{\dagger} \times A^{\dagger} \\
& (A \times B)^{\ddagger}=B^{\ddagger} \times A^{\ddagger} .
\end{aligned}
$$

The commutator of any multivector with a scalar clearly vanishes, and the commutator with a vector can be expressed nicely by decomposing a general multivector as $A=\langle A\rangle_{+}+\langle A\rangle_{-}$and recalling Eqs. (42) and (51) for the inner and outer products. The result is

$$
\begin{aligned}
