& a \times A=a\rfloor\langle A\rangle_{+}+a \wedge\langle A\rangle_{-} \\
& A \times a=\langle A\rangle_{+}\left\lfloor a+\langle A\rangle_{-} \wedge a .\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

This lets me prove an important result about commuting multivectors.
Theorem 28. The following statements are equivalent.

1. A commutes with all multivectors.
2. A commutes with all vectors.
3. $A=\lambda+\mu\langle\boldsymbol{I}\rangle_{-}$.

Item 3 is my sneaky way of saying $A$ equals $\lambda$ in even-dimensional vector spaces and $\lambda+\mu \boldsymbol{I}$ in odddimensional spaces.

Proof. Since scalars commute with everything, I won't mention them again. If $A$ commutes with all multivectors then it obviously commutes with all vectors. On the other hand, if $A$ commutes with all vectors then it commutes with all blades, since these are products of vectors. Therefore $A$ commutes with all sums of blades, and thus all multivectors.

Now for item 3. The first of Eqs. (193) tells me that $a \times \boldsymbol{I}=a \wedge \boldsymbol{I}$ if the vector space is odd-dimensional and $a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{I}$ if the space is even-dimensional. Now $a \wedge \boldsymbol{I}=0$ and $a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{I} \neq 0$ for all $a$, because every vector lies in $\boldsymbol{I}$ and no vector is orthogonal to it; therefore all vectors commute with $\boldsymbol{I}$ in odd-dimensional spaces and no vectors commute with $\boldsymbol{I}$ in even-dimensional spaces. To finish off, let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ be an $r$-blade where $0<r<n$. If $r$ is even, then $\left.a \times \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=a\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, and if this vanished for all $a$ then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ would be orthogonal to the whole space, in violation of Axiom 5. If $r$ is odd, then $a \times \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. Since $r<n$ there certainly exists a vector $a$ outside $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, in which case $a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \neq 0$. $\square$

The most interesting of all is the commutator with a bivector.
Theorem 29.

$$
A_{2} \times A_{r}=\left\langle A_{2} A_{r}\right\rangle_{r},
$$

so the commutator of a bivector and an $r$-vector is an $r$-vector; commutation with a bivector is a grade preserving operation.

Proof. To show this, I note that

$$
\begin{aligned}
& A_{2} A_{r}=A_{2} \downharpoonleft A_{r}+\left\langle A_{2} A_{r}\right\rangle_{r}+A_{2} \wedge A_{r} \\
& A_{r} A_{2}=A_{2} \downharpoonleft A_{r}-\left\langle A_{2} A_{r}\right\rangle_{r}+A_{2} \wedge A_{r}
\end{aligned}
$$

The first equation is obvious when $r \geq 2$; for $r<2$, recall that in such cases $A_{2} \downharpoonleft A_{r}=0$. The second equation follows from the first because of the properties of the inner and outer products under interchange and Eq. (140) when $j=1$. Subtracting these equations yields

$$
A_{2} \times A_{r}=\frac{1}{2}\left(A_{2} A_{r}-A_{r} A_{2}\right)=\left\langle A_{2} A_{r}\right\rangle_{r}
$$ $\square$

In particular, the set of bivectors is closed under commutation. That means the bivectors form a Lie algebra with the commutator serving as the Lie product. That will be important later when I show how to use geometric algebra to describe Lie groups and Lie algebras.

Since commutation with a bivector is grade preserving, the identity in Eq. (189) still holds if $A=A_{2}$ and I replace all geometric products with either inner or outer products:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.A_{2} \times(B\rfloor C\right) & \left.\left.=\left(A_{2} \times B\right)\right\rfloor C+B\right\rfloor\left(A_{2} \times C\right) \\
A_{2} \times(B\lfloor C) & =\left(A_{2} \times B\right)\left\lfloor C+B\left\lfloor\left(A_{2} \times C\right)\right.\right. \\
A_{2} \times(B \wedge C) & =\left(A_{2} \times B\right) \wedge C+B \wedge\left(A_{2} \times C\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

The last of these relations can be generalized in this way.
Theorem 30.

$$
A_{2} \times\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{r} a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge\left(A_{2} \downharpoonright a_{j}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r} .
$$

Proof. As usual, I use induction. The result is true when $r=1$ because the commutator with a vector is the same as the right inner product, and the $r=2$ result follows from the last of Eqs. (197), so let's assume the result is true for $r-1$. Then by associativity of the outer product

$$
A_{2} \times\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right)=A_{2} \times\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{r-1} \wedge a_{r}\right)
$$

where $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}=a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r-1}$. Applying the last of Eqs. (197) and the $r-1$ result yields

$$
\begin{aligned}
A_{2} \times\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right) & =\left(A_{2} \times \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right) \wedge a_{r}+\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \wedge\left(A_{2} \times a_{r}\right) \\
& =\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\left[a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge\left(A_{2}\left\lfloor a_{j}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r-1} \wedge a_{r}\right]+\right. \\
& a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r-1} \wedge\left(A_{2}\left\llcorner a_{r}\right)\right. \\
& =\sum_{j=1}^{r} a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge\left(A_{2}\left\llcorner a_{j}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

which completes the proof. $\square$

I expand the order of operations to include all of these new operations as follows: perform the involutions, then duals, then outer, then inner, then geometric products, then scalar products, and finally commutators. Following this convention, the parentheses in Eqs. (159) and the left hand sides of Eqs. (189), (197), and (198) (but not the right hand sides) may be omitted.

## 6. Geometric algebra in Euclidean space

Now let's apply everything I've done so far to some familiar cases. I'll work through the algebras of twoand three-dimensional real Euclidean space explicitly, revealing some neat surprises along the way.

### 6.1. Two dimensions and complex numbers

First I'll consider the real plane $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ with the Euclidean scalar product; this is often denoted $\mathbb{E}^{2}$. It has an orthonormal basis $\left\{e_{1}, e_{2}\right\}$, which produces a geometric algebra spanned by the elements $1, e_{1}, e_{2}$, and $e_{1} e_{2}$. That last element satisfies

$$
\left|e_{1} e_{2}\right|^{2}=\left(e_{1} e_{2}\right)^{\dagger} e_{1} e_{2}=e_{2} e_{1} e_{1} e_{2}=1 .
$$

Therefore it qualifies as a volume element $\boldsymbol{I}$. Since bivectors change sign under reversion, it also satisfies $\boldsymbol{I}^{2}=-1$. It defines a right-handed orientation, and a few examples show that all vectors anticommute with I. This is consistent with Eq. (178).

Now for the geometric products. We know what a scalar times anything and a vector times a vector look like; all that remains is the product of a vector and a bivector, or equivalently the product of a vector and I. To see what that does, notice that

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \boldsymbol{I} e_{1}=-e_{2} \\
& \boldsymbol{I} e_{2}=e_{1},
\end{aligned}
$$

so multiplication of a unit vector by $\boldsymbol{I}$ results in an orthogonal unit vector. (Which it should, since multiplying by $\boldsymbol{I}$ takes the dual to within a sign.) Eq. (202) actually tells us a bit more: left multiplication by $\boldsymbol{I}$ rotates a vector clockwise through $\pi / 2$. Similarly, right multiplication rotates a vector counterclockwise through the same angle. So $\boldsymbol{I}^{2}=-1$ means that two rotations in the same sense through $\pi / 2$ have the same effect as multiplying by -1. Of course, this is true only in two dimensions.

The even subalgebra of any geometric algebra is always of interest, so let's take a moment to look at it. A generic even multivector can be written $Z=x+\boldsymbol{I} y$ where $x$ and $y$ are real numbers and $\boldsymbol{I}^{2}=-1$; in other words, the even subalgebra of $\mathbb{E}^{2}$ is isomorphic to the algebra of complex numbers. Now this may be a little bit of a surprise, because the even subalgebra represents scalars and areas, while we normally think of complex numbers as vectors in the Argand plane. But there's another way to think of complex numbers: the polar form $z=r e^{i \theta}$ reminds us that $z$ also represents a rotation through angle $\theta$ followed by a dilatation by $r$. How do these two interpretations of complex numbers relate?

It works out because we're in two dimensions. Then and only then, the even subalgebra is isomorphic to the space of vectors; a generic vector in $\mathbb{E}^{2}$ takes the form $z=x e_{1}+y e_{2}$ where $x$ and $y$ are real numbers, and there's a natural isomorphism between the vectors and the even subalgebra of the form

$$
\begin{aligned}
Z & =e_{1} z \\
z & =e_{1} Z .
\end{aligned}
$$

This isomorphism maps a vector in the $e_{1}$ direction onto a pure "real" number, so $e_{1}$ plays the role of the real axis. It also maps a vector in the $e_{2}$ direction onto a pure "imaginary" number, so $e_{2}$ is the imaginary axis. Now think about complex conjugation: it leaves the real part alone while changing the sign of the imaginary part. Therefore complex conjugation is a reflection along the $e_{2}$ axis, which takes $z$ to $z^{\prime}=-e_{2} z e_{2}$. What happens to the corresponding even element? It gets mapped to

$$
\begin{aligned}
Z^{\prime} & =e_{1} z^{\prime} \\
& =-e_{1}\left(e_{2} z e_{2}\right) \\
& =-e_{1} e_{2}\left(e_{1} Z\right) e_{2} \\
& =-\boldsymbol{I} e_{1}(x+\boldsymbol{I} y) e_{2} \\
& =x-\boldsymbol{I} y \\
& =Z^{\dagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

where I used the fact that $\boldsymbol{I}$ anticommutes with all vectors. Therefore complex conjugation corresponds to taking the reverse. Now let $w$ and $z$ be vectors with corresponding even elements $W$ and $Z$; it follows that

$$
\begin{aligned}
w z & =e_{1} W e_{1} Z \\
& =e_{1}(x+\boldsymbol{I} y) e_{1} Z \\
& =(x-\boldsymbol{I} y) Z \\
& =W^{\dagger} Z
\end{aligned}
$$

Now let's look at this. The right hand side is the product of one complex number with the conjugate of another. That has two terms: the real part equals the dot product of the corresponding vectors, while the magnitude of the imaginary part equals the magnitude of the cross product of the vectors. The left hand side is the geometric product of the vectors, which is exactly the same thing. One of the goals of geometric algebra was to take the complex product, which combines the two-dimensional dot and cross products naturally, and generalize it to any number of dimensions. (That was a goal of quaternions too. I'll show how that worked out in the next section.)

Now for rotations. An element $W$ of the even subalgebra has a polar form $r \exp (-\boldsymbol{I} \theta)$ for some $r$ and $\theta$. Letting $r=1$, multiplication by a vector $z$ produces the vector

$$
\begin{aligned}
z^{\prime} & =W z \\
& =\exp (-\boldsymbol{I} \theta) z \\
& =\exp (-\boldsymbol{I} \theta / 2) \exp (-\boldsymbol{I} \theta / 2) z \\
& =\exp (-\boldsymbol{I} \theta / 2) z \exp (\boldsymbol{I} \theta / 2) \\
& =R z R^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

where I defined $R=\exp (-\boldsymbol{I} \theta / 2)$. Clearly $R$ is a rotor, so multiplication by $W$ performs a counterclockwise rotation through $\theta$. What is the corresponding transformation of $Z$ ?

$$
\begin{aligned}
Z^{\prime} & =e_{1} z^{\prime} \\
& =e_{1} W z \\
& =w z \\
& =W^{\dagger} Z \\
& =\exp (\boldsymbol{I} \theta) Z
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus vector $z$ is rotated counterclockwise through $\theta$ when the corresponding even element $Z$ is multiplied by $\exp (\boldsymbol{I} \theta)$, exactly as you'd expect.

In conclusion, the complex numbers are the even subalgebra of the geometric algebra of the Euclidean plane; the identification with vectors is just an accident in two dimensions, just as identifying planes with normal vectors works only in three dimensions. Now while complex algebra is useful, so is complex analysis; we use its techniques to perform many ostensibly real integrals, for example. If geometric algebra generalizes complex algebra to any dimension, then perhaps calculus of geometric algebras could generalize complex analysis too. I'll describe geometric calculus later, and I'll show how it generalizes the Cauchy integral theorem and other useful results.

### 6.2. Three dimensions, Pauli matrices, and quaternions

Much that is true in two dimensions carries over to three: $\mathbb{E}^{3}$ has an orthonormal basis $\left\{e_{1}, e_{2}, e_{3}\right\}$, so its geometric algebra is spanned by $1, e_{1}, e_{2}, e_{3}, e_{1} e_{2}, e_{1} e_{3}, e_{2} e_{3}$, and the volume element $e_{1} e_{2} e_{3}$. This volume element is also denoted $\boldsymbol{I}$, defines a right-handed orientation, satisfies $|\boldsymbol{I}|^{2}=\boldsymbol{I}^{\dagger} \boldsymbol{I}=1$, and squares to -1. Unlike the two-dimensional case, a few examples show that all vectors and bivectors commute with $\boldsymbol{I}$, as required by Theorem 28. Now I've repeatedly mentioned that only in three dimensions can you identify planes with normal vectors, which is why the cross product works there. The map between planes and
normal vectors should be a duality transformation, so the cross product should be the dual of something. Well, it is. If $a$ and $b$ are vectors, then

$$
a \times b=(a \wedge b)^{\perp} .
$$

So cross products are easily converted into outer products and vice versa. Yay. This shows why the cross product is not associative even though the outer product is; the dual gets in the way. Since duality is just multiplication by $-\boldsymbol{I}$ and vectors and bivectors commute with $\boldsymbol{I}$, I can use Eq. (208) to write

$$
a b=a\rfloor b+\boldsymbol{I} a \times b,
$$

which is the three-dimensional analog of the product $W^{\dagger} Z$ of complex numbers $W$ and $Z$. Another popular product in traditional vector algebra is the triple product $a \cdot b \times c$, which relates to geometric algebra by

$$
a \cdot b \times c=(a \wedge b \wedge c)^{\perp} .
$$

This form makes the cyclic property of the triple product obvious. The triple cross product $a \times(b \times c)$ is also pretty common, and it can be expressed in geometric algebra as

$$
a \times(b \times c)=-a\rfloor(b \wedge c) .
$$

From here, it's easy to see that the BAC-CAB rule for expanding this product is really just a special case of Theorem 11.

I'd like to say a little more about cross products. Since $(a \wedge b)^{\perp}=a \downharpoonleft b^{\perp}$ and in three dimensions $b^{\perp}$ is a bivector, it follows that $a \times b$ is also the inner product of $a$ and a bivector. You may recall that in classical mechanics, linear and angular velocity are related by a cross product: $\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{\omega} \times \boldsymbol{r}$. The geometric algebra equivalent is $v=\Omega\left\lfloor r\right.$, where $\Omega=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\perp}$ is an angular velocity bivector in the instantaneous plane of rotation. (Bivectors figure prominently in rotational dynamics, as I'll show in Section 10.1.) You may also recall that the magnetic part of the Lorentz force on a point charge is $\boldsymbol{F}=q \boldsymbol{v} \times \boldsymbol{B}$, where $\boldsymbol{B}$ is the magnetic field vector. In geometric algebra this becomes $F=q v \downharpoonleft B$, where $B=B^{\perp}$ is the magnetic field bivector. I'll show later on that the bivector representation of $B$ is more physically motivated than the vector version.

A consequence of Theorem 26, which I mentioned at the time, is that in dimensions under four, every $r$-vector is actually an $r$-blade. In two dimensions that was obviously true; we had only scalars, vectors, and multiples of $\boldsymbol{I}$. In three dimensions the scalars, vectors, and trivectors are obviously blades (the trivectors are multiples of $\boldsymbol{I}$ ), and I can show using geometry that all bivectors are 2-blades. Consider two 2-blades $\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}$; each represents a plane passing through the origin, and any two such planes in three dimensions share a common line. Therefore $\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}}=a \wedge b$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}=a \wedge c$ where $a$ is a vector along the line shared by the planes. This means that

$$
\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}}+\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}=a \wedge b+a \wedge c=a \wedge(b+c)
$$

is also a 2-blade. Thus any bivector in three dimensions is a 2-blade, as Theorem 26 demands.
Now for the products. As before, we know what a scalar times anything or a vector times a vector looks like; next I'll do a vector times a bivector. Let $a$ be a vector and $\boldsymbol{B}$ be a bivector; then $a=a_{\|}+a_{\perp}$ where $a_{\|}$lies in the plane determined by $\boldsymbol{B}$ and $a_{\perp}$ is perpendicular to it. In that case there exists a vector $b$ perpendicular to $a_{\|}$such that $\boldsymbol{B}=a_{\|} b$, so

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \boldsymbol{B} & =\left(a_{\|}+a_{\perp}\right) a_{\|} b \\
& =a_{\|}^{2} b+a_{\perp} a_{\|} b \\
& =a_{\|}^{2} b+a_{\perp} \wedge a_{\|} \wedge b .
\end{aligned}
$$

So the product of $a$ and $\boldsymbol{B}$ is the sum of two terms: a vector in the plane of $\boldsymbol{B}$ perpendicular to $a$, and the trivector defined by $\boldsymbol{B}$ and the component of $a$ perpendicular to it. Clearly the vector is $a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}$ and the trivector is $a \wedge \boldsymbol{B}$. The trivector can also be written

$$
\pm\left|a_{\perp}\left\|a_{\|}\right\| b\right| \boldsymbol{I}= \pm\left|a_{\|} \| \boldsymbol{B}\right| \boldsymbol{I}
$$

where | | is the magnitude of a multivector defined in Section 5.4. The ± is there because we don't know the orientation of the system defined by the three vectors.

Vector times trivector is even easier. If $a$ is a vector and $\boldsymbol{T}$ is a trivector, then $\boldsymbol{T}=a b c$ where $b$ and $c$ are perpendicular to each other and to $a$, so

$$
a \boldsymbol{T}=a^{2} b c=a^{2} b \wedge c .
$$

So $a \boldsymbol{T}$ is a bivector representing the plane to which $a$ is perpendicular. This is clearly $a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{T}$.
The product of two bivectors looks like this: since all bivectors are 2-blades representing planes, let vector $a$ lie along the direction shared by bivectors $\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}$, so $\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}}=b a$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}=a c$ where $b$ and $c$ are perpendicular to $a$ but not necessarily to each other; then

$$
\begin{aligned}
\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}} & =b a a c \\
& =a^{2} b c \\
& =a^{2} b \downharpoonleft c+a^{2} b \wedge c .
\end{aligned}
$$

So the product of two bivectors is a scalar plus a bivector representing the plane normal to their intersection line. The first term is $\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}}\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}}\left\lfloor\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}\right.$ and the second term is $\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathbf{2}} \times \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}$.

Next, a bivector times a trivector: if bivector $\boldsymbol{B}=a b$ where $a$ and $b$ are perpendicular, then there exists vector $c$ perpendicular to $a$ and $b$ such that trivector $\boldsymbol{T}=b a c$, in which case

$$
\boldsymbol{B} \boldsymbol{T}=b a a b c=a^{2} b^{2} c,
$$

so the product of a bivector and a trivector is a vector perpendicular to the plane of the bivector. This is also $\boldsymbol{B}\rfloor \boldsymbol{T}$.

The product of two trivectors is just a number. In fact, it's the product of the volumes defined by the two trivectors, with the sign determined by their relative orientations.

The general multiplication rule for the basis vectors can be written as

$$
e_{i} e_{j}=\delta_{i j}+\sum_{k} \boldsymbol{I} \epsilon_{i j k} e_{k},
$$

which is exactly the multiplication rule for the Pauli matrices. Therefore the Pauli matrices are just a matrix representation of the basis vectors of three dimensional space. It is well known that the Pauli matrices form a Euclidean Clifford algebra, but the idea that they are literally matrix representations of $\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}, \hat{\boldsymbol{y}}$, and $\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}$ is not so familiar.

Finally, the even subalgebra of the geometric algebra on $\mathbb{E}^{3}$ has some surprises for us too. Let the unit bivectors be labeled

$$
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{1}}=e_{2} e_{3}, \quad \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}=e_{1} e_{3}, \text { and } \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{3}}=e_{1} e_{2} .
$$

Notice that this definition is not consistently right-handed because of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}$. These objects satisfy the relations

$$
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{1}}^{2}=\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}}^{2}=\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{3}}^{2}=-1
$$

and

$$
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{1}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{2}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\mathbf{3}}=-1,
$$

so the even subalgebra of the algebra on $\mathbb{E}^{3}$, which is spanned by 1 and the $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{i}}$, is isomorphic to the quaternions. The quaternions were created to generalize the complex numbers to three dimensions, of course, so something like this was expected; but the quaternions as Hamilton conceived them were intended to correspond to the three unit directions, not three planes. The map between them works differently in two dimensions and three, so while complex numbers can be thought of consistently as either vectors or bivectors, quaternions can be mapped from one to the other only by introducing an inconsistency in the handedness, as I've done here.

## 7. More on projections, reflections, and rotations

In Sections 1.1 and 1.2 I introduced projections along vectors, reflections along vectors, and rotations in planes. My purpose was to get you interested in geometric algebra by showing how well it handled all three operations compared to traditional vector algebra. Well, there's more. It turns out that these operations can be defined on subspaces just as well as vectors; for example, rotating a subspace means rotating all the vectors in it. As I'll show, in geometric algebra this is very easy, and the resulting formulas are almost the same as the formulas for vectors.

### 7.1. Orthogonal projections and rejections

Let's restate what I did in Sections 1.1 and 1.2 a little differently. Let $u$ and $v$ be vectors; then the orthogonal projection of $v$ along $u$ is given by

$$
\left.\left.P_{u}(v)=v \downharpoonleft u u^{-1}=(v\rfloor u\right)\right\rfloor u^{-1}
$$

and the orthogonal rejection of $v$ from $u$ is given by

$$
R_{u}(v)=v \wedge u u^{-1}=v \wedge u\left\lfloor u^{-1} .\right.
$$

(The second parts of each equation are easy to verify.) $P_{u}(v)$ is parallel to $u, R_{u}(v)$ is orthogonal to $u$, and $P_{u}(v)+R_{u}(v)=v$. These operations require $u$ to be invertible, so it can't be a null vector. I promised in Section 1.2 that this would have geometrical meaning, and now we're about to see what it is.

### 7.1.1. Projecting a vector into a subspace

Let's take a moment to consider the general notion of projection into a subspace. Let $S$ be a subspace ( $S$ is not a blade this time; it really is the subspace itself) and let $a$ be a vector not in $S$. Then for any $v \in S$ I can write $a=v+(a-v)$, which is the sum of a vector in $S$ and a vector not in $S$. So which $v$ is the "projection" of $a$ into $S$ ? We can't say without further information. For example, consider two subspaces $S_{1}$ and $S_{2}$ that share only the zero vector; then if a vector lies in their direct sum, it can be expressed only one way as a vector from $S_{1}$ plus a vector from $S_{2}$, and thus has a unique projection into either subspace. Projection into a subspace is specified not only by the subspace itself but also by the subspace the rest of the vector will belong to, and the operation is well-defined only if the two subspaces share only the zero vector.

Now consider orthogonal projection as an example of this. The idea is to express a vector as a sum of two terms, one in subspace $S$ and one in $S^{\perp}$, the orthogonal complement of $S$. This works only if $S$ and $S^{\perp}$ have no nonzero vectors in common, which is true iff the inner product is nondegenerate on $S$. Thus orthogonal projection is well-defined only for a subspace with an invertible blade. In that case, I get this result.

Theorem 31. If $a$ is a vector and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is an invertible blade, then the orthogonal projection of a into and the orthogonal rejection of a from subspace $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ are given by

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}(a)=a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}=\left(a \downarrow \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a)=a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}=a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\left\lfloor\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

Proof. First, it's clear that $P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a)+R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a)=a$. Now, $\left.a\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is the dual of $a$ by $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$; it is zero if $a$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, and otherwise it is an $r-1$-blade representing the subspace of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ orthogonal to $a$. In that case its product with $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ equals its inner product with $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$, which is just the dual by $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$; the result is a vector that lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. On the other hand, $a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is zero if $a$ lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, and otherwise it is an $r+1$-blade that contains $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. In that case the product with $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ equals the right inner product, and is the dual of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ by $\left(a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)^{-1}$, so the result is a vector orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. Both formulas give vectors, they sum to $a$, the first lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and vanishes iff $a$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, and the second is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and vanishes iff $a$ lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. Therefore the two expressions are obviously the orthogonal projection of $a$ into and the orthogonal rejection of $a$ from $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. $\square$

So projecting into a subspace is the same as projecting onto a vector; you just replace the vector with the blade representing the subspace. We'll see several more examples of this idea below.

I can demonstrate directly that $P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a)$ lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ :

$$
\left.\left.P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a) \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\left(a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right) \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\langle a\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rangle_{r+1}=\langle a\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rangle_{r+1}=0 .
$$

Similarly, I can show that $R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a)$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ as follows:

$$
\left.\left.R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\left(a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\left\langle a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rangle_{r-1}=\left\langle a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rangle_{r-1}=0 .
$$

This result applies to the Gram-Schmidt process for producing an orthogonal set of vectors from a linearly independent set with the same span. Let $\left\{a_{j}\right\}_{j=1, \ldots, r}$ be linearly independent; then we build the orthogonal set $\left\{b_{j}\right\}_{j=1, \ldots, r}$ as follows. Let $b_{1}=a_{1}$ to start with. Then $b_{2}$ equals $a_{2}$ minus its projection onto $b_{1}$, or equivalently the orthogonal rejection of $a_{2}$ from $b_{1}$. Next, $b_{3}$ equals the orthogonal rejection of $a_{3}$ from the span of $b_{1}$ and $b_{2}$, and so on through all of the $a_{j}$. Therefore we proceed as follows.

1. Let $b_{1}=a_{1}$.
2. For each $j$ starting with 1 , let $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{j}}=b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{j}$.
3. Then let $b_{j+1}=a_{j+1} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{j}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{j}}^{-1}$.

This procedure will work only if each $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{j}}$ is invertible, which is why it is normally used only in Euclidean spaces.

If $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a blade, then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}$ represents the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. That means that orthogonal projection into $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}$ should equal orthogonal rejection from $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. Using Eqs. (181) and (183), this is easy to show directly.

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft A_{r}^{\perp}\left(A_{r}^{\perp}\right)^{-1} & =\left(a \wedge A_{r}\right)^{\perp}\left(A_{r}^{\perp}\right)^{-1} \\
& =a \wedge A_{r} I^{-1} \boldsymbol{I} \boldsymbol{A}_{r}^{-1} \\
& =a \wedge A_{r} A_{r}^{-1} .
\end{aligned}
$$

If $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ are orthogonal, then the projection of a vector into their direct sum should be the sum of the projections into the subspaces individually. (For example, the projection of a vector into the Euclidean $x y$ plane should be the sum of the projections onto the $x$ and $y$ axes separately.) This can also be shown directly. By Theorem 18, $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, so using the first of Eqs. (74) I find

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}}(a) & =P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}}(a) \\
& =a\rfloor\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)^{-1} \\
& \left.\left.=\left[(a\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right) \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}+(-1)^{r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}(a\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)\right] \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& \left.=a\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}+(-1)^{r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}(a\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Now let's work on that last term. If $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ are orthogonal, $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\left.a\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ are too, so their product is an outer product, so I can interchange them and pick up a factor of $(-1)^{r(s-1)}$. And since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1}$ are multiples of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, their product is also an outer product, so I can interchange them and pick up a factor of $(-1)^{r s}$. Putting all this in Eq. (228),

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}}(a) & \left.=a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}+(-1)^{r+r(s-1)+r s}(a\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1} \\
& =a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}+a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1} \\
& =P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a)+P_{\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}}(a) .
\end{aligned}
$$

### 7.1.2. Projecting a multivector into a subspace

Now that I can project a vector into a subspace, how about projecting one subspace into another? As I suggested above, this seems simple enough: project subspace $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ into subspace $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ by taking every vector in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, projecting it into $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, and seeing what subspace you get. The rejection should be similar: just reject all the vectors individually. However, if I am a bit more precise, I discover a wrinkle. I define

$$
\begin{aligned}
& P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}\right):=P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{s}\right) \\
& R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}\right):=R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{s}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Now suppose the set $\left\{b_{j}\right\}_{j=1, \cdots, s}$ is linearly independent but their projections are not. That would happen necessarily if, for example, I projected a plane into a line. In that case, the projection defined this way vanishes. Instead of objecting to this wrinkle, I decide that it provides useful extra information. If by chance the projections of the members of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ do not form an $s$-dimensional space, so be it; I accept that the projection is zero.

These formulas make geometric sense, but they aren't very easy to use. However, they can be made simpler, and extended to all multivectors to boot. Here's how.

Theorem 32. For any invertible blade $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and vectors $\left\{b_{j}\right\}_{j=1, \ldots, s}$,

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{s}\right) & \left.=\left(b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}\right)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{s}\right) & =\left(b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}\right) \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Proof. I start with the first equation. Since each $b_{j}=P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{j}\right)+R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{j}\right)$, the outer product $b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}$ can be written as a sum of terms, one of which equals $P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\left(b_{s}\right)$ while each of the others contains at least one $R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{j}\right)$. Consider what happens to each term when you take the inner product with $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and multiply by $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$. The term $P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{s}\right)$ lies inside $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, so by Theorem 17 the inner product becomes a product, so the $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ cancel out and you're left with $P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{s}\right)$. On the other hand, each of the other terms contains a factor orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, so the inner product with $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ vanishes. Thus the first equation is valid.

For the second equation, I again write $b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}$ as a sum of terms, but this time I note that one of them equals $R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{s}\right)$ while each of the others contains at least one $P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{j}\right)$. Consider what happens to each term when you take the outer product with $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and multiply by $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$. The term $R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{s}\right)$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, so by Theorem 18 the outer product becomes a product, so the $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ cancel out and you're left with $R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(b_{s}\right)$. On the other hand, each of the other terms contains a factor that lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, so the outer product with $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ vanishes. Thus the second equation is valid too. $\square$

Therefore $P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)=\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ and $R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)=\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ for any blade $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. Taking the obvious step, I define the orthogonal projection and rejection of any multivector to be

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(B):=B \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}=\left(B \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(B):=B \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}=B \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\left\lfloor\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} .\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

You might be surprised that both projection and rejection leave scalars untouched:

$$
P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}(\lambda)=R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}(\lambda)=\lambda .
$$

This had to happen for reasons I'll explain in Section 9.6. Projecting into and rejecting from $\boldsymbol{I}$ do what you think they should (except for that odd bit with scalars):

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{\boldsymbol{I}}(B) & =B \\
R_{\boldsymbol{I}}(B) & =\langle B\rangle .
\end{aligned}
$$

Running it the other way around, here's what happens when you project and reject $\boldsymbol{I}$ :

$$
\begin{aligned}
& P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}(\boldsymbol{I})=\boldsymbol{I} \delta_{r n} \\
& R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}(\boldsymbol{I})=\boldsymbol{I} \delta_{r 0} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Again, this makes sense; only the whole space is big enough to project $\boldsymbol{I}$ into, and only zero-dimensional spaces are small enough to reject $\boldsymbol{I}$ from.

With a little relabeling and rearranging, the first parts of Eqs. (232) become

$$
\begin{aligned}
& A\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=P_{\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}}(A) \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} \\
& A \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=R_{\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}}(A) \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}} .
\end{aligned}
$$

This shows that the inner or outer product of a multivector and a blade can also be expressed as a geometric product, as long as the blade is invertible so projection is defined. Using Theorem 21 this also shows that the norm squared of $A\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ equals the norm squared of $P_{\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}}(A)$ times the norm squared of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, and a similar result holds for the outer product.

Comparing Eqs. (222) and (223) with Eqs. (232), the level of generality achieved is astounding. Starting with the projection of one vector along another, I've shown that the projection of any multivector into a subspace is meaningful and is given by the same expression, with the multivector and blade put in place of the two vectors. The rejection of one vector from another follows the same pattern. It is true that we've lost one property: we no longer have $P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(B)+R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(B)=B$ in general. This makes geometric sense, however, if you look at the proof of Theorem 32; neither a projected blade nor a rejected blade includes all the terms that are partly projected and partly rejected, so to speak.

### 7.2. Reflections

To start, I'll review reflections from Section 1.2. I defined the reflection of vector $v$ along axis $n$ as follows: the projection of $v$ along $n$ gets a minus sign, while the rejection of $v$ from $n$ is unchanged. If the reflection is denoted $v^{\prime}$, then

$$
v^{\prime}=-n v n^{-1} .
$$

What I didn't show in Section 1.2 is that reflections preserve inner products, which I'll show now. Using Eq. (237), the definition of the inner product, and the cyclic property of the scalar part of a product,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.a^{\prime}\right\rfloor b^{\prime} & =\left\langle a^{\prime} b^{\prime}\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle n a n^{-1} n b n^{-1}\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle a b n^{-1} n\right\rangle \\
& =\langle a b\rangle \\
& =a \downharpoonleft b .
\end{aligned}
$$

### 7.2.1. Reflecting a vector in a subspace

Just as I used projection and rejection along an axis to define reflection along an axis, I can use projection and rejection in a subspace to define reflection in a subspace. The reflection of $a$ in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is constructed by giving the projection of $a$ into $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ a minus sign and leaving the rejection of $a$ from $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ alone. Using Theorem 31, Eq. (141), and Eq. (143), I find

$$
\begin{aligned}
a^{\prime} & :=-P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a)+R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a) \\
& =-a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}+a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =-(-1)^{r-1} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\left\lfloor a \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}+(-1)^{r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge a \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right. \\
& =(-1)^{r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} a \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} a^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

In the last line, $a^{* r}$ means $a$ is grade involuted $r$ times; I introduced the notation back in Section 5.1. (You may wonder why I did this instead of just leaving in the $(-1)^{r}$. It will make sense in the next section.) Another way to arrive at this formula is to write $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}$ and reflect $a$ along each of the $a_{j}$ in succession. Once again, an expression in terms of vectors generalizes to subspaces with only minimal change. Reflections in subspaces also preserve inner products; the proof is very similar to Eqs. (238).

### 7.2.2. Reflecting a multivector in a subspace

Now that I can reflect vectors, I can reflect subspaces too: the reflection of subspace $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ in subspace $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is found by taking every vector from $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, reflecting it in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$, and seeing what subspace you get. That would mean something like

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}\right)^{\prime}: & =b_{1}^{\prime} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}^{\prime} \\
& =\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} b_{1}^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} b_{s}^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

Again, this is geometrically sensible but not easy to use. Fear not; I can fix that. To start with, notice what Eq. (239) shows: conjugating a vector by an invertible $r$-blade gives you back a vector. A more general version of that is also true.

Theorem 33. If $A$ is a versor and $B_{s}$ is an $s$-vector, then

$$
A B_{s} A^{\dagger}=\left\langle A B_{s} A^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{s},
$$

so conjugation by an invertible versor is grade preserving.
Proof. The theorem is true for versors if it's true for vectors, so I'll look at $a B_{s} a$. Using Eqs. (64) and (66), I can write

$$
a B_{s} a=\left(a \downharpoonleft B_{s}\right)\left\lfloor a+(a\rfloor B_{s}\right) \wedge a+\left(a \wedge B_{s}\right)\left\lfloor a+a \wedge B_{s} \wedge a .\right.
$$

The first term is grade $s-2$, the middle two terms are grade $s$, and the last term is grade $s+2$, so I'm done if I can ditch the first and last terms. The last term vanishes because $a$ appears twice in the outer product (compare Eq. (86)), and the first term vanishes because it can be rewritten as $(-1)^{s-1}\left(B_{s}\lfloor a)\left\lfloor a=(-1)^{s-1} B_{s}\lfloor(a \wedge a)=0\right.\right.$. Since Theorem 22 tells me that the inverse of a versor, if it has one, is its reverse divided by its norm squared, conjugation by an invertible versor preserves grade too. $\square$

I'll use this to get the result I really want.
Theorem 34. If $A$ is a versor, then

$$
\left(A B A^{\dagger}\right) \wedge\left(A C A^{\dagger}\right)=|A|^{2} A(B \wedge C) A^{\dagger} .
$$

Therefore if $A$ is invertible, $\left(A B A^{-1}\right) \wedge\left(A C A^{-1}\right)=A(B \wedge C) A^{-1}$.
Proof. The result is true for general $B$ and $C$ if it's true for $B_{s}$ and $C_{t}$, and I've already shown that versor conjugation preserves grades, so

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(A B_{s} A^{\dagger}\right) \wedge\left(A C_{t} A^{\dagger}\right) & =\left\langle A B_{s} A^{\dagger} A C_{t} A^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{s+t} \\
& =|A|^{2}\left\langle A B_{s} C_{t} A^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{s+t} \\
& =|A|^{2} A\left\langle B_{s} C_{t}\right\rangle_{s+t} A^{\dagger} \\
& =|A|^{2} A\left(B_{s} \wedge C_{t}\right) A^{\dagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

If $A$ is invertible, then $|A|^{2} \neq 0$, so dividing both sides by $|A|^{4}$ yields the desired result. $\square$

Now for reflections. If $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}$, then

$$
\begin{aligned}
\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\prime} & :=b_{1}^{\prime} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}^{\prime} \\
& =\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} b_{1}^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} b_{s}^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right) \\
& =\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\left(b_{1}^{* r} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}^{* r}\right) \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\left(b_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge b_{s}\right)^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Taking the obvious next step, I define the reflection of multivector $B$ in subspace $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ to be

$$
B^{\prime}:=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} B^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} .
$$

So reflection in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is done by grade involuting $r$ times and then conjugating by $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$. This is a little more complicated than the reflection of a vector along an axis that we started with, Eq. (237), but not much. And of course it reduces to Eq. (237) when $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $B$ are vectors.

The reflection of $\boldsymbol{I}$ in a subspace is

$$
\begin{aligned}
\boldsymbol{I}^{\prime} & =\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{I}^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =(-1)^{n r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{I} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =(-1)^{n r}(-1)^{r(n-1)} \boldsymbol{I} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =(-1)^{r} \boldsymbol{I}
\end{aligned}
$$

This makes sense because $r$ directions in the space were reflected. So the orientation changes iff $r$ is odd.
You may have noticed that I now have two ways to reflect a vector around the origin. The first is grade involution, and the second is to reflect the vector in a volume element. Since both operations have been extended to the whole algebra in a way that respects products, they ought to be equal not just for vectors but for any multivector, or

$$
A^{*}=\boldsymbol{I} A^{* n} \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} .
$$

To show that this really is true, start with Eq. (178), grade involute both sides, and use $\boldsymbol{I}^{*}=(-1)^{n} \boldsymbol{I}$. Then multiply both sides by $\boldsymbol{I}^{-1}$ on the right and voilà.

Finally, I can relate reflection in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and reflection in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}$. Reflection of vector $a$ in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ gives the component in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ a minus sign and leaves the component in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}$ alone, while reflection in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}$ does the opposite. Therefore one reflection should be the negative of the other, or

$$
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp} a^{*(n-r)}\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}\right)^{-1}=-\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} a^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} .
$$

Extending this to general multivectors, I expect

$$
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp} B^{*(n-r)}\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}\right)^{-1}=\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} B^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right)^{*} .
$$

And indeed that's what I find:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp} B_{s}^{*(n-r)}\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}\right)^{-1} & =(-1)^{s(n-r)} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} B_{s}\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}\right)^{-1} \\
& =(-1)^{s(n-r)}(-1)^{s(n-1)} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} B_{s} \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} \boldsymbol{I} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =(-1)^{s}(-1)^{r s} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} B_{s} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} B_{s}^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right)^{*}
\end{aligned}
$$

### 7.3. Rotations

After all this work, rotations are fairly anticlimactic. Once again, I start with a review of Section 1.2. I showed there that a rotation in a plane is the product of two reflections along vectors in that plane, so

$$
v^{\prime}=R v R^{-1}
$$

where $R$ is the product of two invertible vectors, also called a biversor or a rotor. A rotation clearly preserves inner products since it's just two reflections in succession, but you can show it directly by an argument very much like Eqs. (238).

### 7.3.1. Rotating a multivector in a plane

The rotation of a subspace is as simple to understand as the reflection. In fact, it's the example I started this whole section with: you rotate a subspace by rotating all the vectors in it. The argument is identical to the argument for reflections: if $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}$, then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\prime}=a_{1}^{\prime} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}^{\prime}$. Therefore the rotation by $R$ is

$$
\begin{aligned}
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\prime} & :=a_{1}^{\prime} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}^{\prime} \\
& =\left(R a_{1} R^{-1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge\left(R a_{r} R^{-1}\right) \\
& =R\left(a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right) R^{-1} \\
& =R \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} R^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

The grade inversion of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is absent because it is performed twice, once for each factor in the rotor. Therefore the rule for rotating any multivector is

$$
A^{\prime}:=R A R^{-1},
$$

which is exactly the same as the formula for vectors.
Since $\boldsymbol{I}$ commutes with even multivectors (Eq. (178)), rotations leave $\boldsymbol{I}$ alone,

$$
R \boldsymbol{I} R^{-1}=\boldsymbol{I},
$$

as expected.
When I first discussed rotations in Section 1.2, I said that any two axes in the same plane separated by the same angle would generate the same rotation. That means that if I take the two vectors in $R$ and rotate them the same amount in the plane of $R$, the resulting rotor should perform the same rotation. Therefore, if $R$ and $S$ are rotors in the same plane, $S R S^{-1}$ should represent the same rotation as $R$. You can show this directly: $R$ and $S$ are both scalars plus multiples of the same area element, so they commute. Therefore $S R S^{-1}=R S S^{-1}=R$.

Every linear transformation of vectors can be extended to the entire geometric algebra; I'll describe that process later. These three transformations extend in a particularly compact way, but not all transformations do. Rotations and reflections behave as well as they do because they are orthogonal transformations, and geometric algebra is particularly well-suited to represent them. In fact, it's a good idea to pause and notice just how good a job it does; compare Eq. (254) to the increasingly complicated expressions you get when you rotate tensors of ever-increasing rank. One of the great strengths of geometric algebra is its ability to extend orthogonal transformations to the whole algebra in such a simple fashion.

### 7.3.2. Rotations in three dimensions

In a real three dimensional space, rotations have an interesting property that is easy to understand using geometric algebra: the product of two rotations is another rotation. If $R_{1}$ represents the first rotation and $R_{2}$ the second, then their product is $R=R_{2} R_{1}$. We lose no generality by demanding that both $R_{1}$ and $R_{2}$ are unit rotors; and that means $R$ is a unit even versor. In three dimensions the only even grades are zero and two, so $R$ is actually a scalar plus a bivector: $R=\langle R\rangle+\langle R\rangle_{2}$. Therefore $|R|^{2}=1$ becomes $\langle R\rangle^{2}+\left|\langle R\rangle_{2}\right|^{2}=1$. That tells me that $R=\cos (\theta / 2)-B \sin (\theta / 2)$ for some $\theta$ and unit bivector $B$. And in three dimensions every bivector is a 2-blade, so $\boldsymbol{B}$ represents some plane, and thus $R=\exp (-\boldsymbol{B} \theta / 2)$, which is a rotation through $\theta$ in plane $\boldsymbol{B}$. As soon as I climb the ladder to four dimensions, though, I lose this result, because $R_{2} R_{1}$ could have a 4-vector part.

## 8. Frames and bases

Now I'll consider a geometric algebra $\mathcal{G}^{n}$ in which the space of vectors has finite dimension $n$. Let $\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, n}$ be a basis for the vector space, which I will also call a frame. (The $a_{i}$ are not assumed orthogonal.) Then a generic element of the algebra will be the sum of a scalar and terms of the form $a_{i_{1}} a_{i_{2}} \cdots a_{i_{r}}$ for $r \leq n$. Theorem 12 tells me that any such element is a linear combination of blades made up of the $\left\{a_{i_{j}}\right\}$; therefore the scalar 1 and the blades $a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}$ generate the whole geometric algebra. I'll now show that they actually form a basis, and I'll also show how to calculate the components of an arbitrary multivector in this basis.

### 8.1. Reciprocal frames

Given a frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, n}$, another frame $\left\{a^{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, n}$ is called a reciprocal frame to the first if it satisfies

$$
a^{i} \downharpoonleft a_{j}=\delta_{j}^{i} .
$$

If such a set of vectors exists, it is a frame because Eq. (256) guarantees that the $a^{i}$ are linearly independent, so they form a basis. To construct such vectors, consider their definition: $a^{j}$ should be orthogonal to all of the $a_{i}$ except for $a_{j}$, so an obvious way to make it is to take the outer product of all of the $a_{i}$ except for $a_{j}$ and then take its dual, which is what I'll do.

Let $a_{N}=a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}$; then $a_{N}$ is a (possibly unnormalized) volume element. (Even though $a_{N}$ is a blade, I'm not denoting it with capital letters or boldface; you'll see why in the next section.) Then I define

$$
a^{i}:=(-1)^{i-1}\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}\right) a_{N}^{-1} .
$$

$\left\{a^{i}\right\}$ is a reciprocal frame because, using the first of Eqs. (181),

$$
\begin{aligned}
a_{i} \downharpoonleft a^{j} & =(-1)^{j-1} a_{i} \downharpoonleft\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{j} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n} a_{N}^{-1}\right) \\
& =(-1)^{j-1}\left(a_{i} \wedge a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{j} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}\right) a_{N}^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

Now if $i \neq j$ then $a_{i}$ equals one of the other vectors in the outer product, so the whole thing vanishes. If $i=j$, I move $a_{i}$ past the first $i-1$ vectors to its original spot, which cancels out the $(-1)^{j-1}$ prefactor. Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
a_{i} \downharpoonleft a^{j} & =\left(a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}\right) a_{N}^{-1} \delta_{i}^{j} \\
& =\delta_{i}^{j}
\end{aligned}
$$

This definition exactly expresses the geometrical idea I started with; $a_{N}$ was chosen to perform the duality transform because it gets the normalization right.

Since both $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ and $\left\{a^{j}\right\}$ are bases for the vectors, any vector $v$ can be written $v=\sum v^{i} a_{i}$ or $v=\sum v_{j} a^{j}$. In fact, it's obvious that $\left.v^{i}=v\right\lrcorner a^{i}$ and $\left.v_{j}=v\right\lrcorner a_{j}$, so the components of $v$ on either basis are easily calculated using the other basis. Using the definition of $a^{i}$ and the first of Eqs. (181) again, I find that

$$
\begin{aligned}
v^{i} & =v \downharpoonleft a^{i} \\
& \left.=(-1)^{i-1} v\right\rfloor\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n} a_{N}^{-1}\right) \\
& =(-1)^{i-1}\left(v \wedge a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}\right) a_{N}^{-1} \\
& =\left(a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i-1} \wedge v \wedge a_{i+1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}\right) a_{N}^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

Compare this with Eqs. (16) and (17) back in Section 1.2
Since $v$ is a vector, the expressions for its components can be written $v^{i}=v * a^{i}$ and $v_{j}=v * a_{j}$, where * is the scalar product. These forms for the components can be generalized a long way, as I'll show in the next section.

The inner product of any two vectors follows easily from their components:

$$
b \downharpoonleft c=\sum_{i, j} b_{i} c^{j}\left(a^{i} \downharpoonleft a_{j}\right)=\sum_{i} b_{i} c^{i},
$$

and switching the frames on which I expand $b$ and $c$ gives me an equally valid result in terms of the components $b^{i}$ and $c_{i}$.

A frame and its reciprocal satisfy a useful identity.
Theorem 35.

$$
\sum_{i} a_{i} a^{i}=\sum_{i} a^{i} a_{i}=n .
$$

Proof.

$$
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{i} a_{i} a^{i} & =\sum_{i} a_{i} \downharpoonleft a^{i}+\sum_{i} a_{i} \wedge a^{i} \\
& =n+\sum_{i} a_{i} \wedge a^{i}
\end{aligned}
$$

To evaluate the second term, expand $a^{i}$ on the original frame to get $a^{i}=\sum_{j}\left(a^{i} \downharpoonleft a^{j}\right) a_{j}$, so

$$
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{i} a_{i} \wedge a^{i} & =\sum_{i} a_{i} \wedge\left(\sum_{j} a^{i} \downharpoonleft a^{j} a_{j}\right) \\
& \left.=\sum_{i, j}\left(a_{i} \wedge a_{j}\right)\left(a^{i}\right\rfloor a^{j}\right) \\
& =0
\end{aligned}
$$

because $a^{i} \downharpoonleft a^{j}$ is symmetric in $i$ and $j$ while $a_{i} \wedge a_{j}$ is antisymmetric. The proof that $\sum_{i} a^{i} a_{i}=n$ is the same except for exchanging superscripts and subscripts. $\square$

### 8.2. Multivector bases

Before I continue, I need some fancy new notation. Let $I$ be a string of indices $i_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots, i_{r}$, and given a string $I$ let $a_{I}$ be defined by

$$
a_{I}:=a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}},
$$

and similarly for $a^{I}$. I will use the symbol $N$ only to refer to the string $1,2, \ldots, n$, to be consistent with $a_{N}$ in the previous section. I also allow $I$ to be the "empty" sequence, in which case I define $a_{I}=a^{I}=1$. Then I immediately know several things:

1. $a_{I}=a^{I}=0$ iff the string $I$ contains at least one index twice.
2. If $I$ and $J$ contain the same elements but in a different order, then $a_{I}=(\operatorname{sgn} \sigma) a_{J}$ and $a^{I}=(\operatorname{sgn} \sigma) a^{J}$, where $\sigma$ is the permutation that changes $I$ to $J$.
3. Theorem 12 tells me that given a frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ for the vectors, the set $\left\{a_{I}\right\}$ (or $\left\{a^{I}\right\}$ ) where $I$ ranges over all increasing sequences generates $\mathcal{G}^{n}$. ( $I$ is an increasing sequence if $i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{r}$.)

To show either set forms a basis, I'll use this result.
Theorem 36.

$$
a^{I} * a_{J}=\delta_{J}^{I}
$$

where $\delta_{J}^{I}$ vanishes if either $I$ or $J$ repeats indices or if $I$ is not a permutation of $J$ (including having a different length), and otherwise it equals the sign of the permutation that takes $I$ to $J$.

Proof. If either string repeats indices then both sides vanish, and both sides also vanish when the lengths of $I$ and $J$ are different (the right side by definition, the left side because $a^{I}$ and $a_{J}$ have different grades); to get the other results, I let $I=i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{r}$ and $J=j_{1}<j_{2}<\cdots<j_{r}$ and use Eq. (156) to find

$$
\begin{aligned}
a^{I} * a_{J} & =\left\langle\left(a^{i_{1}} \wedge a^{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{r}}\right)^{\dagger}\left(a_{j_{1}} \wedge a_{j_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{j_{r}}\right)\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}} \wedge a^{i_{1}}\right)\left(a_{j_{1}} \wedge a_{j_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{j_{r}}\right)\right\rangle \\
& \left.=\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}} \wedge a^{i_{1}}\right)\right\rfloor\left(a_{j_{1}} \wedge a_{j_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{j_{r}}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Now consider the case where the $i_{k}$ equal the $j_{k}$; using the third of Eqs. (83) and Eq. (77),

$$
\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}} \wedge a^{i_{1}}\right) *\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right)
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.\left.=\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}}\right)\right\rfloor\left[a^{i_{1}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}}\right)\right\rfloor\left[\sum_{j=1}^{r}(-1)^{j-1} a^{i_{1}} \downharpoonleft a_{i_{j}} a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i_{j}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right] \\
& \left.=\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}}\right)\right\rfloor\left(a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

which can be repeated for the $i_{2}$ term and for each successive term until the final result

$$
\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}} \wedge a^{i_{1}}\right) *\left(a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right)=1
$$

is reached. Now suppose that $i_{k}$ equals none of the $j_{l}$; then when the evaluation of the scalar product as shown above reaches the $k$ th iteration, all of the $\left.a^{i_{k}}\right\rfloor a_{j_{l}}$ terms will vanish, and so will the scalar product. This establishes the result for $I$ and $J$ increasing; the general result follows from the properties of $a^{I}$ and $a_{J}$ under rearrangement of elements. $\square$

From this result it's pretty obvious that for any multivector $A$,

$$
A=\sum_{I} A^{I} a_{I} \quad \text { where } \quad A^{I}=A * a^{I}
$$

and the sum extends over all increasing sequences $I$ (including the null sequence to pick up the scalar part). Therefore, given a frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$, the elements $a_{I}$ form a true basis for the geometric algebra, and the equation above shows how to expand any multivector on this basis. (Incidentally, the roles of the frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ and the reciprocal frame $\left\{a^{j}\right\}$ can be exchanged in this expansion, just as vectors can be expanded on either set with the other used to compute the coefficients.) Since the number of distinct $r$-blades in a basis for each $r$ is $\binom{n}{r}$, it follows that

$$
\operatorname{dim} \mathcal{G}^{n}=\sum_{r=0}^{n} \operatorname{dim} \mathcal{G}_{r}^{n}=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom{n}{r}=2^{n} .
$$

I can also express the scalar product of any two multivectors $B$ and $C$ in terms of their components:

$$
B * C=\sum_{I, J} B_{I} C^{J}\left(a^{I} * a_{J}\right)=\sum_{I} B_{I} C^{I},
$$

and switching the bases on which I expand $B$ and $C$ gives me an equally valid result in terms of the components $B^{I}$ and $C_{I}$.

A consequence of all this is the following theorem.
Theorem 37. Multivector $A$ is uniquely determined by either of the following.

1. $A * B$ for every multivector $B$.
2. $\langle A\rangle$ and $a\rfloor A$ for every vector $a$.

Proof. Part 1 is obvious. In fact, it's overkill; $A * B$ for all $B$ in a basis for the algebra will do. By the distributive property, part 2 is equivalent to this statement: if $\langle A\rangle=0$ and all $a \downharpoonleft A=0$, then $A=0$. So that's what I'll prove. To do this, I assume $A$ is an $r$-vector $A_{r}$; if the result is true for $r$-vectors then it's true for general multivectors too. If $r=0$ or 1 then I'm done, so let $r>1$. Let $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ be a frame and $\left\{a^{i}\right\}$ its reciprocal frame; then a component of $A_{r}$ on the basis defined by $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ is $\left.\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}} \wedge a^{i_{1}}\right)\right\rfloor A_{r}$ for some strictly ascending choice of $i_{1}$ through $i_{r}$. However,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}} \wedge a^{i_{1}}\right)\right\rfloor A_{r} & \left.=\left(a^{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{2}}\right)\right\rfloor\left(a^{i_{1}} \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right) \\
& =0
\end{aligned}
$$

since $a^{i_{1}} \downharpoonleft A_{r}=0$. So all the components of $A_{r}$ vanish, so $A_{r}=0$.

This proves the theorem in every finite-dimensional algebra, but it's usually true in infinitedimensional spaces too. In fact, extra structures are usually imposed on infinite-dimensional spaces for exactly this purpose, and I will happily assume henceforth that this has always been done. $\square$

This result can be extended ad nauseum: $A$ is uniquely determined by $\langle A\rangle,\langle A\rangle_{1}$, and $A_{2} \downharpoonleft A$ for every bivector $A_{2}$, and so on.

While any $r$-vector can be expanded using the frame $r$-vectors, it can also be expanded using only the frame vectors; but now the coefficients aren't necessarily scalars.

Theorem 38.

$$
\sum_{i} a^{i} a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r}=\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right)=r A_{r}
$$

and the same is true if the frame and its reciprocal are interchanged.
Proof. For the first equality, note that

$$
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{i} a^{i} a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r} & \left.=\sum_{i} a^{i}\right\rfloor\left(a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right)+\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right) \\
& \left.=\sum_{i}\left(a^{i} \wedge a_{i}\right)\right\rfloor A_{r}+\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right) \\
& \left.=\left(\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge a_{i}\right)\right\rfloor A_{r}+\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right) \\
& \left.=\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i}\right\rfloor A_{r}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

because I showed in the proof of Theorem 35 that $\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge a_{i}=0$.
The second equality is true for all $r$-vectors if it's true for all members of a basis for $r$-vectors, so I have to prove it only on a basis; and I know just the basis to use. Let $A_{r}=a^{j_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{j_{r}}$ for some increasing sequence of indices; then

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i}\right\rfloor A_{r}\right) & \left.=\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left[a_{i}\right\rfloor\left(a^{j_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{j_{r}}\right)\right] \\
& =\sum_{i, k}(-1)^{k-1}\left(a_{i} \downharpoonleft a^{j_{k}}\right) a^{i} \wedge a^{j_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}^{j_{k}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{j_{r}} \\
& =\sum_{k}(-1)^{k-1} a^{j_{k}} \wedge a^{j_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}^{j_{k}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{j_{r}} .
\end{aligned}
$$

In each term of the sum, I move $a^{j_{k}}$ past $k-1$ other vectors to return it to its original spot, which cancels the $(-1)^{k-1}$ factor, so

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i}\right\rfloor A_{r}\right) & =\sum_{k} a^{j_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{j_{r}} \\
& =r A_{r}
\end{aligned}
$$

That completes the first half of the proof, and exchanging superscripts and subscripts provides the other half. $\square$

Just as the original frame has a volume element $a_{N}$, the reciprocal frame has a volume element $a^{N}$ defined in an analogous way: $a^{N}=a^{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{n}$. Now $a_{N}$ and $a^{N}$ have to be scalar multiples of each other, and since Theorem 36 shows that $a_{N} * a^{N}=1$, I conclude that

$$
a^{N}=\frac{a_{N}}{\left|a_{N}\right|^{2}} .
$$

A quick calculation shows

$$
\left|a^{N}\right|^{2}=\left|a_{N}\right|^{-2},
$$

so for real algebras, the weights of the volume elements of a frame and its reciprocal are themselves reciprocals.
Given a frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$, I have Eq. (257) for the members $a^{i}$ of the reciprocal frame, but I don't have an equally direct formula for the reciprocal multivectors $a^{I}$; all I can do right now is take the outer product of Eq. (257) several times. However, I can get a nicer formula for $a^{I}$ using the same logic that got me the reciprocal vectors in the first place. By construction, $a^{i}$ is orthogonal to the outer product of all the frame vectors except $a_{i}$, and similarly for $a^{j}$; therefore $a^{i} \wedge a^{j}$ is orthogonal to the outer product of all the frame vectors except $a_{i}$ and $a_{j}$. Therefore $a^{i} \wedge a^{j}$ is dual to $a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{j} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}$. To make this easier to write out, for any string of indices $I$ let me define $I^{c}$ to be its ascending complement, so $I^{c}$ includes exactly the indices not in $I$ in ascending order. In these terms, $a^{I}$ is dual to $a_{I^{c}}$. To be more precise, I have

Theorem 39. If I represents ascending $i_{1}$ through $i_{r}$,

$$
a^{I}=(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(i_{j}-1\right)} a_{I^{c}} a_{N}^{-1} .
$$

Notice that this includes Eq. (257) as a special case when $I$ has only one index $i$.
Proof. To prove this, I'll calculate $a^{I} a_{N}$.

$$
\begin{aligned}
a^{I} a_{N} & =a^{I} \downharpoonleft a_{N} \\
& \left.=\left(a^{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{r}}\right)\right\rfloor a_{N} \\
& \left.=\left(a^{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{r-1}}\right)\right\rfloor\left(a^{i_{r}} \downharpoonleft a_{N}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

To calculate $\left.a^{i_{r}}\right\rfloor a_{N}$ I use Eq. (77):

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.a^{i_{r}}\right\rfloor a_{N} & \left.=a^{i_{r}}\right\rfloor\left(a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}\right) \\
& \left.=\sum_{j}(-1)^{j-1}\left(a^{i_{r}}\right\rfloor a_{j}\right) a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{j} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n} \\
& =(-1)^{i_{r}-1} a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i_{r}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n} \\
& =(-1)^{i_{r}-1} a_{i_{r}^{c}} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Now Eq. (281) becomes

$$
\begin{aligned}
a^{I} a_{N} & \left.=(-1)^{i_{r}-1}\left(a^{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{r-1}}\right)\right\rfloor a_{i_{r}^{c}} \\
& \left.=(-1)^{i_{r}-1}\left(a^{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{r-2}}\right)\right\rfloor\left(a^{i_{r-1}} \downharpoonleft a_{i_{r}^{c}}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

When I evaluate $a^{i_{r-1}} \downharpoonleft a_{i_{r}^{c}}$ using Eq. (77) again, I remove $a_{i_{r-1}}$ and multiply by $(-1)^{i_{r-1}-1}$. (This is why I put the indices of $I$ in ascending order; $i_{r}$ is later than $i_{r-1}$, so $a_{i_{r-1}}$ is still in position $i_{r-1}$ in $a_{i_{r}^{c}}$.) Thus each step removes a factor $a_{i_{j}}$ from $a_{N}$ and multiplies by $(-1)^{i_{j}-1}$, with the final result

$$
a^{I} a_{N}=(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(i_{j}-1\right)} a_{I^{c}} .
$$

Now I'll take care of the special cases on each extreme: $I$ is empty and $I=N$. When $I$ is empty, Eq. (280) reduces to $a^{I}=a_{N} a_{N}^{-1}=1$, which is correct, and when $I=N, I^{c}$ is empty so $a_{I^{c}}=1$, so Eq. (280) becomes

$$
\begin{aligned}
a^{N} & =(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{n}(j-1)} a_{N}^{-1} \\
& =(-1)^{n(n-1) / 2} \frac{a_{N}}{a_{N}{ }^{2}} \\
& =\frac{a_{N}}{\left|a_{N}\right|^{2}},
\end{aligned}
$$

where I used Eq. (171) in the last step. Since this matches Eq. (278), it too is correct and the theorem is proved. $\square$

To wrap up this part, let me consider the special case where $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ is an orthonormal frame, which I'll denote $\left\{e_{i}\right\}$. Then the reciprocal frame is a lot easier to find: it's clear on inspection that $e^{i}=e_{i}^{-1}=e_{i}^{-2} e_{i}$ fits the bill. Since the frame vectors are normalized, $e_{i}^{2}= \pm 1$, so $e^{i}= \pm e_{i}$. For any $I$, let $I_{m}$ be the number of elements in the product for $e_{I}$ that have negative square; then $e^{I}=(-1)^{I_{m}} e_{I}$, so the multivector basis and its reciprocal differ at most by signs. On such a basis, Eq. (272) for the scalar product becomes

$$
B * C=\sum_{I}(-1)^{I_{m}} B_{I} C_{I}=\sum_{I}(-1)^{I_{m}} B^{I} C^{I} .
$$

If the space of vectors is Euclidean, then $I_{m}=0$ for any $I$, so the magnitude is positive definite, so the entire geometric algebra is also a Euclidean space under the scalar product. If the space of vectors is non-Euclidean, then the algebra has (very) mixed signature under the scalar product.

### 8.3. Orthogonal projections using frames

In traditional vector algebra, the orthogonal projection of a vector into a subspace is given as a sum of projections onto a basis for the subspace. Although we don't need to do that in geometric algebra, we still can. Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}$; then

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(a) & =a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =a \downharpoonleft\left(a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right) \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =\sum_{j=1}^{r}(-1)^{j-1} a \downharpoonleft a_{j}\left(a_{i} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{j} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right) \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

Now $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a volume element for its subspace, so comparing with Eq. (257) shows me that the vectors in the sum above are the reciprocal frame to $\left\{a_{j}\right\}$, or

$$
P_{a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}}(a)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(a \downharpoonleft a_{j}\right) a^{j} .
$$

Since the reciprocal frame volume element $\boldsymbol{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}}=a^{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{r}$ equals $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} /\left|\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right|^{2}$ (cf. Eq. (285)), projection using either volume element gives the same result; had I used $\boldsymbol{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}}$, I'd have ended up with Eq. (288) with $a_{j}$ and $a^{j}$ interchanged.

I can do the same thing with any $s$-vector $B_{s}$. Let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ be defined as before; then using Eq. (95) from Theorem 14 I find that

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(B_{s}\right) & =B_{s} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =B_{s} \downharpoonleft\left(a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right) \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =\sum(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\left(i_{j}-j\right)}\left(B_{s} \downharpoonleft a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{s}}\right)\left(a_{i_{s+1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =(-1)^{s(s-1) / 2} \sum(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\left(i_{j}-1\right)}\left(B_{s} \downharpoonleft a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{s}}\right)\left(a_{i_{s+1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& \left.=\sum(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\left(i_{j}-1\right)}\left(B_{s}^{\dagger}\right\rfloor a_{i_{1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{s}}\right)\left(a_{i_{s+1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1},
\end{aligned}
$$

where in the next to last line I used Eq. (102). If I now let $I$ be the sequence $i_{1}$ through $i_{s}$ and use Eq. (280) for the reciprocal multivector basis, I find

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\left(B_{s}\right) & \left.=\sum_{I}\left(B_{s}^{\dagger}\right\rfloor a_{I}\right)(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\left(i_{j}-1\right)} a_{I^{c}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& =\sum_{I}\left(B_{s} * a_{I}\right) a^{I} .
\end{aligned}
$$

This expression is still true if I let the sum run over increasing sequences of any length, since all the additional terms vanish. Therefore for any multivector $B$

$$
P_{a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}}(B)=\sum_{I}\left(B * a_{I}\right) a^{I},
$$

where the sum runs over all increasing sequences, and the expression is still true if the bases $\left\{a_{I}\right\}$ and $\left\{a^{I}\right\}$ are interchanged.

## 9. Linear algebra

Now that I've said so much about linear spaces, let's take the next step and put some linear functions on them. If $U$ and $V$ are vector spaces with the same set of scalars, a function $F: U \rightarrow V$ is said to be linear if $F(\alpha u+\beta v)=\alpha F(u)+\beta F(v)$, so linear functions respect linear combinations. Linear functions have a very well-developed theory, and they're important all over applied mathematics; in fact, when a function isn't linear, one of the first things we do is consider its local linear approximation, the derivative.

In this section I'll hit the highlights of linear algebra using the tools and perspective of geometric algebra. I'll start by reviewing some basic properties of linear functions, and then I'll introduce the adjoint. I'll use it to describe three special types of functions: symmetric, skew symmetric, and orthogonal, each of which relates to its adjoint in a certain way. All three have special forms in geometric algebra, which I'll consider in detail. After that, I'll take a giant step into geometric algebra proper by showing how to take a linear function on vectors and extend it in a very natural way to every multivector in the whole algebra. This is where geometric algebra really starts to shine, because it lets me see old topics in new and useful ways. For example, our old friend the eigenvector will be joined by eigenplanes, eigenvolumes, and more, and I'll show how to use them to describe linear functions.
