(It's immediately clear that a rotation has an eigenplane with eigenvalue 1, for example.) I'll also give a very easy and intuitive definition of the determinant, and I'll show how easy determinants are to calculate in geometric algebra.

I'm going to focus on functions that take vectors to vectors, and their extensions to the whole algebra will be grade-preserving. To some of you that might seem rather timid; since a geometric algebra is itself a (big) vector space, why not just jump in with both feet and go right for linear functions from multivectors to multivectors, whether they preserve grade or not? Well, of course you can, and we already have; duality does that, for example. General tensors will also do that, and I'll consider them in due course.

### 9.1. Preliminaries

If $F$ and $G$ are linear, then so are their linear combinations, and so are their inverses if they exist. If $F: U \rightarrow V$, then $U$ and $V$ are called the domain and codomain of $F$ respectively. (Some authors call the codomain the range.) $F$ singles out two special subspaces: the kernel of $F$, or $\operatorname{Ker}(F)$, is a subspace of the domain consisting of all the vectors that $F$ maps to 0 , and the $\operatorname{range}$ of $F$, or $\operatorname{Range}(F)$, is a subspace of the codomain containing all the vectors that $F$ maps something to. (The range is sometimes called the image, presumably by the same folks who've already used the word range to mean the codomain.) It's suggestive to think of $\operatorname{Ker}(F)$ as $F^{-1}(0)$ and $\operatorname{Range}(F)$ as $F(U) . F$ is one-to-one iff $\operatorname{Ker}(F)=\{0\}$, and $F$ is onto iff $\operatorname{Range}(F)=V$. The dimension of $\operatorname{Ker}(F)$ is called the nullity of $F$, or $\operatorname{null}(F)$, and the dimension of $\operatorname{Range}(F)$ is called the $\operatorname{rank}$ of $F$, or $\operatorname{rank}(F)$. If the dimension of the domain $U$ is finite, the rank-nullity theorem says

$$
\operatorname{rank}(F)+\operatorname{null}(F)=\operatorname{dim} U .
$$

So if both domain and codomain have the same finite dimension, $F$ is one-to-one iff it's also onto. Therefore to show invertibility, you only have to show either one-to-one or onto, and the other part follows automatically. If $W$ is a subspace of the domain of $F$, then the restriction of $F$ to $W$ is well-defined and also linear; it's denoted $F_{W}$. Since blades represent subspaces, I'll sometimes write $F_{\boldsymbol{A}}$ for the restriction of $F$ to the subspace $\boldsymbol{A}$ represents.

Often we care specifically about linear functions from $U$ to itself, which I'll call linear transformations or linear operators. A pretty popular linear operator on any space is the identity; I denote the identity on $U$ by $\operatorname{Id}_{U}$.

Since our subject is geometric algebra, I will assume that all vector spaces have inner products and belong to geometric algebras. With that, recall that Theorem 37 in Section 8 shows that any multivector is determined uniquely by its scalar products with all multivectors. Looking only at vectors, that means $u$ is uniquely fixed if one knows $u * v$ (or equivalently $u \downharpoonleft v$ ) for all $v$. This has two useful consequences. The first lets me reconstruct linear functions.

Theorem 40. A linear function $F: U \rightarrow V$ is completely determined by knowledge of $F(u) * v$ for all $u \in U$ and $v \in V$.

Proof. In the finite-dimensional case, $F$ can be constructed explicitly: $F(u)=\sum_{i}\left[a^{i} * F(u)\right] a_{i}$, where $\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, n}$ is any frame in $V$ and $\left\{a^{j}\right\}_{j=1, \ldots, n}$ is its reciprocal frame. Since we know all the $a^{i} * F(u)$, we know $F(u)$. We can use any frame for this construction since the set of all inner products determines $F(u)$ uniquely for each $u$ by Theorem 37.

In the infinite-dimensional case, it's not obvious we can perform this construction, but for all applications I know of, the space is rigged in some way to allow something like this to be done. So I'll assume I can do it. $\square$

Any linear transformation $F$ defines a new bilinear product on vectors by $a \downharpoonleft F(b)$. The second consequence of Theorem 37 lets me go the other way: I start with the product and define $F$.

Theorem 41. If ○ is a bilinear function from vectors to scalars, there's unique a linear transformation F such that $u \circ v=u \downharpoonleft F(v)$ for all $u$ and $v$.

Proof. Again, in finite dimensions the proof is by construction: Let $F(u)=\sum_{i}\left(a^{i} \circ u\right) a_{i}$. This defines a linear function because the product ∘ is bilinear, and it satisfies

$$
\begin{aligned}
u\rfloor F(v) & =u \downharpoonleft\left(\sum_{i}\left(a^{i} \circ v\right) a_{i}\right) \\
& =\sum_{i}\left(a^{i} \circ v\right)\left(u \downharpoonleft a_{i}\right) \\
& \left.=\left(\sum_{i}(u\rfloor a_{i}\right) a^{i}\right) \circ v \\
& =u \circ v .
\end{aligned}
$$

Since $F$ is determined by $u \downharpoonleft F(v)=u \circ v$ for all $u$ and $v$, this function is unique.
In the infinite-dimensional case, I will assume that whatever structure is needed to make this result true has been added. $\square$

This is useful, because any given vector space can support many different inner products, but only one inner product at a time can be encoded into a geometric algebra. This theorem tells me I have a way to use the other products if I decide I need to. Also, there are bilinear products that I want to use that can't be inner products because they aren't symmetric. This theorem lets me include those products too, although of course the corresponding function $F$ will have different properties. What those properties are will be the subject of future sections.

### 9.2. The adjoint

If $F: U \rightarrow V$ is linear, then its adjoint is the unique linear function $\bar{F}: V \rightarrow U$ defined by

$$
\bar{F}(v) * u:=v * F(u) \quad \text { for all } u \in U \text { and } v \in V .
$$

Notice that $\bar{F}$ switches domain and codomain compared to $F$ and that $*$ is interchangeable with either 」 or $\left\lfloor\right.$ in this definition. The adjoint of the identity is pretty easy: $\overline{\mathrm{Id}_{U}}=\mathrm{Id}_{U}$. Theorem 40 tells me how to construct $\bar{F}$ explicitly in the finite-dimensional case:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\bar{F}(v) & =\sum_{i}\left[a^{i} * \bar{F}(v)\right] a_{i} \\
& =\sum_{i}\left[F\left(a^{i}\right) * v\right] a_{i} .
\end{aligned}
$$

(Notice that for this to make sense, $v \in V$ while the frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\} \subset U$.) The bilinearity of the inner product shows that taking the adjoint is itself a linear operation:

$$
\overline{\alpha F+\beta G}=\alpha \bar{F}+\beta \bar{G} .
$$

The relationship between $F$ and $\bar{F}$ is symmetric, so each is the adjoint of the other, or equivalently

$$
\overline{\bar{F}}=F .
$$

Suppose $F: U \rightarrow V$ and $G: V \rightarrow W$, and let $G F: U \rightarrow W$ denote the composition of $F$ and $G$. Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
w * G F(u) & =\bar{G}(w) * F(u) \\
& =\bar{F} \bar{G}(w) * u,
\end{aligned}
$$

which tells me that

$$
\overline{G F}=\bar{F} \bar{G} .
$$

A special case of this arises if $F$ is a operator on $U$, in which case $F^{n}$ is defined for any $n$ and

$$
\overline{F^{n}}=(\bar{F})^{n} .
$$

Now suppose $F: U \rightarrow V$ is invertible, so there's an $F^{-1}: V \rightarrow U$ such that $F^{-1} F=\operatorname{Id}_{U}$ and $F F^{-1}=\operatorname{Id}_{V}$. In that case, since $\bar{F} \overline{F^{-1}}=\overline{F^{-1} F}=\overline{\mathrm{Id}_{U}}=\mathrm{Id}_{U}$, and similarly $\overline{F^{-1}} \bar{F}=\mathrm{Id}_{V}$,

$$
(\bar{F})^{-1}=\overline{F^{-1}} .
$$

The special subspaces defined by a linear function and its adjoint are related in interesting ways.
Theorem 42. If $F: U \rightarrow V$ is linear,

$$
\operatorname{Ker}(F)=\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})^{\perp} .
$$

If in addition $U$ is finite-dimensional,

$$
\operatorname{rank}(F)=\operatorname{rank}(\bar{F}) .
$$

Proof. For the first part,

$$
\begin{array}{lll}
u \in \operatorname{Ker}(F) & \text { iff } & F(u)=0 \\
& \text { iff } & F(u)\rfloor v=0 \quad \text { for all } v \in V \\
& \text { iff } & u \downharpoonleft \bar{F}(v)=0 \quad \text { for all } v \in V \\
& \text { iff } & u \in \operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})^{\perp} .
\end{array}
$$

For the second part, we start with the rank-nullity theorem and the result of the first part:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{rank}(F) & =\operatorname{dim} U-\operatorname{null}(F) \\
& =\operatorname{dim} U-\operatorname{dim} \operatorname{Ker}(F) \\
& =\operatorname{dim} U-\operatorname{dim} \operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})^{\perp}
\end{aligned}
$$

Now $\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})$ and $\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})^{\perp}$ are duals, so their dimensions add up to the dimension of $U$; so picking up where I left off,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{rank}(F) & =\operatorname{dim} U-\operatorname{dim} \operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})^{\perp} \\
& =\operatorname{dim} \operatorname{Range}(\bar{F}) \\
& =\operatorname{rank}(\bar{F})
\end{aligned}
$$ $\square$

### 9.3. Normal operators

In the next few sections I'll be considering operators that commute with their adjoints: $F \bar{F}=\bar{F} F$. These are called normal operators, and they have properties that I'll describe here so I can use them later.

Theorem 43. Any power of a normal operator is also normal.
Proof. If $F$ is normal, then $F^{n}(\bar{F})^{n}$ is easily transformed to $(\bar{F})^{n} F^{n}$ by moving all the $\bar{F}$ factors past all the factors of $F$. $\square$

Theorem 44. $F$ is normal iff $F(u)\rfloor F(v)=\bar{F}(u)\rfloor \bar{F}(v)$ for any $u$ and $v$.
Proof. First assume $F$ is normal. Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
F(u)\rfloor F(v) & =\bar{F} F(u)\rfloor v \\
& =F \bar{F}(u) \downharpoonleft v \\
& =\bar{F}(u)\rfloor \bar{F}(v) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Now assume the relation holds. Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
F \bar{F}(u)\rfloor v & =\bar{F}(u)\rfloor \bar{F}(v) \\
& =F(u)\rfloor F(v) \\
& =\bar{F} F(u)\rfloor v,
\end{aligned}
$$

so by Theorem $40 F \bar{F}=\bar{F} F$ and $F$ is normal. $\square$

Theorem 45. $F$ is normal iff $F(u)^{2}=\bar{F}(u)^{2}$ for all $u$.
Proof. Since

$$
F(u)\rfloor F(v)=\frac{1}{2}\left[F(u+v)^{2}-F(u)^{2}-F(v)^{2}\right],
$$

$F(u)^{2}=\bar{F}(u)^{2}$ for all $u$ implies $\left.\left.F(u)\right\rfloor F(v)=\bar{F}(u)\right\rfloor \bar{F}(v)$ for all $u$ and $v$. On the other hand, $F(u)\rfloor F(v)=\bar{F}(u) \downharpoonleft \bar{F}(v)$ for all $u$ and $v$ implies $F(u)^{2}=\bar{F}(u)^{2}$ for all $u$ just by considering the case $u=v$. So squares are equal iff inner products are equal, which takes us back to the previous theorem. $\square$

Theorem 46. If $F$ is normal and the inner product is nondegenerate on both $\operatorname{Range}(F)$ and $\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})$, then $\operatorname{Ker}(F)=\operatorname{Ker}(\bar{F})$. If in addition the domain of $F$ is finite-dimensional, $\operatorname{Range}(F)=\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})$.

Proof. For the first part,

$$
\begin{aligned}
u \in \operatorname{Ker}(F) & \Longrightarrow F(u)=0 \\
& \Longrightarrow \bar{F} F(u)=0 \\
& \Longrightarrow F \bar{F}(u)=0 \\
& \Longrightarrow \bar{F}(u) \in \operatorname{Ker}(F) .
\end{aligned}
$$

By Theorem 42, $\operatorname{Ker}(F)=\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})^{\perp}$, so $\bar{F}(u) \in \operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})^{\perp}$. But wait a second: $\bar{F}(u) \in$ $\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})$ by definition, and $\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})$ is nondegenerate, so it must be that $\bar{F}(u)=0$, so $u \in$ $\operatorname{Ker}(\bar{F})$. Therefore $\operatorname{Ker}(F) \subset \operatorname{Ker}(\bar{F})$. The same argument with $F$ and $\bar{F}$ interchanged shows $\operatorname{Ker}(\bar{F}) \subset \operatorname{Ker}(F)$, so $\operatorname{Ker}(F)=\operatorname{Ker}(\bar{F})$.

For the second part, since the domain of $F$ is finite-dimensional any subspace and its orthogonal complement are duals, so each is the orthogonal complement of the other. That and Theorem 42 tell me that $\operatorname{Range}(F)=\operatorname{Ker}(\bar{F})^{\perp}$ and $\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})=\operatorname{Ker}(F)^{\perp}$. But I just showed that $\operatorname{Ker}(F)=\operatorname{Ker}(\bar{F})$, so $\operatorname{Range}(F)=\operatorname{Range}(\bar{F})$ too. $\square$

So if the conditions of this theorem are satisfied, normal $F$ and $\bar{F}$ are both one-to-one (or onto) or neither one is.

### 9.4. Symmetric and skew symmetric operators

A linear operator is symmetric if it equals its adjoint, $\bar{F}=F$, and skew symmetric or skew if it is the negative of its adjoint, $\bar{F}=-F$. The names come from this theorem.

Theorem 47. The bilinear product $a \circ b:=a \downharpoonleft F(b)$ is (anti)symmetric iff $F$ is (skew) symmetric.
Proof. Since $a \downharpoonleft F(b)=b \downharpoonleft \bar{F}(a)$, it follows that $a \circ b=b \circ a$ iff $\bar{F}=F$ and $a \circ b=-b \circ a$ iff $\bar{F}=-F$. $\square$

Recall that every bilinear product has this form for some $F$ (Theorem 41).
Both types of operator are normal, so all the results of Section 9.3 apply to them. (They're all pretty trivial in these cases, I have to admit.) Further, every linear operator is the sum of a symmetric and a skew symmetric operator, because

$$
F=\frac{1}{2}(F+\bar{F})+\frac{1}{2}(F-\bar{F}) .
$$

Also, for any linear operator $F$, both $\bar{F} F$ and $F \bar{F}$ are symmetric.
Powers of symmetric and skew symmetric operators are themselves symmetric or skew symmetric.
Theorem 48. Any power of a symmetric operator is symmetric. Any even power of a skew symmetric operator is symmetric, and any odd power is skew symmetric.

Proof. Since $\overline{F^{n}}=(\bar{F})^{n}, \bar{F}=F$ implies $\overline{F^{n}}=F^{n}$, so $F^{n}$ is symmetric also, and $\bar{F}=-F$ implies $\overline{F^{n}}=(-1)^{n} F^{n}$, so $F^{n}$ is symmetric or skew as $n$ is even or odd. $\square$

The spectral theorem says that every symmetric $F$ has a frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ of eigenvectors with eigenvalues $\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}$, which means $F(a)$ for any $a$ is given by

$$
F(a)=\sum_{i} \lambda_{i}\left(a * a^{i}\right) a_{i} .
$$

Conversely, every $F$ of this form is symmetric. Analogous results hold on infinite-dimensional spaces with various additional restrictions.

Skew symmetric operators also have a canonical form, which is expressed very nicely in geometric algebra. As motivation, notice that if $F$ is skew, then $a \downharpoonleft F(a)=0$, so $F$ maps any vector to an orthogonal vector. Well, I know something else that does that: taking the dual by a bivector. In fact, the function $F(a)=a \downharpoonleft A_{2}$ for any bivector $A_{2}$ is skew, because the resulting bilinear product is antisymmetric:

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \circ b & \left.=a\rfloor(b\rfloor A_{2}\right) \\
& =(a \wedge b)\rfloor A_{2} \\
& =-(b \wedge a)\rfloor A_{2} \\
& \left.=-b \downharpoonleft(a\rfloor A_{2}\right) \\
& =-b \circ a .
\end{aligned}
$$

It turns out all skew functions are of this form.
Theorem 49. $F$ is skew iff $F(a)=a \downharpoonleft A_{2}$ for a unique bivector $A_{2}$.
Proof. I just finished showing that any $F$ of this form is skew. Knowing $F$, I can reconstruct $A_{2}$ uniquely using any frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ and Theorem 38 when $r=2$ :

$$
\begin{aligned}
A_{2} & \left.=\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i}\right\rfloor A_{2}\right) \\
& =\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge F\left(a_{i}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Now assume $F$ is skew and let $A_{2}$ be defined as above. I find that for any $a$,

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft A_{2} & \left.=\frac{1}{2} a\right\rfloor\left(\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge F\left(a_{i}\right)\right) \\
& \left.=\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i}\left(a \downharpoonleft a^{i}\right) F\left(a_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i} a^{i}(a\rfloor F\left(a_{i}\right)\right) \\
& \left.=\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i}\left(a \downharpoonleft a^{i}\right) F\left(a_{i}\right)+\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i} a^{i}(F(a)\rfloor a_{i}\right) \\
& \left.=\frac{1}{2} F\left(\sum_{i}\left(a \downharpoonleft a^{i}\right) a_{i}\right)+\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i}(F(a)\rfloor a_{i}\right) a^{i} \\
& =\frac{1}{2} F(a)+\frac{1}{2} F(a) \\
& =F(a) .
\end{aligned}
$$ $\square$

Therefore every antisymmetric bilinear product is of the form $a \wedge b\rfloor A_{2}=a \downharpoonleft A_{2}\left\lfloor b\right.$ for some $A_{2}$.

### 9.5. Isometries and orthogonal transformations

The final special linear operator is an isometry, which preserves inner products: $F(u)\rfloor F(v)=u \downharpoonleft v$. (Equivalently, isometries preserve squares of vectors.) Isometries are always one-to-one, because

$$
\begin{aligned}
F(u)=0 & \Longrightarrow F(u)\rfloor F(v)=0 \quad \text { for all } v \\
& \Longrightarrow u \downharpoonleft v=0 \quad \text { for all } v \\
& \Longrightarrow u=0
\end{aligned}
$$

So in finite dimensions, isometries are also onto and thus invertible. An invertible isometry is called an orthogonal transformation. The two are distinct only on infinite-dimensional spaces, but most of the results I'll show don't actually require invertibility, so I'll continue to make the distinction.

Any power of an isometry is also an isometry, as is clear from the definition. An isometry satisfies

$$
u\rfloor v=F(u)\rfloor F(v)=\bar{F} F(u)\rfloor v,
$$

so if $F$ is an isometry then $\bar{F} F=\mathrm{Id}$. If $F$ is also invertible, then its inverse has to be $\bar{F}$, so we also have $F \bar{F}=\mathrm{Id}$. Therefore orthogonal transformations satisfy $\bar{F}=F^{-1}$ and are also normal, and as a bonus $F^{-1}$ is orthogonal too. And as with isometries, any power of an orthogonal transformation is also orthogonal.

### 9.5.1. Isometries and versors

So far I've described three isometries: the parity operation (which was extended to the whole algebra as grade involution in Section 5.1), reflections, and rotations (both in Section 7). Now a rotation is two reflections, and as I showed in Section 7.2.2, the parity operation is reflection in a volume element, which amounts to $n$ reflections in succession. So every isometry I've shown so far is a composition of reflections. That's no accident: the Cartan-Dieudonné theorem shows that every isometry in an $n$-dimensional space is the composition of at most $n$ reflections along axes. That's fantastic news, because reflections are easy to do in geometric algebra; so now we have powerful tools to perform and analyze any isometry at all.

So what does a general isometry look like? Remembering Eq. (237), I find that the isometry $F$ that takes vector $u$ and reflects it along axes $a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{r}$ in succession is

$$
\begin{aligned}
F(u) & =(-1)^{r}\left(a_{r} \cdots a_{2} a_{1}\right) u\left(a_{1}^{-1} a_{2}^{-1} \cdots a_{r}^{-1}\right) \\
& =\left(a_{r} \cdots a_{2} a_{1}\right) u^{* r}\left(a_{r} \cdots a_{2} a_{1}\right)^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
=A_{r} u^{* r} A_{r}^{-1}
$$

where $A_{r}=a_{r} \cdots a_{2} a_{1}$ is an invertible $r$-versor. Thus a general isometry in finite dimensions is grade involution followed by conjugation with an invertible versor. (This is why I defined versors in the first place, and it's also why I've been proving so many results not just for blades but for versors in general.) Now this looks a lot like Eq. (239) for reflecting a vector in a subspace; in fact, Eq. (239) is just a special case of this result, since a blade is a special type of versor. Therefore this operation extends to the whole algebra the same way reflection in subspaces did in Section 7.2.2 a general isometry on multivectors takes the form

$$
F(B)=A_{r} B^{* r} A_{r}^{-1}
$$

and it reduces to reflection in a subspace iff the versor $A_{r}$ is an $r$-blade. This also makes it clear that the isometries generated by $A_{r}$ and $A_{r}^{-1}$ are inverses of each other.

Even though every versor is associated with an isometry, the association isn't exactly one-to-one. After all, $A_{r}$ and $\lambda A_{r}$ generate the same isometry for any $\lambda \neq 0$. (The ultimate reason for this is that $a$ and $\lambda a$ represent the same axis, and thus the same reflection.) We can eliminate most of that ambiguity, however, by composing our versors out of unit vectors; in that case, $A_{r}$ is a unit versor. That doesn't eliminate the sign ambiguity, but we can live with that. Is there any further ambiguity? Amazingly, no. I'll show this in two steps. First, basically the same argument used to derive Eq. (247) shows that

$$
A_{r} \boldsymbol{I}^{* r} A_{r}^{-1}=(-1)^{r} \boldsymbol{I},
$$

so isometries divide into two classes: even isometries, which leave $\boldsymbol{I}$ alone and are represented by even versors, and odd isometries, which change the sign of $\boldsymbol{I}$ and are represented by odd versors. This also shows that an isometry is odd iff it's the composition of an even isometry and one reflection. As I said I would back in Section 2, I'll now start referring to any even invertible versor as a rotor, so rotors represent even isometries.

Now I'll prove the result.
Theorem 50. Versors $A_{r}$ and $B_{s}$ represent the same isometry iff $A_{r}=\lambda B_{s}$ for some $\lambda \neq 0$.
So if we consider only unit versors, the association of versors to isometries is exactly two-to-one.
Proof. If $A_{r}$ is a nonzero multiple of $B_{s}$, we know they represent the same isometry, so let's prove it the other way. Suppose $A_{r} u^{* r} A_{r}^{-1}=B_{s} u^{* s} B_{s}^{-1}$ for all $u$. Since $r$ and $s$ are both even or both odd, I can drop the grade involutions and I'm left with $A_{r} u A_{r}^{-1}=B_{s} u B_{s}^{-1}$, which can be sneakily rewritten

$$
B_{s}^{-1} A_{r} \times u=0 \quad \text { for all } u .
$$

Then Theorem 28 tells me that $B_{s}^{-1} A_{r}=\lambda+\mu\langle\boldsymbol{I}\rangle_{-}$. But $B_{s}^{-1} A_{r}$ is even, so its odd part vanishes and I'm left with $B_{s}^{-1} A_{r}=\lambda$. Now if $\lambda=0$, by Theorem 24 both $A_{r}$ and $B_{s}$ would be null, which they aren't since I've been inverting both of them. Therefore $\lambda \neq 0$ and $A_{r}=\lambda B_{s}$. $\square$

You might be tempted at this point to associate an isometry with a unique sequence of reflections, but you can't. That's because the factorization of a versor into vectors isn't unique. For example, suppose $a$ and $b$ are orthogonal Euclidean unit vectors; then

$$
a b=\left(\frac{a-b}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\left(\frac{a+b}{\sqrt{2}}\right),
$$

so in this case two different sequences of reflections give the same isometry. In fact, reflections along any orthonormal basis for a subspace will result in reflection in that subspace, so in that case infinitely many reflection sequences produce the same isometry. But that's only because they all produce the same versor to within a sign.

### 9.5.2. Rotors and biversors

Nothing I've done in this section up to now has made any assumptions about the scalars, but for this last part I assume the scalars are real. Every rotor is a product of biversors, so I want to take a moment to examine them. Consider $a b$ where $a$ and $b$ are unit vectors; it represents the composition of reflections in the $b$ and $a$ directions in succession, so the resulting isometry acts in the $a \wedge b$ plane. My plan to analyze $a b$ is to expand it as $a\rfloor b+a \wedge b$ and figure out each piece separately. I'll do that by starting with Eq. (8) from back in Section 1.1

$$
a^{2} b^{2}=(a \downharpoonleft b)^{2}-(a \wedge b)^{2} .
$$

Since $a$ and $b$ are unit vectors, the left hand side of Eq. (321) is ±1. In what follows, I will set $\mu=a \downharpoonleft b$, and I will set $a \wedge b=\lambda \boldsymbol{B}$ where $\boldsymbol{B}$ is a 2-blade. In the cases when $(a \wedge b)^{2} \neq 0$, I'll choose $\lambda$ so $\boldsymbol{B}$ is a unit blade; otherwise I'll come up with some other way to choose $\lambda$.

Before I get into the general cases, I'll handle a special case that I'll need to refer back to later: let $a$ and $b$ be orthogonal. Since $a \downharpoonleft b=0,(a \wedge b)^{2}= \pm 1$. So $a b=a \wedge b$ is already a unit blade $\boldsymbol{B}$. To see what isometry it generates, let $u$ lie in $\boldsymbol{B}$; then its product with $\boldsymbol{B}$ is an inner product, so they anticommute, so

$$
\boldsymbol{B} u \boldsymbol{B}^{-1}=-u \boldsymbol{B} \boldsymbol{B}^{-1}=-u .
$$

So versor $\boldsymbol{B}$ generates a reflection in the plane it represents. (Which we already knew from Section 7.2.1) The result in the general case depends on the sign of $\boldsymbol{B}^{2}$.

1. Suppose first that $\boldsymbol{B}^{2}=-1$. Then Eq. (175) when $n=2$ tells me that the number of negative-square vectors in a frame for $\boldsymbol{B}$ is either 0 or 2, so the inner product on $\boldsymbol{B}$ is either positive definite or negative definite, which I call the Euclidean or elliptic case. Then $a^{2}$ and $b^{2}$ have the same sign, so $a^{2} b^{2}=1$. Putting all this in Eq. (321), I find
$$
1=\mu^{2}+\lambda^{2} .
$$
Therefore $\mu=\cos (\theta / 2)$ and $\lambda=-\sin (\theta / 2)$ for some $\theta$, so
$$
\begin{aligned}
a b & =a \downharpoonleft b+a \wedge b \\
& =\cos (\theta / 2)-\boldsymbol{B} \sin (\theta / 2) \\
& =\exp (-\boldsymbol{B} \theta / 2)
\end{aligned}
$$
where the exponential is defined by its power series. You may remember this from the end of Section 1.2 it's a rotation through angle $\theta$ in the plane defined by $\boldsymbol{B}$. When $\theta=\pi$, I recover the special case I solved above: a rotation by $\pi$ in a Euclidean plane equals a reflection in the plane.
2. Now suppose $\boldsymbol{B}^{2}=1$. In this case the inner product is indefinite, which is called the hyperbolic case. Now I have to give some thought to $a^{2}$ and $b^{2}$. First let them have the same sign, so Eq. (321) becomes
$$
1=\mu^{2}-\lambda^{2} .
$$
Therefore $\mu= \pm \cosh (\phi / 2)$ and $\lambda=\mp \sinh (\phi / 2)$ for some $\phi$, so
$$
\begin{aligned}
a b & =a \downharpoonleft b+a \wedge b \\
& = \pm(\cosh (\phi / 2)-\boldsymbol{B} \sinh (\phi / 2)) \\
& = \pm \exp (-\boldsymbol{B} \phi / 2)
\end{aligned}
$$
where again the exponential is defined by its power series. (This isometry, by the way, is a rotation in the hyperbolic plane, and in special relativity it's a boost to velocity $c \tanh \phi$.) This time I couldn't absorb the sign of $a$ ↓ $b$ into a choice for the parameter, because cosh is always positive; but that affects only the rotor, not the corresponding isometry. So aside from that, the rotors for the last two cases have the same polar form, and the difference in their expansions as scalar plus bivector is due to the different behaviors of the area element.

3. Sticking with $\boldsymbol{B}^{2}=1$, now I consider $a^{2}=-b^{2}$. Eq. (321) becomes
$$
-1=\mu^{2}-\lambda^{2},
$$
so $\mu$ and $\lambda$ change roles: $\mu=\mp \sinh (\phi / 2)$ and $\lambda= \pm \cosh (\phi / 2)$ for some $\phi$, so for the rotor I get
$$
\begin{aligned}
a b & =a \downharpoonleft b+a \wedge b \\
& =\mp \sinh (\phi / 2) \pm \boldsymbol{B} \cosh (\phi / 2) \\
& = \pm \boldsymbol{B}(\cosh (\phi / 2)-\boldsymbol{B} \sinh (\phi / 2)) \\
& = \pm \boldsymbol{B} \exp (-\boldsymbol{B} \phi / 2)
\end{aligned}
$$
This rotor is the product of the previous one and the area element. As I showed in the special case above, this extra factor generates a reflection in the plane. Why is it showing up as a separate factor? Because unlike the Euclidean case, there is no hyperbolic rotation that performs a reflection in the plane, so it has to be included separately.
4. Finally, suppose $\boldsymbol{B}^{2}=0$, which means by Theorem 23 that the inner product is degenerate. (This also has a name: the parabolic case.) Now Eq. (321) reduces to $\pm 1=\mu^{2}$. This doesn't make sense if the left hand side can be -1, so let me show that it can't. The inner product may be degenerate, but it can't be identically zero, or every vector would be null and there would be no axes in the plane to reflect along. Therefore there's a non-null vector somewhere in there which I'll call $v$, and the direction orthogonal to it is a null vector which together with $v$ spans the plane. Because of this, the length squared of any vector in the plane is just $v^{2}$ times the square of its component along $v$, so they all have the same sign. Thus $a^{2} b^{2}=1$ and $\mu= \pm 1$. Let $\boldsymbol{B}$ be any 2-blade that's convenient to use to represent the plane; then $a \wedge b=\mp \lambda \boldsymbol{B}$ for some $\lambda$, so the rotor becomes
$$
\begin{aligned}
a b & =a \downharpoonleft b+a \wedge b \\
& = \pm\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{2} \boldsymbol{B}\right) \\
& = \pm \exp (-\boldsymbol{B} \lambda / 2)
\end{aligned}
$$
where once again the exponential is defined by its power series.
You may wonder what this rotor does. Its inverse is $\pm\left(1+\frac{\lambda}{2} \boldsymbol{B}\right)$, so for any vector $u$
$$
\begin{aligned}
(a b) u(a b)^{-1} & =\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{2} \boldsymbol{B}\right) u\left(1+\frac{\lambda}{2} \boldsymbol{B}\right) \\
& =u+\frac{\lambda}{2}(u \boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{B} u)-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4} \boldsymbol{B} u \boldsymbol{B} \\
& =u+\lambda u \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4} \boldsymbol{B} u \boldsymbol{B} .
\end{aligned}
$$
Each term on the right hand side is a vector, and you can directly verify that the square of the whole thing really is $u^{2}$. The verification is an interesting exercise; you find that $u \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}$ is orthogonal to $u$, $\boldsymbol{B} u \boldsymbol{B}$ is orthogonal to $u \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}, \boldsymbol{B} u \boldsymbol{B}$ is null, and the inner product of $u$ and $\boldsymbol{B} u \boldsymbol{B}$ cancels the square of $u \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}$.

Putting all this together, I've shown that a general even isometry on a real vector space consists of any number of rotations in planes (elliptic, hyperbolic, or parabolic) and reflections in hyperbolic planes. An odd isometry is the same thing plus one reflection along an axis. All the rotations can be represented in the same polar form; the properties of the different area elements produce different types of rotations. If the whole space is Euclidean, things simplify further because there are no hyperbolic or parabolic planes: every isometry is a sequence of rotations in planes, preceded (or followed) by one reflection if it's odd.

The set of all isometries on a finite-dimensional real vector space forms a group called the orthogonal group on that space. All of this analysis tells us two main things about such groups:

1. The subset of even isometries forms a group of its own (it includes the identity and is closed under products), and the subset of odd isometries is a one-to-one copy of the even subgroup. A reflection along any axis provides the relation between the two subsets.
2. Aside from reflections in hyperbolic planes, all elements of the even subgroup are functions of parameters that can be continuously varied to zero, which results in the identity transformation.

These properties tell me that a space's orthogonal group is an example of a Lie group. This is a group which can be divided into a finite number of isomorphic subsets, and within each subset the elements can be labeled with a finite number of continuously-variable parameters. Whichever subset is lucky enough to contain the identity is a subgroup in its own right, so a Lie group is a continuously-parameterised group together with some isomorphic copies. (In our case, reflections along axes and in hyperbolic planes move us back and forth between the copies.) After I learn how to use geometric algebra to study Lie groups in general, I believe I'll be showing that they form even subalgebras which are generated by exponentiating bivectors. But this is more than enough on isometries for now.

### 9.6. Extending linear functions to the whole algebra

So far, I have considered only linear functions defined on vectors; but functions on vectors have an obvious extension to the whole algebra. For example, consider the $r$-dimensional space spanned by $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$; this is mapped by linear function $F$ to the space spanned by $\left\{F\left(a_{i}\right)\right\}$. Since blades represent subspaces, it seems very natural to define $F$ not just on vectors but on blades too; I set

$$
F\left(a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right):=F\left(a_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge F\left(a_{r}\right) .
$$

If I then require this formal extension of $F$ to be linear over multivectors, I get $F(A \wedge B)=F(A) \wedge F(B)$ for any $A$ and $B$. Well, almost any $A$ and $B$; my picture doesn't tell me what $F$ should do to scalars. Assuming I figure that out, then I've found a way to naturally extend any linear function on vectors to a function on the whole geometric algebra that is not only linear but also respects outer products. As a matter of fact, I've actually done this already no fewer than six times. Four of the extensions were orthogonal projections, orthogonal rejections, reflections, and rotations in Section 7, and the fifth was general isometries in Section 9.5. I defined all five on vectors to start with, and I extended them in exactly the way I just suggested: I had them respect outer products. The sixth extension was grade involution in Section 5.1; I started with the parity operation $u \rightarrow-u$, but I extended it by making it respect not outer products but the full geometric product. (It ended up respecting outer products too, as the third equality in Eqs. (120) shows.) In retrospect, that looks a little daring; after all, a product of vectors contains terms of many different grades, and the rules I imposed on, say, three-fold and five-fold products could have put conflicting requirements on trivectors. So far, though, it looks like everything worked out. Whew. While my other five extensions look safer, because I didn't have different grades crossing over, they do raise a question: how do they act on products? Do you operate on each factor separately and then multiply them back together? That looks like it'd work for general isometries (and thus reflections and rotations) because the internal factors would cancel out, but I'm not too sure about projections and rejections. And then there's one property that all six extensions have in common: they leave scalars alone. What's up with that? Grade involution does it by definition, but the others were found to do so after their final definitions were stated. How come?

### 9.6.1. Outermorphisms

To answer these questions, I want to lay some groundwork by describing something a little more general. A linear function on geometric algebras that preserves outer products is called an outermorphism. It's not too hard to show that the composition of outermorphisms is also an outermorphism and that the inverse of an outermorphism, if it exists, is an outermorphism too. However, a linear combination of outermorphisms is not an outermorphism. To show this, let $\mathcal{F}=\alpha \mathcal{F}_{1}+\beta \mathcal{F}_{2}$ where $\mathcal{F}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{F}_{2}$ are outermorphisms, and try evaluating both $\mathcal{F}(A \wedge B)$ and $\mathcal{F}(A) \wedge \mathcal{F}(B)$. You'll see the problem pretty quickly. For this reason, compositions and inverses of outermorphisms pop up frequently, but linear combinations don't.

Next, outermorphisms are very restricted in how they handle scalars. First linearity has a say: an outermorphism $\mathcal{F}$ has to be linear over everything, not just vectors, which means $\mathcal{F}(\lambda)=\mathcal{F}(\lambda 1)=\lambda \mathcal{F}(1)$.

So $\mathcal{F}(1)$ determines $\mathcal{F}$ for all scalars. But scalar multiplication is also an outer product, so $1=1 \wedge 1$. Thus the outermorphism property requires $\mathcal{F}(1)=\mathcal{F}(1) \wedge \mathcal{F}(1)$. So like $1, \mathcal{F}(1)$ must equal its outer product with itself. Something that equals its own square is said to be idempotent; since we have several different products, we have several different types of idempotency. I've pointed out that 1 is an outer idempotent, and thanks to the outermorphism property, $\mathcal{F}(1)$ has to be an outer idempotent too. It turns out there aren't too many of those.

Theorem 51. If $A=A \wedge A$, then $A=0$ or 1.
Proof. Let $A=\sum_{r} A_{r}$; then $A=A \wedge A$ becomes

$$
A=\sum_{s, t}\left\langle A_{s} A_{t}\right\rangle_{s+t} .
$$

I'll look at this one grade at a time. The grade- $r$ part of this expression is

$$
A_{r}=\sum_{s=0}^{r}\left\langle A_{s} A_{r-s}\right\rangle_{r} .
$$

When $r=0$, this becomes

$$
A_{0}=A_{0}^{2},
$$

so $A_{0}$ is either 0 or 1 . When $r=1$, I find

$$
A_{1}=2 A_{0} A_{1} .
$$

Whether $A_{0}$ is 0 or 1 , this equation requires $A_{1}=0$.
To show that all remaining $A_{r}$ vanish, I proceed by induction. Suppose it's true for $r-1$; then most of the terms in the sum for $A_{r}$ drop out, leaving

$$
A_{r}=2 A_{0} A_{r} .
$$

Whether $A_{0}$ is 0 or 1, this gives me $A_{r}=0$, and that completes the proof. $\square$

So $\mathcal{F}(1)=0$ or 1 . That means outermorphisms can do only two things to scalars.
Theorem 52. If $\mathcal{F}$ is an outermorphism, then either $\mathcal{F}=0$ or $\mathcal{F}(\lambda)=\lambda$.
This is why all six extensions left scalars alone; they had to.
Proof. Either $\mathcal{F}(1)=0$ or $\mathcal{F}(1)=1$. In the former case, for any $A$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{F}(A) & =\mathcal{F}(1 A) \\
& =\mathcal{F}(1 \wedge A) \\
& =\mathcal{F}(1) \wedge \mathcal{F}(A) \\
& =0,
\end{aligned}
$$

so $\mathcal{F}=0$. In the latter case, $\mathcal{F}(\lambda)=\lambda$ for all $\lambda$ by linearity. $\square$

Next, I'll take a passing look at adjoints. Linear functions on geometric algebras have adjoints just as they do on any other vector spaces: if $\mathcal{F}: \mathcal{G}^{1} \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^{2}$ is linear, $\overline{\mathcal{F}}: \mathcal{G}^{2} \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^{1}$ is a linear function given by

$$
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(B) * A:=B * \mathcal{F}(A) \quad \text { for all } A \in \mathcal{G}^{1} \text { and } B \in \mathcal{G}^{2} .
$$

Outermorphisms are linear, so they have adjoints which are linear. I can't say more than that, though, until I consider a special class of outermorphisms, which is what's next.

### 9.6.2. Outermorphism extensions

Now, back to the reason we're here. My goal is to start with a linear function from vectors to vectors, define it on scalars by having it leave them alone (as Theorem 52 says I have to do), and then extend it to the rest of the algebra by linearity and respecting outer products. The result is an outermorphism that matches the original function on vectors. Can I start with any linear function and do this? Why sure; in fact, I can do it exactly one way.

Theorem 53. Let $F$ be a nonzero linear function that maps vectors to vectors; then there exists a unique outermorphism $[F]$ that reproduces $F$ when applied to vectors.

Proof. Existence is obvious, because definition on scalars and vectors, plus the outermorphism property, plus linearity is enough to define $[F]$ on any multivector. Uniqueness follows for the same reason. $\square$

I had to specify $F \neq 0$ because technically if $F=0$ then Theorem 52 allows two extensions: one is $[F]=0$, and the other is $[F](A)=\langle A\rangle$. Both extensions vanish on all vectors and all blades; they differ only in the scalar option from Theorem 52 they use.

It's clear that $[F]$ maps $r$-blades into $r$-blades, so

$$
\langle[F](A)\rangle_{r}=[F]\left(\langle A\rangle_{r}\right) .
$$

So by requiring $[F]$ to preserve grade 1, I find that it preserves all grades. It also follows that

$$
\begin{aligned}
& {[F](A)^{*}=[F]\left(A^{*}\right)} \\
& {[F](A)^{\dagger}=[F]\left(A^{\dagger}\right)} \\
& {[F](A)^{\ddagger}=[F]\left(A^{\ddagger}\right) .}
\end{aligned}
$$

If $\mathcal{G}_{1}$ is the vector space of $\mathcal{G}$, then it's also pretty clear that

$$
\left[\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right]=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{G}} .
$$

Now let's see how outermorphism extensions behave under composition, inverses, and adjoints; I'll show compositions and inverses first.

Theorem 54. If $U, V$, and $W$ are the vector spaces of $\mathcal{G}^{1}, \mathcal{G}^{2}$, and $\mathcal{G}^{3}$ respectively, and $F: U \rightarrow V$ and $G: V \rightarrow W$ are linear, then

(a) $[G F]=[G][F]$.
(b) if $F$ is invertible, $[F]^{-1}=\left[F^{-1}\right]$.

Proof. For part (a), there's no problem with the action on scalars or vectors, so all I need to check is the outermorphism property. I will check it for the product of vectors, which covers all the higher-grade cases too.

$$
\begin{aligned}
{[G]\left[[F]\left(u_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge u_{r}\right)\right] } & =[G]\left[F\left(u_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge F\left(u_{r}\right)\right] \\
& =G F\left(u_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge G F\left(u_{r}\right) \\
& =[G F]\left(u_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge u_{r}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Part (b) follows from part (a):

$$
\begin{aligned}
{\left[F^{-1}\right][F] } & =\left[F^{-1} F\right] \\
& =\left[\mathrm{Id}_{U}\right] \\
& =\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{G}^{1}},
\end{aligned}
$$

and similarly $[F]\left[F^{-1}\right]=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{G}^{2}}$, so $\left[F^{-1}\right]$ is the inverse of $[F]$. $\square$

As for adjoints, outermorphism extensions obey these relations, which (for the moment) use an amazing number of brackets.

Theorem 55.

$$
\begin{aligned}
A\rfloor\{[\bar{F}](B)\} & =[\bar{F}](\{[F](A)\}\rfloor B) \\
\{[F](A)\}\lfloor B & =[F](A\lfloor\{[\bar{F}](B)\})
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore $\overline{[F]}=[\bar{F}]$.
Because this theorem is hard to read, I'm going to state it in words. The purpose of the adjoint is to let you move $F$ from one side of the scalar product to the other, as long as you change $F$ to $\bar{F}$ along the way. This theorem says you can also move $[F]$ from the "high side" of the inner product to the "low side," as long as (a) you change $[F]$ to $[\bar{F}]$ and (b) you then act on the whole thing with $[F]$.

Take a look at two extreme cases. If the multivector on the low side is a scalar, the inner products become products, $[F]$ leaves the scalar alone, and it factors out. That's why there's an extra $[F]$ (or $[\bar{F}]$ ) acting on the whole thing. On the other extreme, if both sides have the same grade then the inner products are scalars and the extra $[F]$ or [F] drops out because it leaves scalars alone. With a little tweaking, that gets us back to the definition of the adjoint in Eq. (338). I'll do that tweaking in the proof.

Proof. I'll prove only the first relation; the second relation is the reverse of the first with a few substitutions. The result is true for general $A$ and $B$ if it's true for $A_{r}$ and $B_{s}$. If $r>s$ both sides vanish identically, so let $r \leq s$. If $r=0$, then $A_{r}=\lambda$ and both sides reduce to $\lambda[\bar{F}]\left(B_{s}\right)$. If $s=0$, then $r=0$ so we're back to the previous case. For the remaining cases, I consider blades $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$. Next I'll prove $r=1$ and any $s \geq 1$. It's true for $s=1$, so assume it's true for $s-1$, let $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=b \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}$, and consider

$$
\begin{aligned}
a\rfloor[\bar{F}]\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) & =a\rfloor[\bar{F}]\left(b \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right) \\
& =a\rfloor\left[\bar{F}(b) \wedge[\bar{F}]\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=[a\rfloor \bar{F}(b)][\bar{F}]\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right)-\bar{F}(b) \wedge[a\rfloor[\bar{F}]\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=[F(a)\rfloor b][\bar{F}]\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right)-\bar{F}(b) \wedge[\bar{F}](F(a)\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right) \\
& \left.\left.=[\bar{F}][(F(a)\rfloor b) \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right]-[\bar{F}]\left[b \wedge(F(a)\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=[\bar{F}][(F(a)\rfloor b) \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}-b \wedge\left(F(a) \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=[\bar{F}][F(a)\rfloor\left(b \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\mathbf{1}}\right)\right] \\
& =[\bar{F}]\left(F(a) \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Now for general $r \leq s$. Fix $s$ and assume the result is true for $r-1$; then let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \wedge a$ and consider

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right\rfloor[\bar{F}]\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) & \left.=\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \wedge a\right)\right\rfloor[\bar{F}]\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) \\
& \left.=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor\left(a 」[\bar{F}]\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)\right) \\
& \left.\left.=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right\rfloor[\bar{F}](F(a)\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) \\
& \left.\left.=[\bar{F}]\left[[F]\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right)\right](F(a)] \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=[\bar{F}]\left[\left([F]\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}}\right) \wedge F(a)\right)\right] \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right] \\
& \left.=[\bar{F}]\left([F]\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}-\mathbf{1}} \wedge a\right)\right] \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) \\
& \left.=[\bar{F}]\left([F]\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

And that takes care of all cases.
Finally, I'll show that $\overline{[F]}=[\bar{F}]$.

$$
\left.A *[\bar{F}](B)=\left\langle A^{\dagger}\right\rfloor\{[\bar{F}](B)\}\right\rangle
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.=\left\langle[\bar{F}]\left(\left\{[F]\left(A^{\dagger}\right)\right\}\right\rfloor B\right)\right\rangle \\
& \left.=[\bar{F}]\left(\left\langle\left\{[F]\left(A^{\dagger}\right)\right\}\right\rfloor B\right\rangle\right) \\
& \left.=\left\langle\left\{[F]\left(A^{\dagger}\right)\right\}\right\rfloor B\right\rangle \\
& \left.=\left\langle\{[F](A)\}^{\dagger}\right\rfloor B\right\rangle \\
& =[F](A) * B .
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus $[\bar{F}]$ satisfies Eq. (338) with $\mathcal{F}=[F]$, so $\overline{[F]}=[\bar{F}]$. $\square$

Given the uniqueness of the outermorphism extension and its good behavior under composition, inverses, and adjoints, I will now happily drop the $[F]$ notation and let $F$ refer either to the linear function on vectors or the resulting outermorphism. That certainly makes Theorem 55 easier to read: I'll take $A\rfloor F(B)=$ $F(\bar{F}(A)\rfloor B)$ any day.

Recall that the restriction of $F$ to the subspace represented by $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is denoted $F_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}$. This restriction and $F\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)$ are related in an important way.

Theorem 56. $F\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)=0$ iff $\operatorname{rank}\left(F_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\right)<r$, which is true iff $F_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}$ is not one-to-one.
Proof. $F\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)=0$ iff $F$ maps $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ to a subspace of dimension smaller than $r$, which means $\operatorname{rank}\left(F_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\right)<r$. Since $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is finite-dimensional, by the rank-nullity theorem this is true iff $\operatorname{null}\left(F_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\right)>0$, so $F$ is not one-to-one. $\square$

So $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \in \operatorname{Ker} F$ iff $\operatorname{Ker}\left(F_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}\right) \neq\{0\}$. Note the two different meanings of $F$ in these two statements.
Now only one mystery remains unsolved: why did grade involution turn out to be an outermorphism even though I made it preserve geometric products instead of outer products? Because the parity operation on which it's based has a special property.

Theorem 57. The following conditions on $F$ are equivalent.

1. $F$ is an isometry.
2. $F(A B)=F(A) F(B)$ for all $A$ and $B$.
3. $F(A\rfloor B)=F(A)\rfloor F(B)$ and $F(A\lfloor B)=F(A)\lfloor F(B)$ for all $A$ and $B$.

Proof. I first assume $F$ is an isometry. That means $F(a)\rfloor F(b)=a \downharpoonleft b$ for all vectors; but $a \downharpoonleft b$ is a scalar, so $F(a \downarrow b)=a\rfloor b$, so $F(a\rfloor b)=F(a) \downharpoonleft F(b)$. Since $F(a \wedge b)=F(a) \wedge F(b)$ by the outermorphism property, it follows that $F(a b)=F(a) F(b)$. I can extend this to $F\left(a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right)=$ $F\left(a_{1}\right) F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right)$ by induction: the result is true for $r=2$, so assume it's true for $r-1$ and consider

$$
\begin{aligned}
F\left(a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right) & \left.=F\left[a_{1}\right\rfloor\left(a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right)\right]+F\left[a_{1} \wedge\left(a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right)\right] \\
& \left.=F\left[\sum_{j=2}^{r}(-1)^{j-2} a_{1}\right\rfloor a_{j} a_{2} \cdots \check{a}_{j} \cdots a_{r}\right]+F\left(a_{1}\right) \wedge F\left[a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right] \\
& \left.=\sum_{j=2}^{r}(-1)^{j-2} a_{1}\right\rfloor a_{j} F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots \check{F}\left(a_{j}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right)+F\left(a_{1}\right) \wedge\left\{F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right)\right\} \\
& \left.=\sum_{j=2}^{r}(-1)^{j-2} F\left(a_{1}\right)\right\rfloor F\left(a_{j}\right) F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots \check{F}\left(a_{j}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right)+F\left(a_{1}\right) \wedge\left\{F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right)\right\} \\
& \left.=F\left(a_{1}\right)\right\rfloor\left\{F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right)\right\}+F\left(a_{1}\right) \wedge\left\{F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right)\right\} \\
& =F\left(a_{1}\right) F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

Now let's look at $F(A B)$; by linearity it's a sum of terms of the form $F\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)$. If $r=0$ or $s=0$ then $F\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)=F\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right) F\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)$ by linearity and Theorem 52, so let $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=b_{1} b_{2} \cdots b_{s}$; then Eq. (348) lets me show

$$
\begin{aligned}
F\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right) & =F\left(a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r} b_{1} b_{2} \cdots b_{s}\right) \\
& =F\left(a_{1}\right) F\left(a_{2}\right) \cdots F\left(a_{r}\right) F\left(b_{1}\right) F\left(b_{2}\right) \cdots F\left(b_{s}\right) \\
& =F\left(a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right) F\left(b_{1} b_{2} \cdots b_{s}\right) \\
& =F\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right) F\left(\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore $F(A B)=F(A) F(B)$. Notice that the products among the $a_{i}$ equal outer products, and the same is true of products among the $b_{j}$; it is the product of $a_{r}$ and $b_{1}$ that does not equal an outer product, and this is the reason I needed $F(a b)=F(a) F(b)$, not just $F(a \wedge b)=F(a) \wedge F(b)$, for the proof.

Next, assume $F(A B)=F(A) F(B)$ for all $A$ and $B$. Then, since $F$ commutes with all grade operators,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.F\left(A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{s}\right) & =F\left(\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{s-r}\right) \\
& =\left\langle F\left(A_{r} B_{s}\right)\right\rangle_{s-r} \\
& =\left\langle F\left(A_{r}\right) F\left(B_{s}\right)\right\rangle_{s-r} \\
& \left.=F\left(A_{r}\right)\right\rfloor F\left(B_{s}\right),
\end{aligned}
$$

with the last line following because $F$ preserves grades. Thus

$$
F(A\rfloor B)=F(A)\rfloor F(B)
$$

in general. Replacing $s-r$ with $r-s$ in the proof yields the same result for the right inner product.

Finally, assume $F(A\rfloor B)=F(A) \downharpoonleft F(B)$ for all $A$ and $B$; then $F(a) \downharpoonleft F(b)=F(a \downarrow b)=a \downharpoonleft b$ for any two vectors $a$ and $b$, where the last equality follows because $a \downharpoonleft b$ is a scalar. Therefore $F$ is an isometry, and all three results listed above are equivalent.

If I had assumed $F$ was not just an isometry but orthogonal, I could have used $\bar{F}=F^{-1}$ and Theorem 55 to prove the third part, but this way is better because it assumes less (on infinite-dimensional spaces at least). $\square$

Thus an isometry may be extended by having it respect either outer products or geometric products, with the same result. That won't work for anything else, though. This is why grade involution came out fine, and that's why I could indeed have extended reflections and rotations by respecting products instead of outer products. Projections and rejections had to be done the way I did them, however.

### 9.7. Eigenblades and invariant subspaces

Vector $a$ is an eigenvector of $F$ with eigenvalue $\lambda$ if $F(a)=\lambda a$, or equivalently $a \in \operatorname{Ker}(F-\lambda \operatorname{Id})$. In this definition $a$ can't be zero, but $\lambda$ can. Now suppose $a_{1}$ and $a_{2}$ are eigenvectors with eigenvalues $\lambda_{1}$ and $\lambda_{2}$ and let $\boldsymbol{A}=a_{1} \wedge a_{2}$; then

$$
\begin{aligned}
F(\boldsymbol{A}) & =F\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2}\right) \\
& =F\left(a_{1}\right) \wedge F\left(a_{2}\right) \\
& =\lambda_{1} \lambda_{2} a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \\
& =\lambda_{1} \lambda_{2} \boldsymbol{A} .
\end{aligned}
$$

So $\boldsymbol{A}$ is an eigenblade of $F$; it's mapped by $F$ to a multiple of itself.
An eigenblade is defined generally by $F(\boldsymbol{A})=\lambda \boldsymbol{A}$, regardless of what the factors of $\boldsymbol{A}$ do. Rotation operators nicely illustrate the different ways eigenblades can arise, and how they are related (or not) to eigenvectors. Let $F(A)=\exp (-\boldsymbol{B} \theta / 2) A \exp (\boldsymbol{B} \theta / 2)$, so $F$ is a rotation in plane $\boldsymbol{B}$ through angle $\theta$. Then

$$
F(\boldsymbol{B})=\exp (-\boldsymbol{B} \theta / 2) \boldsymbol{B} \exp (\boldsymbol{B} \theta / 2)
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& =\exp (-\boldsymbol{B} \theta / 2) \exp (\boldsymbol{B} \theta / 2) \boldsymbol{B} \\
& =\boldsymbol{B},
\end{aligned}
$$

so $\boldsymbol{B}$ is an eigenplane of the rotation operator with eigenvalue 1 . However, since every vector in $\boldsymbol{B}$ gets rotated, in general none of them are eigenvectors. (The exception is $\boldsymbol{B}^{2}=-1$ and $\theta=\pi$, which is a reflection: every vector in the plane is an eigenvector with eigenvalue -1.) You can check that $\boldsymbol{B}^{\perp}$ is also an eigenblade with eigenvalue 1, but that's for a different reason: every vector in $\boldsymbol{B}^{\perp}$ is left alone by the rotation. So $F(\boldsymbol{A})=\boldsymbol{A}$ is consistent with $F(a)=a$ for every vector in $\boldsymbol{A}$, but it's consistent with many other things too.

If $\lambda=0$, then Theorem 56 tells me that $F$ is not one-to-one on $\boldsymbol{A}$, but that's all it tells me. On the other hand, $\lambda \neq 0$ tells me all sorts of things. First, Theorem 56 says $F$ is one-to-one on $\boldsymbol{A}$, so $F_{\boldsymbol{A}}$ is invertible. But it also hints at what $\operatorname{Range}\left(F_{\boldsymbol{A}}\right)$ is. In fact, it's hard to see how $F$ could map $\boldsymbol{A}$ to a multiple of itself unless it also mapped all members of $\boldsymbol{A}$ back into $\boldsymbol{A}$. That would make $\boldsymbol{A}$ an invariant subspace of $F$; that is, a subspace that is mapped to itself by $F$. Put that together with Theorem 56 and you get this result.

Theorem 58. Any eigenblade of $F$ with nonzero eigenvalue is an invariant subspace of $F$ on which $F$ is invertible.

Proof. Suppose $F(\boldsymbol{A})=\lambda \boldsymbol{A}$ and $\lambda \neq 0$. We already know that $F$ is invertible on $\boldsymbol{A}$, so I'll prove the first part. If $a$ lies in $\boldsymbol{A}$, then $\boldsymbol{A} \wedge a=0$, in which case

$$
\begin{aligned}
\boldsymbol{A} \wedge F(a) & =\lambda^{-1} \lambda \boldsymbol{A} \wedge F(a) \\
& =\lambda^{-1} F(\boldsymbol{A}) \wedge F(a) \\
& =\lambda^{-1} F(\boldsymbol{A} \wedge a) \\
& =0,
\end{aligned}
$$

so $F(a)$ lies in $\boldsymbol{A}$ too. $\square$

So $F$ maps $\boldsymbol{A}$ invertibly onto $\boldsymbol{A}$. Now this is true for any $\lambda \neq 0$; but what does the actual value of $\lambda$ tell us? Well, if $F(\boldsymbol{A})=\lambda \boldsymbol{A}$ then $|F(\boldsymbol{A})|^{2}=\lambda^{2}|\boldsymbol{A}|^{2}$, so the value of $\lambda$ determines how much the norm squared of $\boldsymbol{A}$ changes. If the scalars are real, I can interpret this further. Recalling Eq. (173) for the weight of a blade, I find that

$$
\operatorname{weight}(F(\boldsymbol{A}))=|\lambda| \operatorname{weight}(\boldsymbol{A}),
$$

so $F$ multiplies the weight associated with $\boldsymbol{A}$ by $|\lambda|$; and $F$ changes the orientation of $\boldsymbol{A}$ if $\lambda$ is negative. This suggests to me that $\lambda$ is actually the determinant of $F_{\boldsymbol{A}}$, since it seems to be the factor by which the volume of $\boldsymbol{A}$ changes. The idea that the determinant of a linear transformation is actually an eigenvalue, not of the original transformation but of its outermorphism extension, is worth following up on and has general validity, even if the scalars aren't real. So I'll do that next.

### 9.8 The determinant

The determinant of a linear transformation is the factor by which it multiplies the volume element of the space it acts on. Finding it in geometric algebra is easy; we consider $F(\boldsymbol{I})$. This is an $n$-blade, so it has to be a multiple of $\boldsymbol{I}$. (Put another way, $\boldsymbol{I}$ is an eigenblade of all linear transformations.) That multiple is the determinant, or

$$
F(\boldsymbol{I})=: \operatorname{det}(F) \boldsymbol{I} .
$$

An equivalent definition is

$$
\operatorname{det}(F)=F(\boldsymbol{I})^{\perp} .
$$

This way of defining the determinant is very intuitive and also very easy to use in calculations, as I'll show.
First, it's obvious that $\operatorname{det}(\mathrm{Id})=1$. Now let $F$ and $G$ be linear transformations; since

$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{det}(F G) \boldsymbol{I} & =F G(\boldsymbol{I}) \\
& =F(\operatorname{det}(G) \boldsymbol{I})
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& =\operatorname{det}(G) F(\boldsymbol{I}) \\
& =\operatorname{det}(G) \operatorname{det}(F) \boldsymbol{I},
\end{aligned}
$$

I find with minimum fuss that

$$
\operatorname{det}(F G)=\operatorname{det}(F) \operatorname{det}(G) .
$$

Therefore if $F$ is invertible,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{det}\left(F^{-1}\right) \operatorname{det}(F) & =\operatorname{det}\left(F^{-1} F\right) \\
& =\operatorname{det}(\mathrm{Id}) \\
& =1
\end{aligned}
$$

so

$$
\operatorname{det}\left(F^{-1}\right)=\operatorname{det}(F)^{-1} .
$$

That tells me that $\operatorname{det}(F) \neq 0$ if $F$ is invertible. I'll use that later.
Now for adjoints. From the definition in Eq. (338),

$$
\begin{aligned}
\bar{F}(\boldsymbol{I}) * \boldsymbol{I} & =\boldsymbol{I} * F(\boldsymbol{I}) \\
\operatorname{det}(\bar{F}) \boldsymbol{I} * \boldsymbol{I} & =\operatorname{det}(F) \boldsymbol{I} * \boldsymbol{I} \\
\operatorname{det}(\bar{F}) & =\operatorname{det}(F)
\end{aligned}
$$

It's easy when you know how.
Next I'll calculate the determinants of some specific operators.

- From Eq. (235) I get the determinants of orthogonal projections and rejections:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{det}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\right) & =\delta_{r n} \\
\operatorname{det}\left(R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{r}}\right) & =\delta_{r 0} .
\end{aligned}
$$
- Let $F$ be a symmetric operator with a frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ of eigenvectors with eigenvalues $\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}$. Then $a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}$ is a volume element, so
$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{det}(F) a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n} & =F\left(a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}\right) \\
& =F\left(a_{1}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge F\left(a_{n}\right) \\
& =\lambda_{1} \cdots \lambda_{n} a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}
\end{aligned}
$$
so
$$
\operatorname{det}(F)=\lambda_{1} \cdots \lambda_{n} .
$$
- If $F$ is orthogonal, then $F(v)=A_{r} v^{* r} A_{r}^{-1}$ for some rotor $A_{r}$, so Eq. (318) shows that
$$
\operatorname{det}(F)=(-1)^{r} .
$$

In real matrix algebra, there's a well-known relationship between determinants, invertibility, and adjoints: a matrix is invertible iff its determinant is nonzero, in which case its inverse is its adjugate (adjoint of the cofactor matrix) divided by its determinant. The geometric algebra equivalent, as you would expect, applies to the linear operator itself, not its matrix representation on some basis. On top of that, it's valid for any multivector.

Theorem 59. $F$ is invertible iff $\operatorname{det}(F) \neq 0$, and for any multivector $A$

$$
F^{-1}(A)=\frac{\bar{F}\left(A^{-\perp}\right)^{\perp}}{\operatorname{det}(F)} .
$$

Recall that ${ }^{\perp}$ is the duality transform and ${ }^{-\perp}$ is its inverse.
Proof. I've already proven that if $F$ is invertible, $\operatorname{det}(F) \neq 0$, so let's go the other way. Suppose $\operatorname{det}(F) \neq 0$ and let $G$ be defined by $G(A)=\bar{F}\left(A^{-\perp}\right)^{\perp}$. Then I use the result $\left.A\right\rfloor \bar{F}(B)=$ $\bar{F}(F(A)\rfloor B)$ from Theorem 55 to get

$$
\begin{aligned}
G F(A) & =G[F(A)] \\
& =\bar{F}\left[F(A)^{-\perp}\right]^{\perp} \\
& =\bar{F}[F(A)\rfloor \boldsymbol{I}] \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} \\
& =[A\rfloor \bar{F}(\boldsymbol{I})] \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} \\
& =\operatorname{det}(\bar{F})(A\rfloor \boldsymbol{I}) \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} \\
& =\operatorname{det}(F) A \boldsymbol{I} \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} \\
& =\operatorname{det}(F) A
\end{aligned}
$$

A similar argument shows $F G(A)=\operatorname{det}(F) A$, so $F^{-1}=G / \operatorname{det}(F)$. $\square$

You can see that you need the full apparatus of geometric algebra to do this: I take the dual, which is a geometric product with a volume element, and I need to use the outermorphism extension of $F$, because calculating $F^{-1}(a)$ involves calculating $\bar{F}\left(a^{-\perp}\right)$, and $a^{-\perp}$ is not a vector. (Unless we're in a two-dimensional space, I suppose.)

## 10. Applications

### 10.1. Classical particle mechanics

The most obvious place to apply geometric algebra is classical mechanics, since it relies heavily on vector algebra already. In this section only I'll adopt the notational conventions of classical mechanics, so vectors are denoted $\boldsymbol{a}, \boldsymbol{b}$, and so on, the magnitude of vector $\boldsymbol{a}$ is denoted $a$, unit vectors are indicated with an overhat ${ }^{\text {^ }}$, and the derivative of any quantity with respect to time is indicated by an overdot '. Since boldface means something else, I will not use boldface for blades in this section. The material in this section is largely drawn from 3 and 4.

### 10.1.1. Angular momentum as a bivector

As a particle moves over time, its position vector $\boldsymbol{r}$ sweeps out area at a rate that a picture easily shows to be

$$
\dot{A}=\frac{1}{2} \boldsymbol{r} \wedge \boldsymbol{v}
$$

where $\boldsymbol{v}=\dot{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is the particle's velocity vector. Unsurprisingly, the rate at which area is swept out is a 2-blade. This blade is proportional to the dynamical quantity

$$
L:=\boldsymbol{r} \wedge \boldsymbol{p}=m \boldsymbol{r} \wedge \boldsymbol{v}=2 m \dot{A},
$$

called the angular momentum. In standard vector algebra, angular momentum is defined to be the vector $\boldsymbol{L}=\boldsymbol{r} \times \boldsymbol{p}$, the cross product of $\boldsymbol{r}$ and $\boldsymbol{p}$; the definition given here is the dual of that vector (see Eq. (208)), which is more natural given the association with areas. Nonetheless, since the algebraic properties of the outer and cross products are so similar, much of what one knows from the standard treatment holds without change; for example,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\dot{L} & =m \boldsymbol{v} \wedge \boldsymbol{v}+m \boldsymbol{r} \wedge \dot{\boldsymbol{v}} \\
& =m \boldsymbol{r} \wedge \dot{\boldsymbol{v}} \\
& =\boldsymbol{r} \wedge \boldsymbol{F},
\end{aligned}
$$

so $L$ is conserved iff the force $\boldsymbol{F}$ is central (parallel or antiparallel to $\boldsymbol{r}$ ). Since $L$ is conserved iff $\dot{A}=0, \mathrm{I}$ have Kepler's Second Law: the position vector of a particle subject to central forces sweeps out equal areas in equal times. Further, the plane in which central force motion takes place is $L$ itself. Writing $\boldsymbol{r}=r \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}$, which implies $\boldsymbol{v}=\dot{r} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}+r \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}$, I find

$$
\begin{aligned}
L & =\boldsymbol{r} \wedge \boldsymbol{p}=m \boldsymbol{r} \wedge \boldsymbol{v} \\
& =m r \hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge(\dot{r} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}+r \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}) \\
& =m r^{2} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}
\end{aligned}
$$

since $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}=0$. But I know a bit more than that; $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a unit vector, or $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}\rfloor \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}=1$, the time derivative of which is $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}\rfloor \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}=0$. This is just the familiar fact that a constant-length vector and its time derivative must always be perpendicular. (Incidentally, this shows that $\boldsymbol{v}=\dot{r} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}+r \dot{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a decomposition into radial and tangential components.) If $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}\rfloor \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}=0$, then $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}$, so

$$
L=m r^{2} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}} .
$$

Now this is nice because the geometric product has better properties than the outer product, and this is the first algebraic feature of this treatment that is genuinely new. Since $L$ is a bivector,

$$
L=-L^{\dagger}=-m r^{2} \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}
$$

so the scalar $l$, the magnitude of $L$, is given by

$$
l^{2}:=|L|^{2}=L^{\dagger} L=-L^{2}=m^{2} r^{4} \dot{\hat{r}}^{2} .
$$

Notice that $l$ equals the magnitude of the angular momentum vector from standard treatments. It should, of course, since bivector $L$ and the angular momentum vector are duals.

### 10.1.2. The Kepler problem

The Kepler problem is to determine the motion of a point particle of mass $m$ moving in a potential of the form $V=-k / r$, where $r$ is the particle's distance from some fixed origin. The particle experiences a force

$$
\boldsymbol{F}=-\frac{k}{r^{2}} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}
$$

where the constant $k$ is positive for an attractive force and negative for a repulsive force, so the particle's acceleration is given by

$$
\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}=-\frac{k}{m r^{2}} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}} .
$$

Now take a look at Eqs. (373) and (377). One is proportional to $r^{2}$ while the other is inversely proportional to $r^{2}$, so their product is independent of $r$. In fact, let me calculate the product:

$$
\begin{aligned}
L \dot{\boldsymbol{v}} & =\left(-m r^{2} \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)\left(-\frac{k}{m r^{2}} \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}\right) \\
& =k \dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}},
\end{aligned}
$$

and since $L$ is conserved and $k$ is a constant, this implies

$$
\frac{d}{d t}(L \boldsymbol{v}-k \hat{\boldsymbol{r}})=0
$$

Well, look at that: another constant of motion. The second term in the constant, $k \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}$, is clearly a vector, and the first term can be written

$$
L \boldsymbol{v}=L\lfloor\boldsymbol{v}+L \wedge \boldsymbol{v}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& =L\lfloor\boldsymbol{v}+m \boldsymbol{r} \wedge \boldsymbol{v} \wedge \boldsymbol{v} \\
& =L\lfloor\boldsymbol{v} .
\end{aligned}
$$

This is hardly a surprise; $\boldsymbol{v}$ is a vector in the plane defined by $L$, so by Theorems 16 and 17, $L \boldsymbol{v}=L\lfloor\boldsymbol{v}$ is a nonzero vector in the plane of $L$ perpendicular to $\boldsymbol{v}$. Thus the conserved quantity is a vector in the plane of motion; it is often called the "Laplace-Runge-Lenz vector," and in traditional vector algebra treatments of the Kepler problem it typically appears at the end as the result of a great deal of work. Here it was the first thing I found.

I would actually prefer to define a dimensionless conserved vector, and this quantity clearly has dimensions of $k$, so I define a conserved vector $\boldsymbol{e}$ by

$$
e:=\frac{L v}{k}-\hat{r} .
$$

I'd like to use this equation to get further expressions describing the motion of the particle; first is the polar equation, $r$ as a function of direction. Since the expression for $\boldsymbol{e}$ has $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}$ in it and $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{r}=r$, it follows that I can get an equation for $r$ by multiplying Eq. (380) by $k \boldsymbol{r}$, with the result

$$
L \boldsymbol{v} \boldsymbol{r}=k(\hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{r}+\boldsymbol{e r}) .
$$

The left hand side equals

$$
\begin{aligned}
L \boldsymbol{v} \boldsymbol{r} & =L(\boldsymbol{v}\rfloor \boldsymbol{r}+\boldsymbol{v} \wedge \boldsymbol{r}) \\
& =(\boldsymbol{r}\rfloor \boldsymbol{v}) L-\frac{L^{2}}{m} \\
& \left.=\frac{l^{2}}{m}+(\boldsymbol{r}\rfloor \boldsymbol{v}\right) L
\end{aligned}
$$

while $\hat{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{r}=r$ and $\boldsymbol{e r}=e r \cos \theta+\boldsymbol{e} \wedge \boldsymbol{r}$, so putting it all together

$$
\frac{l^{2}}{m}+(\boldsymbol{r} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{v}) L=k(r+e r \cos \theta+\boldsymbol{e} \wedge \boldsymbol{r}),
$$

or on separating the scalar and bivector parts,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{l^{2}}{m} & =k(r+e r \cos \theta) \\
(\boldsymbol{r}\rfloor \boldsymbol{v}) L & =\boldsymbol{e} \wedge \boldsymbol{r} .
\end{aligned}
$$

The scalar equation can be solved for $r$ with the result

$$
r=\frac{l^{2} / m k}{1+e \cos \theta},
$$

which is the equation for a conic section with eccentricity $e$ and one focus at the origin. Since the length of $\boldsymbol{e}$ is the eccentricity of the orbit, $\boldsymbol{e}$ is naturally called the eccentricity vector, which is the name I'll use for it henceforth.

The direction of $\boldsymbol{e}$ also has a geometrical meaning, but it's different in the attractive and repulsive cases, so I'll do one at a time. First I assume $k>0$ and I note that $r$ equals its minimum and maximum values when $\theta=0$ and $\pi$ respectively, which means that $\boldsymbol{e}$ points toward the particle's point of closest approach, called its periapsis, and away from its point of farthest retreat, called the apoapsis. (Fun fact: these two points are called the perigee and apogee if you're orbiting the earth, the perihelion and aphelion if you're orbiting the sun, and the pericynthion and apocynthion if you're orbiting the moon. So now you know.)

Now the repulsive case. If $k<0$, we run into a problem: $r$ has to be non-negative, so we have to have $1+e \cos \theta \leq 0$ for at least some values of $\theta$, and the orbit may include only those values. This is possible iff $e>1$, with the result that the orbit is a hyperbola. In this case, $r$ takes on its smallest value when $\theta=\pi$, so in the repulsive case the eccentricity vector points away from the periapsis.

The motion in the Kepler problem is completely determined by two vectors, the initial position and velocity, which are themselves determined by six parameters. The conserved angular momentum supplies three parameters because it's a bivector, and the conserved eccentricity vector supplies two more (only two because the angular momentum fixes the plane of the motion), so the motion is completely determined by these two constants plus one further parameter, which may be taken to be the initial value of $\theta$. If that's the case, then anything that doesn't depend on the starting point, such as any other constants of motion, should be a function of only $L$ and $\boldsymbol{e}$. I'll now show that this is the case for the energy by finding the magnitude of the eccentricity vector.

$$
\begin{aligned}
(L \boldsymbol{v}-k \hat{\boldsymbol{r}})^{2} & =k^{2} e^{2} \\
\left.(L \boldsymbol{v})^{2}-2 k(L \boldsymbol{v})\right\rfloor \hat{\boldsymbol{r}}+k^{2} & =k^{2} e^{2}
\end{aligned}
$$

Using the fact that a vector equals its own reverse, the first term on the left hand side can be calculated as

$$
\begin{aligned}
(L \boldsymbol{v})^{2} & =L \boldsymbol{v} L \boldsymbol{v} \\
& =(L \boldsymbol{v})^{\dagger} L \boldsymbol{v} \\
& =\boldsymbol{v} L^{\dagger} L \boldsymbol{v} \\
& =l^{2} v^{2} .
\end{aligned}
$$

The second term on the left is $-2 k$ times

$$
\begin{aligned}
(L \boldsymbol{v})\rfloor \hat{\boldsymbol{r}} & =\frac{(L \boldsymbol{v})\rfloor \boldsymbol{r}}{r} \\
& =\frac{\langle L \boldsymbol{v} \boldsymbol{r}\rangle}{r} \\
& =\frac{l^{2}}{m r},
\end{aligned}
$$

where in the last line I used Eq. (382), so now Eq. (386) becomes

$$
\begin{aligned}
l^{2} v^{2}-\frac{2 k l^{2}}{m r} & =k^{2}\left(e^{2}-1\right) \\
\frac{2 l^{2}}{m}\left(\frac{1}{2} m v^{2}-\frac{k}{r}\right) & =k^{2}\left(e^{2}-1\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

or

$$
E=\frac{m k^{2}}{2 l^{2}}\left(e^{2}-1\right) .
$$

This gives the energy in terms of $l$ and $e$.
I have derived all the main results of the Kepler problem (except for the time evolution) a whole lot more easily than standard treatments do. In fact, many textbooks don't even get to the eccentricity vector. Here geometric algebra is clearly superior to standard vector algebra both for solving the equations and for understanding the results.

## A. Summary of definitions and formulas

## A.1. Notation

| $\mathcal{G}$ | Geometric algebra |
| :--- | :--- |
| $\mathcal{G}_{r}$ | Grade- $r$ subspace of $\mathcal{G}$ (space of $r$-vectors) |
| $\mathcal{G}^{n}$ | Geometric algebra of an $n$-dimensional vector space |
| $A, B$, etc. | General multivector |
| $\lambda, \mu$, etc. | Scalar |


| $a, b, u, v$, etc. | Vector |
| :--- | :--- |
| $A_{r}$ | $r$-vector (sometimes grade- $r$ part of $A$ ) |
| $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ | $r$-blade |
| $A_{+}$ | Even-grade multivector |
| $A_{-}$ | Odd-grade multivector |
| I | Volume element |
| $\langle A\rangle_{r}$ | Grade- $r$ part of $A$ |
| $\langle A\rangle$ | Scalar (grade-0) part of $A$ |
| $\langle A\rangle_{+}$ | Even-grade part of $A$ |
| $\langle A\rangle_{-}$ | Odd-grade part of $A$ |
| $A^{-1}$ | Inverse of $A$ |
| $A^{*}$ | Grade involution of $A$ |
| $A^{* r}$ | $r$ times grade involuted $A$ |
| $A^{\dagger}$ | Reverse of $A$ |
| $A^{\ddagger}$ | Clifford conjugate of $A$ |
| $\|A\|^{2}$ | Squared norm of $A$ |
| $A^{\perp}$ | Dual of $A$ |
| $A^{-\perp}$ | Inverse dual of $A$ |
| $A B$ | Geometric product of $A$ and $B$ |
| $A\rfloor B$ | Left inner product of $A$ into $B$ |
| $A\lfloor B$ | Right inner product of $A$ by $B$ |
| $A \wedge B$ | Outer product of $A$ and $B$ |
| $A * B$ | Scalar product of $A$ and $B$ |
| $A \times B$ | Commutator of $A$ and $B$ |
| $P_{A_{r}}(B)$ | Orthogonal projection of $B$ into $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ |
| $R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(B)$ | Orthogonal rejection of $B$ from $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ |
| $U, V, W$, etc. | Vector space |
| $F, G$, etc. | Linear function of vectors (or its outermorphism extension) |
| $F_{U}$ | Restriction of $F$ to subspace $U$ |
| $F_{\boldsymbol{A}}$ | Restriction of $F$ to subspace represented by $\boldsymbol{A}$ |
| Id | Identity function |
| $\operatorname{Ker}(F)$ | Kernel of $F$ |
| Range $(F)$ | Range of $F$ |
| $\operatorname{null}(F)$ | Nullity of $F$ |
| $\operatorname{rank}(F)$ | Rank of $F$ |
| $\bar{F}$ | Adjoint of $F$ |
| $\operatorname{det}(F)$ | Determinant of $F$ |
| $\mathcal{F}$ | Outermorphism |

## A.2. Axioms

A geometric algebra $\mathcal{G}$ is a set with two composition laws, addition and multiplication, that satisfy these axioms.

Axiom 1. $\mathcal{G}$ is a ring with unit. The additive identity is called 0 and the multiplicative identity is called 1.
Axiom 2. $\mathcal{G}$ contains a field $\mathcal{G}_{0}$ of characteristic zero which includes 0 and 1.
Axiom 3. $\mathcal{G}$ contains a subset $\mathcal{G}_{1}$ closed under addition, and $\lambda \in \mathcal{G}_{0}, v \in \mathcal{G}_{1}$ implies $\lambda v=v \lambda \in \mathcal{G}_{1}$.
Axiom 4. The square of every vector is a scalar.
Axiom 5. The inner product is nondegenerate.

Axiom 6. If $\mathcal{G}_{0}=\mathcal{G}_{1}$, then $\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{0}$. Otherwise, $\mathcal{G}$ is the direct sum of all the $\mathcal{G}_{r}$.

## A.3. Contents of a geometric algebra

An $r$-blade $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is the outer product of $r$ vectors, $a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}$. It represents the subspace spanned by $\left\{a_{j}\right\}_{j=1, \ldots, r}$, with a weight and orientation if the scalars are real.
$\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0$ iff the $a_{j}$ are linearly dependent.
$\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ define the same subspace iff $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\lambda \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$.
If $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a proper subspace of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ can be factored out of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ from either the left ( $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{r}}$ ) or the right $\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)$. The grade- $s-r$ factors in each case may be chosen to be the same except for at most a sign.
$a \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0$ iff $a$ lies in $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$.
$a \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=0$ iff $a$ is orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$.
The reflection of multivector $B$ in subspace $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} B^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$.
$\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}, \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{*}, \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\dagger}, \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\ddagger}$, and $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ (if it exists) represent the same subspace.

## A.4. The inner, outer, and geometric products

$$
\begin{gathered}
A_{r} B_{s}=\sum_{j=0}^{\min \{r, s\}}\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{|r-s|+2 j} \\
\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r+s-2 j}=(-1)^{r s-j}\left\langle B_{s} A_{r}\right\rangle_{r+s-2 j}
\end{gathered}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\langle A B\rangle & =\langle B A\rangle \\
& =\left\langle A^{*} B^{*}\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle A^{\dagger} B^{\dagger}\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle A^{\ddagger} B^{\ddagger}\right\rangle
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& A\rfloor B=\sum_{r, s}\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{s-r} \\
& A\left\lfloor B=\sum_{r, s}\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r-s}\right. \\
