& A \wedge B=\sum_{r, s}\left\langle A_{r} B_{s}\right\rangle_{r+s}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.A_{r}\right\rfloor B_{s} & =(-1)^{r(s-1)} B_{s}\left\lfloor A_{r}\right. \\
A_{r} \wedge B_{s} & =(-1)^{r s} B_{s} \wedge A_{r}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}=\left\langle a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}\right\rangle_{r}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft A & =\frac{1}{2}\left(a A-A^{*} a\right) \\
a \wedge A & =\frac{1}{2}\left(a A+A^{*} a\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& A\lfloor a=-a\rfloor A^{*} \\
& A \wedge a=a \wedge A^{*}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
a \wedge A \wedge b=-b \wedge A \wedge a
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft(A B) & =(a \downharpoonleft A) B+A^{*}(a \downharpoonleft B) \\
& =(a \wedge A) B-A^{*}(a \wedge B)
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \wedge(A B) & \left.=(a \wedge A) B-A^{*}(a\rfloor B\right) \\
& =(a\rfloor A) B+A^{*}(a \wedge B)
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft(A \wedge B) & \left.=(a\lrcorner A) \wedge B+A^{*} \wedge(a\lrcorner B\right) \\
a \wedge(A\lfloor B) & =(a \wedge A)\left\lfloor B-A^{*}\lfloor(a\rfloor B)\right. \\
a \wedge(A\rfloor B) & \left.=(a\rfloor A)\rfloor B+A^{*}\right\rfloor(a \wedge B)
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
a \downharpoonleft\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right) & =\sum_{j=1}^{r}(-1)^{j-1}\left(a \downharpoonleft a_{j}\right) a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{j} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r} \\
a_{1} \wedge\left(a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right) & =a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}
\end{aligned}
$$

If $r \leq s$ then

$$
B_{r} \downharpoonleft\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{s}\right)=\sum(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(i_{j}-j\right)}\left(B_{r} \downharpoonleft a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}}\right) a_{i_{r+1}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{s}}
$$

where the sum is performed over all possible choices of $\left\{a_{i_{j}}\right\}_{j=1, \ldots, r}$ out of $\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, s}$, and in each term $i_{1}$ through $i_{r}$ and $i_{r+1}$ through $i_{s}$ separately are in ascending order.

$$
\begin{aligned}
A \wedge(B \wedge C) & =(A \wedge B) \wedge C \\
A\rfloor(B\lfloor C) & =(A\rfloor B)\lfloor C \\
A\rfloor(B\rfloor C) & =(A \wedge B)\rfloor C \\
A\lfloor(B \wedge C) & =(A\lfloor B)\lfloor C
\end{aligned}
$$

If $A=a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}$, then

$$
A B_{s} A^{\dagger}=\left\langle A B_{s} A^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{s}
$$

and

$$
\left(A B A^{\dagger}\right) \wedge\left(A C A^{\dagger}\right)=|A|^{2} A(B \wedge C) A^{\dagger}
$$

A.5. The geometric meaning of the inner and outer products
$\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ iff $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ share nonzero vectors.
$\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, if nonzero, represents the direct sum of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.
$\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=0$ iff $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ contains a nonzero vector orthogonal to $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.
$\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, if nonzero, represents the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ in $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.
If $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ and $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ are orthogonal, then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \wedge \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.
If $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ is a subspace of $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$, then $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \downharpoonleft \boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$.
The converses of the previous two statements are true if (1) $r=1$ or $s=1$ or (2) $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$ or $\boldsymbol{B}_{\boldsymbol{s}}$ is invertible.

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left.\left.\left.P_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(B)=B\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}=(B\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right)\right\rfloor \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1} \\
& R_{\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}(B)=B \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}=B \wedge \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\left\lfloor\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

A.6. Grade involution

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda^{*} & =\lambda \\
a^{*} & =-a \\
(A B)^{*} & =A^{*} B^{*} \\
(A+B)^{*} & =A^{*}+B^{*}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{gathered}
A_{r}^{*}=(-1)^{r} A_{r} \\
A^{*}=\langle A\rangle_{+}-\langle A\rangle_{-} \\
A^{*}=\boldsymbol{I} A^{* n} \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} \\
\langle A\rangle_{ \pm}=\frac{1}{2}\left(A \pm A^{*}\right) \\
A^{* *}=A \\
\left(A^{-1}\right)^{*}=\left(A^{*}\right)^{-1}
\end{gathered}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
(A\rfloor B)^{*} & \left.=A^{*}\right\rfloor B^{*} \\
\left(A\lfloor B)^{*}\right. & =A^{*}\left\lfloor B^{*}\right. \\
(A \wedge B)^{*} & =A^{*} \wedge B^{*}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
A \boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{I} A^{*(n-1)}
$$

## A.7. Reversion

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda^{\dagger} & =\lambda \\
a^{\dagger} & =a \\
(A B)^{\dagger} & =B^{\dagger} A^{\dagger} \\
(A+B)^{\dagger} & =A^{\dagger}+B^{\dagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
A_{r}^{\dagger}=(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2} A_{r}
$$

$$
A^{\dagger \dagger}=A
$$

$$
\left(A^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}=\left(A^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& (A\rfloor B)^{\dagger}=B^{\dagger}\left\lfloor A^{\dagger}\right. \\
& \left(A\lfloor B)^{\dagger}=B^{\dagger}\right\rfloor A^{\dagger} \\
& (A \wedge B)^{\dagger}=B^{\dagger} \wedge A^{\dagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

## A.8. Clifford conjugation

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda^{\ddagger} & =\lambda \\
a^{\ddagger} & =-a \\
(A B)^{\ddagger} & =B^{\ddagger} A^{\ddagger} \\
(A+B)^{\ddagger} & =A^{\ddagger}+B^{\ddagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
A^{\ddagger}=A^{* \dagger}=A^{\dagger *}
$$

$$
A_{r}^{\ddagger}=(-1)^{r(r+1) / 2} A_{r}
$$

$$
A^{\ddagger \ddagger}=A
$$

$$
\left(A^{-1}\right)^{\ddagger}=\left(A^{\ddagger}\right)^{-1}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& (A\rfloor B)^{\ddagger}=B^{\ddagger}\left\lfloor A^{\ddagger}\right. \\
& \left(A\lfloor B)^{\ddagger}=B^{\ddagger}\right\rfloor A^{\ddagger} \\
& (A \wedge B)^{\ddagger}=B^{\ddagger} \wedge A^{\ddagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

## A.9. The scalar product and norm

$$
\begin{aligned}
A * B & =\left\langle A^{\dagger} B\right\rangle \\
& \left.=\left\langle A^{\dagger}\right\rfloor B\right\rangle \\
& =\left\langle A^{\dagger}\lfloor B\rangle\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
A * B & =\sum_{r} A_{r} * B_{r} \\
& \left.=\sum_{r} A_{r}^{\dagger}\right\rfloor B_{r} \\
& =\sum_{r} A_{r}^{\dagger}\left\lfloor B_{r}\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
A * B & =B * A \\
& =A^{*} * B^{*} \\
& =A^{\dagger} * B^{\dagger} \\
& =A^{\ddagger} * B^{\ddagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
A *(B C) & =\left(B^{\dagger} A\right) * C \\
A *(B\lfloor C) & =\left(B^{\dagger}\lfloor A) * C\right. \\
A *(B\rfloor C) & =\left(B^{\dagger} \wedge A\right) * C \\
A *(B \wedge C) & \left.=\left(B^{\dagger}\right\rfloor A\right) * C
\end{aligned}
$$

Multivector $A$ is uniquely determined by either of the following:

1. $A * B$ for every multivector $B$.
2. $\langle A\rangle$ and $a\lrcorner A$ for every vector $a$.

$$
\begin{aligned}
|A|^{2} & =A * A \\
& =\left|A^{*}\right|^{2} \\
& =\left|A^{\dagger}\right|^{2} \\
& =\left|A^{\ddagger}\right|^{2}
\end{aligned}
$$

If $A=a_{1} a_{2} \cdots a_{r}$, then

(a) $|A|^{2}=A^{\dagger} A=a_{1}^{2} a_{2}^{2} \cdots a_{r}^{2}$.
(b) $A^{-1}$ exists iff $|A|^{2} \neq 0$, in which case $A^{-1}=A^{\dagger} /|A|^{2}$ and $\left|A^{-1}\right|^{2}=|A|^{-2}$.
(c) $(A B) *(A C)=(B A) *(C A)=|A|^{2} B * C$.

For any blade $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$,

(a) $\left|\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right|^{2}=0$ iff the inner product is degenerate on $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}$.
(b) $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}$ exists iff $\left|\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right|^{2} \neq 0$, in which case $\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}=(-1)^{r(r-1) / 2} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} /\left|\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\right|^{2}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} / \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2}$.

## A.10. The dual

A volume element $\boldsymbol{I}$ is a unit $n$-blade.

$$
\begin{aligned}
A^{\perp} & =A\rfloor \boldsymbol{I}^{-1} \\
& =A \boldsymbol{I}^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
A^{-\perp} & =A\rfloor \boldsymbol{I} \\
& =A \boldsymbol{I} \\
& =\boldsymbol{I}^{2} A^{\perp}
\end{aligned}
$$

$\boldsymbol{A}^{\perp}$ is the orthogonal complement of $\boldsymbol{A}$.

$$
\begin{aligned}
(A B)^{\perp} & =A B^{\perp} \\
(A \wedge B)^{\perp} & =A\rfloor B^{\perp} \\
(A\rfloor B)^{\perp} & =A \wedge B^{\perp}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\left(A^{\perp}\right)^{-1}=\boldsymbol{I} A^{-1}
$$

$$
\left(A^{*}\right)^{\perp}=(-1)^{n}\left(A^{\perp}\right)^{*}
$$

$$
\left(A^{\dagger}\right)^{\perp}=\left[\boldsymbol{I}^{\dagger}\left(A^{\perp}\right)^{\dagger}\right]^{\perp}
$$

$$
\left(A^{\ddagger}\right)^{\perp}=\left[I^{\ddagger}\left(A^{\perp}\right)^{\ddagger}\right]^{\perp}
$$

$$
A^{\perp} * B^{\perp}=|\boldsymbol{I}|^{-2} A * B
$$

$$
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp} B^{*(n-r)}\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\perp}\right)^{-1}=\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}} B^{* r} \boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{-1}\right)^{*}
$$

$$
R_{\boldsymbol{A}}(a)=P_{\boldsymbol{A}^{\perp}}(a)
$$

## A.11. The commutator

$$
A \times B=\frac{1}{2}(A B-B A)
$$

$$
A \times(B C)=(A \times B) C+B(A \times C)
$$

$$
A \times(B \times C)+B \times(C \times A)+C \times(A \times B)=0 .
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& (A \times B)^{*}=A^{*} \times B^{*} \\
& (A \times B)^{\dagger}=B^{\dagger} \times A^{\dagger} \\
& (A \times B)^{\ddagger}=B^{\ddagger} \times A^{\ddagger}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\lambda \times A=0
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& a \times A=a \downharpoonleft\langle A\rangle_{+}+a \wedge\langle A\rangle_{-} \\
& A \times a=\langle A\rangle_{+}\left\lfloor a+\langle A\rangle_{-} \wedge a\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
A_{2} \times A_{r}=\left\langle A_{2} A_{r}\right\rangle_{r}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left.A_{2} \times(B\rfloor C\right) & \left.\left.=\left(A_{2} \times B\right)\right\rfloor C+B\right\rfloor\left(A_{2} \times C\right) \\
A_{2} \times(B\lfloor C) & =\left(A_{2} \times B\right)\left\lfloor C+B\left\lfloor\left(A_{2} \times C\right)\right.\right. \\
A_{2} \times(B \wedge C) & =\left(A_{2} \times B\right) \wedge C+B \wedge\left(A_{2} \times C\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
A_{2} \times\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{r} a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge\left(A_{2} \downharpoonright a_{j}\right) \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}
$$

$A$ commutes with all multivectors iff $A$ commutes with all vectors iff $A=\lambda+\mu\langle\boldsymbol{I}\rangle_{-}$.

## A.12. Frames and bases

If $\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1, \ldots, n}$ is a frame with volume element $a_{N}=a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}$, the reciprocal frame is given by

$$
a^{i}=(-1)^{i-1}\left(a_{1} \wedge a_{2} \wedge \cdots \wedge \check{a}_{i} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{n}\right) a_{N}^{-1} .
$$

It satisfies

$$
a^{i} \downharpoonleft a_{j}=\delta_{j}^{i} .
$$

Let $I$ be an increasing string of indices $i_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots, i_{r}$; then $a_{I}$ and $a^{I}$ are

$$
\begin{aligned}
& a_{I}=a_{i_{1}} \wedge a_{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{i_{r}} \\
& a^{I}=a^{i_{1}} \wedge a^{i_{2}} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{i_{r}} .
\end{aligned}
$$

They satisfy

$$
a_{I} * a^{J}=\delta_{I}^{J},
$$

and for any multivector $A$,

$$
\begin{aligned}
A & =\sum_{I} A^{I} a_{I} \quad \text { where } \quad A^{I}=A * a^{I} \\
& =\sum_{I} A_{I} a^{I} \quad \text { where } \quad A_{I}=A * a_{I} .
\end{aligned}
$$

If $I$ is increasing and $I^{c}$ is the increasing string of indices complementary to $I$, then

$$
a^{I}=(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(i_{j}-1\right)} a_{I^{c}} a_{N}^{-1} .
$$

A frame and its reciprocal satisfy these identities:

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \sum_{i} a^{i} a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r}=\sum_{i} a^{i} \wedge\left(a_{i} \downharpoonleft A_{r}\right)=r A_{r} \quad \text { for any } A_{r} . \\
& \left.\left.\sum_{i} a_{i} a^{i}\right\rfloor A_{r}=\sum_{i} a_{i} \wedge\left(a^{i}\right\rfloor A_{r}\right)=r A_{r} \quad \text { for any } A_{r} .
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\sum_{i} a_{i} a^{i}=\sum_{i} a^{i} a_{i}=n .
$$

The volume element of the reciprocal frame, $a^{N}=a^{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a^{n}$, is also given by

$$
a^{N}=\frac{a_{N}}{\left|a_{N}\right|^{2}} .
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{a_{1} \wedge \cdots \wedge a_{r}}(B) & =\sum_{I}\left(B * a_{I}\right) a^{I} \\
& =\sum_{I}\left(B * a^{I}\right) a_{I}
\end{aligned}
$$

## A.13. The adjoint of a linear operator

$$
\bar{F}(B) * A=B * F(A)
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& A\rfloor \bar{F}(B)=\bar{F}(F(A)\rfloor B) \\
& F(A)\lfloor B=F(A\lfloor\bar{F}(B))
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\overline{\bar{F}}=F
$$

$$
\overline{G F}=\bar{F} \bar{G}
$$

$$
\bar{F}^{-1}=\overline{F^{-1}}
$$

$$
\operatorname{det}(\bar{F})=\operatorname{det}(F)
$$

## A.14. Symmetric and skew symmetric operators

$F$ is symmetric if $\bar{F}=F$ and skew symmetric (or skew) if $\bar{F}=-F$.
$F$ is (skew) symmetric iff $a \downharpoonleft F(b)$ is (anti)symmetric.
$F$ is symmetric iff $F(a)=\sum_{i} \lambda_{i}\left(a * a^{i}\right) a_{i}$ for some frame $\left\{a_{i}\right\}$ of eigenvectors with eigenvalues $\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}$.
$F$ is skew iff $F(a)=a \downharpoonleft A_{2}$ for some bivector $A_{2}$.

## A.15. Isometries and orthogonal transformations

$F$ is an isometry if $F(u)\rfloor F(v)=u \downharpoonleft v$ for all $u$ and $v$.
$F$ is an isometry $\operatorname{iff} \bar{F} F=\mathrm{Id}$.
$F$ is an orthogonal transformation if $F$ is an invertible isometry.
$F$ is orthogonal iff $\bar{F}=F^{-1}$.
$F$ is an isometry on a finite-dimensional space iff $F(a)=A_{r} a^{* r} A_{r}^{-1}$ for some invertible $r$-versor $A_{r}$.
The extension of orthogonal $F$ on a finite-dimensional space to all multivectors is $F(B)=A_{r} B^{* r} A_{r}^{-1}$.

## A.16. Eigenvalues, invariant subspaces, and determinants

$\boldsymbol{A} \neq 0$ is an eigenblade of $F$ if $F(\boldsymbol{A})=\lambda \boldsymbol{A}$.
An eigenblade of $F$ is an invariant subspace on which $F$ is invertible. The eigenvalue is $\operatorname{det}\left(F_{\boldsymbol{A}}\right)$.

$$
\begin{gathered}
\operatorname{det} F=F(\boldsymbol{I})^{\perp} \\
F^{-1}(A)=\frac{\bar{F}\left(A^{-\perp}\right)^{\perp}}{\operatorname{det}(F)}
\end{gathered}
$$

## B. Topics for future versions

These are the subjects I plan to add to the notes next, in no particular order. The items with asterisks are most interesting to me at the moment.

- Linear algebra
    - More on invariant subspaces and determinants*
    - Representing a general linear operator as a sequence of multivector multiplications*
- Differential and integral calculus
    - The directed integral of a multivector
    - The derivative of a multivector-valued function defined in terms of the directed integral*
    - Recovering traditional vector calculus
    - The fundamental theorem of calculus and its corollaries (Gauss' theorem, Stokes' theorem, Green's theorem, etc.)*
    - Taylor series*
    - Generalizations of Cauchy's integral formula*
    - The invertibility of the derivative (cf. the exterior derivative)
    - Solutions to standard ODEs and PDEs (simple harmonic oscillator, wave equation, etc.)*
    - Fourier analysis
    - Manifold theory
    - Lie groups and Lie algebras
    - Curvature*
- Geometry
    - Meet and join of subspaces
    - Projective splits (e.g. Minkowski spacetime into any observer's space + time)*
    - Different models of space (Euclidean, projective, conformal)
    - Geometric algebra on a vector space without an inner product
- Physics
    - Rotational dynamics and the inertia tensor*
    - Relativistic particle mechanics*
    - Electricity and magnetism in 3D and 4D*
    - Lagrangian and Hamiltonian mechanics*
    - Continuum mechanics and elasticity theory*
    - The Dirac equation
    - General relativity
    - The Galilei and Lorentz groups and their Lie algebras
